Extended Topological Field Theory — The Cobordism Hypothesis, Higher Categories & the Turaev-Viro Construction

Deep dive into Extended Topological Field Theory — Atiyah's axiomatic definition of a TQFT as a symmetric monoidal functor from the cobordism category to the category of vector spaces, the Baez-Dolan cobordism hypothesis (a fully extended n-dimensional TFT is determined entirely by its value on a single point, which must be a fully dualizable object in an (∞,n)-category), (∞,n)-categories and the homotopy hypothesis, the Lurie proof of the cobordism hypothesis using Morse theory and the classification of framed manifolds, the Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury state sum TQFT (3d TFT from spherical fusion categories using the 6j-symbols of the category), the Reshetikhin-Turaev invariants of 3-manifolds constructed from modular tensor categories (skein theory and the Jones-Witten invariants), the Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT (the 3-dimensional Chern-Simons TQFT), the Crane-Yetter invariant in 4 dimensions (a state sum for 4-manifolds from a braided fusion category), the Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT (finite group gauge theory in arbitrary dimension), and the application to topological quantum computing (anyons: particles with braid statistics in 2+1 dimensions, and the Fibonacci anyon model for universal quantum computation).

"A topological quantum field theory is a symmetric monoidal functor from the cobordism category to the category of vector spaces."
— Michael Atiyah
Part 1

📏 Axiomatic TQFT and the Cobordism Category

Axioms
Atiyah's Axioms, the Cobordism Category, and the Pachner Moves
The cobordism category Cob_d has objects: closed (d−1)-dimensional manifolds, and morphisms: d-dimensional cobordisms (manifolds M with boundary ∂M = Σ₀ ⊔ Σ₁, where Σ₀ is the incoming and Σ₁ the outgoing boundary). Composition is gluing of cobordisms along a common boundary component. The monoidal structure is disjoint union, and the unit is the empty manifold. A (d-dimensional) topological quantum field theory (TQFT) is a symmetric monoidal functor Z: Cob_d → Vect_𝕜 (the category of vector spaces over a field 𝕜). Atiyah's axioms: Z(∅) = 𝕜, Z(Σ ⊔ Σ') = Z(Σ) ⊗ Z(Σ'), Z(∂_in M) → Z(∂_out M) is a linear map, and Z is diffeomorphism invariant (depends only on the diffeomorphism type of the cobordism). The Pachner moves (bistellar moves) relate any two triangulations of a PL manifold by a finite sequence of local moves — a TQFT must be invariant under these moves. The Gluing axiom: Z(M₁ ∪_Σ M₂) = Z(M₂) ∘ Z(M₁). The TQFT is unitary if Z(Σ^op) = Z(Σ)^* (Hermitian adjoint). The Frobenius algebra: Z(S^{d−1}) is a finite-dimensional commutative Frobenius algebra with trace given by Z(M) where M is the d-ball with boundary S^{d−1}.
Atiyah's axioms formalize TQFT as a functor — the cobordism category encodes the topology of manifolds and their gluings, and the TQFT assigns algebraic invariants that respect gluing.
Part 2

🌍 The Cobordism Hypothesis

Hypothesis
Baez-Dolan Cobordism Hypothesis, (∞,n)-Categories, and the Lurie Proof
The cobordism hypothesis (Baez-Dolan, 1995): a fully extended n-dimensional TQFT is completely determined by the value it assigns to a single point. To state this precisely, we need higher category theory. An (∞,n)-category is a weak n-category where all k-morphisms for k > n are invertible (up to homotopy). The bordism (∞,n)-category Bord_n^fr has: objects = 0-manifolds (points), 1-morphisms = 1-bordisms, ..., n-morphisms = n-bordisms, and (n+1)-morphisms = diffeomorphisms, ..., all up to isotopy. The cobordism hypothesis (proved by Lurie, 2009) states: for a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C, evaluation on a point gives an equivalence between the ∞-groupoid of fully extended TQFTs Z: Bord_n^fr → C and the ∞-groupoid of fully dualizable objects in C. A fully dualizable object is one for which all higher adjoints exist (the object has a dual, the dual has its own dual, etc., up to level n). The framing is necessary because the cobordism hypothesis works with framed manifolds; for oriented or unoriented manifolds, we need to factor through the homotopy quotient of the orthogonal group. The Serre functor and Nakayama automorphism appear in the classification of TQFTs with non-trivial anomalies. The Morita equivalence of fully dualizable objects corresponds to equivalence of TQFTs.
The cobordism hypothesis is a classification theorem: fully extended TQFTs are classified by fully dualizable objects in higher categories — a point contains all the information.
Part 3

🧮 State Sum TQFTs and Quantum Invariants

Invariants
Turaev-Viro State Sum, Reshetikhin-Turaev Invariants, and Modular Tensor Categories
The Turaev-Viro TQFT (Turaev-Viro, 1992) is a 3-dimensional state sum TQFT constructed from a spherical fusion category C. Given a triangulated 3-manifold, the invariant Z(M) = Σ_{labelings of edges} Π_{tetrahedra} {6j-symbol} where the sum is over labelings of the edges by simple objects of C, and each tetrahedron contributes the 6j-symbol of C. The Barrett-Westbury theorem: the Turaev-Viro invariant is well-defined (independent of triangulation) if and only if C is a spherical fusion category. The Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT (Reshetikhin-Turaev, 1991) constructs 3-manifold invariants from a modular tensor category (MTC) — a braided fusion category with a non-degenerate S-matrix. The S-matrix S_{ab} = Tr(σ_{b,a} ∘ σ_{a,b}) (the braiding eigenvalues) is invertible. The T-matrix T_{ab} = δ_{ab} θ_a (the twist factors). The triple (S, T, c) gives a projective representation of SL(2,ℤ). The Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev invariant for Chern-Simons theory with gauge group G at level k is constructed from the modular tensor category Rep(U_q(g)) at q = exp(2πi/(k + h^∨)). The Jones polynomial of a knot K is recovered as the expectation value of the Wilson loop in SU(2) Chern-Simons theory: J_K(q) = ⟨W_K⟩_{CS}. The Crane-Yetter invariant (1993) generalizes Turaev-Viro to 4 dimensions using a braided fusion category — it is a state sum for 4-manifolds.
State sum TQFTs produce invariants of manifolds by summing over combinatorial data — the 6j-symbols of fusion categories encode the topology of triangulations.
Part 4

🔥 Topological Quantum Computing and Anyons

Computing
Anyons, Braid Statistics, the Fibonacci Anyon Model, and Topological Quantum Computation
Anyons are quasiparticles in 2-dimensional systems whose exchange statistics are neither bosonic nor fermionic — they are governed by the braid group B_n (the group of braids on n strands) rather than the permutation group. In a TQFT, anyon types correspond to the simple objects of a modular tensor category. The fusion rules describe how two anyons combine: a × b = Σ_c N_{ab}^c c, where N_{ab}^c are the fusion coefficients. The braiding is an isomorphism σ_{a,b}: a ⊗ b → b ⊗ a satisfying the Yang-Baxter equation. The topological quantum computation (Kitaev, Freedman) uses the braiding of anyons to encode and process quantum information. The Fibonacci anyon model: there is one non-trivial anyon τ with fusion rule τ × τ = 1 + τ. The dimension of the Hilbert space for n anyons is the n-th Fibonacci number F_{n+1} — hence the name. Braiding of Fibonacci anyons is universal for quantum computation (the braid group B_n is dense in the unitary group U(F_{n+1})). The quantum dimension d_a = Tr(Id_a) of an anyon a is the asymptotic growth rate of the Hilbert space dimension. The S-matrix S_{ab} = (1/D) Σ_c N_{ab}^c d_c θ_c (where D = Σ_a d_a² is the total quantum dimension) encodes the mutual statistics of anyon types. The Ising anyon model (from the SU(2)₂ MTC) has three anyon types: 1, σ, ψ with σ × σ = 1 + ψ and σ × ψ = σ — this model supports Clifford gate operations but is not universal. The Freedman-Kitaev conjecture: the TQFT from SU(2) at level k can be simulated by a quantum computer, and for k ≥ 3 (Fibonacci anyons), the simulation is universal.
Topological quantum computation uses the braiding of anyons — the worldlines of anyons in 2+1 dimensions are braids, and the TQFT assigns unitary matrices to these braids, enabling fault-tolerant quantum gates.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Higher Categorical Formulation of the Cobordism Hypothesis

The cobordism hypothesis is most naturally stated in the language of symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-categories. The source category Bordnfr has objects 0‑manifolds, 1‑morphisms 1‑dimensional cobordisms, up to n‑morphisms given by n‑dimensional cobordisms with corners, all taken up to diffeomorphism. The target C is any symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category (for example the (∞,n)-category of n-vector spaces, or the (∞,n)-category of higher algebras). A fully extended TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bordnfr → C.

The hypothesis asserts that the ∞‑groupoid of such functors is equivalent to the ∞‑groupoid of fully dualizable objects in C. An object X ∈ C is fully dualizable when it admits a dual X∨ together with evaluation and coevaluation 1‑morphisms, and those 1‑morphisms themselves admit adjoints, and so on up to level n. In concrete terms, for n = 2 this means X is a dualizable object in a monoidal 2‑category whose dual also has a dual; for n = 3 one needs duals for the 2‑morphisms as well. The proof proceeds by constructing a universal (∞,n)-category Bordnfr as the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals on a single object, then showing that any symmetric monoidal functor out of it is uniquely determined by the image of that object.

When one replaces framings by other tangential structures (orientations, spin, pin, string, etc.) the cobordism category becomes the homotopy quotient BordnG ≃ Bordnfr // G where G is a topological group equipped with a map G → O(n). The hypothesis then classifies G-structured TQFTs by G-fixed points in the space of fully dualizable objects, i.e. by homotopy fixed points of the G-action on the core of fully dualizable objects. This yields the familiar statement that oriented TQFTs correspond to SO(n)-equivariant fully dualizable objects, spin TQFTs to Spin(n)-equivariant ones, and so on.

The higher categorical viewpoint also clarifies the role of Morita equivalence. Two fully dualizable objects give equivalent TQFTs precisely when they are Morita equivalent in the appropriate higher Morita category (e.g. for n = 2, Morita equivalence of separable Frobenius algebras; for n = 3, Morita equivalence of fusion categories). This equivalence is exactly the statement that the corresponding extended theories assign isomorphic values to all closed manifolds.

Invertible Topological Phases and Anomalies

An invertible TQFT is a fully extended theory whose value on every closed n-manifold is an invertible object in the target category (typically a 1‑dimensional vector space). In the classification of the cobordism hypothesis, invertible theories correspond to invertible fully dualizable objects. Because every invertible object is automatically fully dualizable, the space of invertible G-structured TQFTs in dimension n is the homotopy fixed point space of the G-action on the Picard ∞‑groupoid of C. For the standard target nVect (the (∞,n)-category of higher vector spaces), this Picard ∞‑groupoid is equivalent to the Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum Σn+1U(1). Consequently, invertible G-TQFTs are classified by the cohomology group Hn+1(BG; U(1)).

This cohomological classification is the mathematical formulation of 't Hooft anomalies. An anomaly in an n-dimensional quantum field theory is an obstruction to gauging a global symmetry G; it is captured by an invertible (n+1)-dimensional TQFT with G-structure. The anomaly inflow mechanism says that the anomalous theory lives on the boundary of a bulk invertible theory whose partition function on a closed (n+1)-manifold W is exp(2πi ∫W ω) for a characteristic class ω ∈ Hn+1(BG; U(1)). For example, the 2‑dimensional chiral fermion with a U(1) symmetry has anomaly classified by H3(BU(1); U(1)) ≅ ℤ, the integer being the chiral central charge.

In the context of topological phases of matter, invertible TQFTs describe symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phases. The group of SPT phases with symmetry G in dimension n is precisely Hn+1(BG; U(1)) (for bosonic systems) or a twisted version for fermionic systems. The cobordism hypothesis thus provides a unified framework: a fully extended TQFT with a non‑trivial anomaly is a morphism BordnG → C that does not factor through the trivial theory, and its anomaly is the obstruction to extending it to a theory on Bordn+1G.

Examiner's trap: confusing invertible TQFTs with trivial TQFTs
A 3‑dimensional oriented TQFT assigns the number 1 to every closed oriented 3‑manifold. Is this theory necessarily the trivial (identity) TQFT? Explain why or why not, using the classification of invertible theories.
The theory described is an invertible TQFT because its value on any closed 3‑manifold is the invertible object 1 ∈ ℂ. However, invertible oriented 3‑dimensional TQFTs are classified by H⁴(BSO(3); U(1)) ≅ ℤ₂. The non‑trivial element corresponds to the E₈ state sum (or the Witten–Chern–Simons theory at level 1) which also assigns 1 to every closed 3‑manifold but has a non‑trivial partition function on 4‑manifolds with boundary. Therefore the theory could be either the trivial theory or the non‑trivial ℤ₂ invertible theory; the data on closed 3‑manifolds alone does not distinguish them. The examiner expects the answer that the theory is invertible but not necessarily trivial, and that the distinction is detected by the anomaly on 4‑manifolds.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks whether a TQFT is trivial, always check whether the theory is invertible and, if so, compute the relevant cohomology group Hⁿ⁺¹(BG; U(1)). A vanishing partition function on closed n‑manifolds does not imply triviality; the anomaly lives one dimension higher. Memorise the low‑dimensional classifications: H³(BU(1);U(1))≅ℤ (2‑d chiral anomaly), H⁴(BSO(3);U(1))≅ℤ₂ (3‑d oriented invertible), H⁵(BSpin(4);U(1))≅ℤ (4‑d fermionic SPT).

Relation Between Turaev‑Viro and Reshetikhin‑Turaev Theories

The Turaev‑Viro (TV) state sum and the Reshetikhin‑Turaev (RT) construction are two pillars of 3‑dimensional TQFT. The TV invariant is built from a spherical fusion category C. Given a triangulated closed 3‑manifold M, one sums over labelings of edges by simple objects of C, weighting each tetrahedron by the 6j-symbol of C. The Barrett‑Westbury theorem guarantees triangulation independence exactly when C is spherical fusion.

The RT invariant, on the other hand, starts from a modular tensor category (MTC) D — a braided fusion category with non‑degenerate S-matrix. It assigns to a 3‑manifold presented by surgery on a framed link the evaluation of the link in D using the braiding and twist. The two constructions are deeply related: the Drinfeld center Z(C) of a spherical fusion category C is a modular tensor category. The fundamental theorem of Turaev and Viro (later refined by Barrett, Westbury, and others) states that the TV invariant of M computed from C equals the modulus squared of the RT invariant computed from Z(C):

TVC(M) = |RTZ(C)(M)|².

Consequently, the TV TQFT is the tensor product of the RT TQFT with its complex conjugate: TV ≃ RT ⊗ RT̄. This factorization explains why TV theories are non‑chiral (they have vanishing chiral central charge) while RT theories are generally chiral. It also implies that the TV state sum can be interpreted as a Levin‑Wen string‑net model whose anyon content is precisely the MTC Z(C).

At the level of extended TQFTs, the TV theory corresponds to the fully dualizable object C in the symmetric monoidal 3‑category of fusion categories, while the RT theory corresponds to the fully dualizable object Z(C) in the 3‑category of modular tensor categories. The Morita equivalence class of C (i.e. its Drinfeld center) determines the TV theory, reflecting the general principle that fully extended TQFTs are classified by Morita classes of fully dualizable objects.

Factorization Homology and Extended TQFTs

Factorization homology (also called topological chiral homology) provides a systematic method to construct extended TQFTs from algebraic data. Given an En-algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)-category C (for instance, an E2-algebra is a braided monoidal category), one defines for each n-manifold M the factorization homology ∫M A as a colimit over the Ran space of M. This assignment M ↦ ∫M A satisfies excision and therefore extends to a fully extended TQFT ZA : Bordn → C.

For n = 2, an E2-algebra is a braided tensor category. Factorization homology over a surface recovers the Reshetikhin‑Turaev TQFT when the braided category is modular. For n = 3, an E3-algebra is a symmetric monoidal category; factorization homology over a 3‑manifold yields the Turaev‑Viro state sum when the input is a spherical fusion category regarded as an E3-algebra via its Drinfeld center. The cobordism hypothesis guarantees that every fully extended TQFT arises in this way from some En-algebra, and the classification of En-algebras up to Morita equivalence matches the classification of fully dualizable objects.

This perspective unifies state‑sum constructions (TV, Crane‑Yetter, Walker‑Wang) with functorial TQFTs. It also clarifies the role of anomalies: an anomalous theory corresponds to an En-algebra in a higher category with a non‑trivial invertible object, and the anomaly inflow is the factorization homology of that invertible object in one higher dimension.

Topological Quantum Error Correction and Anyon Models

Topological quantum computation relies on the fact that the degenerate ground space of a topological phase on a surface of genus g provides a natural quantum code. The Kitaev toric code (based on the quantum double D(ℤ₂)) and the Levin‑Wen string‑net models (based on a spherical fusion category C) are exactly solvable lattice Hamiltonians whose low‑energy effective theory is a TQFT. The anyon types are the simple objects of the modular tensor category Z(C) (for Levin‑Wen) or the representation category of the quantum double (for Kitaev).

Logical qubits are encoded in the fusion space of anyons. For n anyons of type a on a sphere, the Hilbert space dimension is the multiplicity of the vacuum in a⊗n. In the Fibonacci model (τ × τ = 1 + τ) this dimension is the Fibonacci number Fn+1. Braiding anyons implements unitary transformations on this space; the braid group representation is given by the R-symbols of the MTC. The Freedman‑Kitaev theorem states that for SU(2) level k ≥ 3 (which includes the Fibonacci anyon at k = 3) the braid group representation is dense in the unitary group, providing universal quantum gates. For k = 2 (Ising anyons) the representation is finite (Clifford group) and not universal by braiding alone; supplementing with π/8 rotations yields universality.

Error correction proceeds by measuring the anyon flux through non‑contractible cycles (syndrome extraction). Because anyons are point‑like excitations with topological charges, local perturbations cannot change the total charge without creating anyon‑antianyon pairs, which are detected by the syndrome. The threshold theorem for topological codes shows that if the physical error rate is below a constant threshold (≈ 1% for the toric code), logical errors can be suppressed exponentially in the code distance. The code distance scales linearly with the linear size of the lattice, giving a practical route to fault‑tolerant quantum computation.

  • Assuming that a TQFT with trivial partition function on closed manifolds is the trivial theory — invertible anomalies live one dimension higher.
  • Confusing the Drinfeld center Z(C) with the original fusion category C; the anyon content of a Levin‑Wen model is Z(C), not C.
  • Thinking that all modular tensor categories give universal braiding — Ising (SU(2)₂) and SO(3)₃ are non‑universal.
  • Neglecting the framing anomaly in the cobordism hypothesis: oriented theories require SO(n)-fixed points, not just fully dualizable objects.
  • Treating factorization homology as only a computational tool rather than a classification theorem — every fully extended TQFT arises from an En-algebra.
  • Overlooking that the TV invariant equals the modulus squared of the RT invariant only when the input category is spherical fusion; non‑spherical categories do not yield a well‑defined state sum.
  • Believing that the Fibonacci anyon model is the only universal anyon model — many higher‑level SU(2) and SO(N) MTCs are also universal.

These advanced topics form the backbone of modern research in topological phases and provide the conceptual tools examiners use to test deep understanding.

Classification of Fully Dualizable Objects in Symmetric Monoidal (∞,n)-Categories

The cobordism hypothesis asserts that a fully extended topological quantum field theory of dimension n is uniquely determined by its value on a point, which must be a fully dualizable object in the target symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C. Full dualizability means that the object admits duals at every level up to n: a left and right dual 1‑morphism, dual 2‑morphisms for the evaluation and coevaluation maps, and so on up to n‑morphisms. In practice, for n = 1 this reduces to the familiar notion of a dualizable object in a monoidal category (finite‑dimensional vector spaces, for example). For n = 2 the object must be a separable Frobenius algebra in a braided monoidal category, and for n = 3 it must be a spherical fusion category regarded as an E₃‑algebra. Lurie’s proof shows that the space of fully extended TQFTs Bordₙ → C is equivalent to the maximal ∞‑groupoid of fully dualizable objects in C. This classification is not merely existential; it provides a concrete recipe: given a fully dualizable object X, the value of the theory on a closed k‑manifold is obtained by iterating the duality data along a handle decomposition. The higher duality data encode the gluing axioms and guarantee invariance under Morse moves. Consequently, any construction that produces a fully dualizable object — such as the Drinfeld center of a fusion category, a modular tensor category, or an Eₙ‑algebra in chain complexes — immediately yields a fully extended TQFT. The classification also clarifies why certain state‑sum models (Turaev‑Viro, Crane‑Yetter) are fully extended: their input categories are precisely the fully dualizable objects in the appropriate higher Morita category of fusion categories.

Higher Morita Theory and Eₙ‑Algebras in Extended TQFT

Higher Morita theory organizes Eₙ‑algebras in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)-category into an (∞,n)-category Algₙ(C) whose objects are Eₙ‑algebras, 1‑morphisms are bimodules, 2‑morphisms are bimodule maps, and so on. The cobordism hypothesis identifies the target category C for an n‑dimensional fully extended TQFT with Algₙ(C) when C is the (∞,1)-category of chain complexes, vector spaces, or more generally a stable ∞‑category. An Eₙ‑algebra A determines a factorization homology functor ∫₋ A : Mfdₙ → C which satisfies excision and therefore extends to a fully extended TQFT Z_A : Bordₙ → C. The Morita equivalence of Eₙ‑algebras corresponds exactly to the equivalence of the resulting TQFTs. For n = 2, an E₂‑algebra is a braided monoidal category; Morita equivalence coincides with the equivalence of their Drinfeld centers as modular tensor categories. For n = 3, an E₃‑algebra is a symmetric monoidal category; a spherical fusion category C gives an E₃‑algebra via its Drinfeld center Z(C), and the associated TQFT is the Turaev‑Viro theory. The higher Morita perspective also explains the relation between Reshetikhin‑Turaev and Turaev‑Viro theories: the RT theory is the factorization homology of the modular tensor category Z(C) regarded as an E₂‑algebra, while the TV theory is the factorization homology of C regarded as an E₃‑algebra. The two are related by the fact that the E₃‑algebra structure on C factors through the forgetful map E₃ → E₂, and the resulting TQFTs satisfy |TV|² = RT on closed 3‑manifolds when C is spherical. This Morita‑theoretic framework is the modern language for classifying gapped topological phases of matter: each phase corresponds to a Morita class of Eₙ‑algebras, and the cobordism hypothesis guarantees that the classification of fully extended TQFTs matches the classification of such phases.

Technique: Identifying Fully Dualizable Objects via Higher Morita Theory
Given a spherical fusion category C, explain why C regarded as an E₃‑algebra is fully dualizable in the (∞,3)-category of E₃‑algebras in vector spaces, and deduce that the associated Turaev‑Viro TQFT is fully extended.
A spherical fusion category C is a finite semisimple rigid monoidal category with a pivotal structure that makes left and right traces coincide. In the (∞,3)-category Alg₃(Vect) of E₃‑algebras in vector spaces, objects are E₃‑algebras (symmetric monoidal categories), 1‑morphisms are bimodule categories, 2‑morphisms are bimodule functors, and 3‑morphisms are natural transformations. The dualizability data for C are constructed as follows: the dual object is the opposite category C^op with reversed monoidal product; the evaluation 1‑morphism is the bimodule category C regarded as a (C ⊠ C^op)-module via the tensor product; the coevaluation 1‑morphism is the same category with reversed actions. Because C is fusion, these bimodule categories are invertible up to equivalence, providing the required adjoints at level 1. At level 2, the evaluation and coevaluation bimodules admit duals given by the internal Hom categories, which exist because C is finite semisimple. At level 3, the pivotal spherical structure supplies the necessary invertible 3‑morphisms (the spherical isomorphism between left and right traces). Hence C is fully dualizable. By the cobordism hypothesis, the fully dualizable object C determines a unique fully extended TQFT Z_C : Bord₃ → Alg₃(Vect). The value of Z_C on a closed 3‑manifold M is the factorization homology ∫_M C, which by definition is the Turaev‑Viro state sum. Therefore the Turaev‑Viro invariant extends to a fully extended TQFT.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks to prove that a given categorical input yields a fully extended TQFT, first verify the dimension n and identify the appropriate (∞,n)-category of Eₙ‑algebras. Then check the dualizability conditions level by level: existence of dual objects, invertibility of evaluation/coevaluation bimodules, and the pivotal or spherical structure for the top level. Cite the cobordism hypothesis as the classification theorem that turns full dualizability into a fully extended TQFT. Avoid merely stating “C is fusion therefore fully dualizable”; explicitly name the duality data at each level.

Boundary Conditions, Defects, and the Cobordism Hypothesis with Singularities

Extended TQFTs naturally accommodate boundary conditions and topological defects by enlarging the cobordism category to include manifolds with corners and stratified singularities. In the (∞,n)-category Bordₙdef of n‑dimensional cobordisms with codimension‑k defects labeled by objects of a target (∞,n)-category C, a fully extended TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bordₙdef → C that sends a point with a label X to the object X itself. The cobordism hypothesis with singularities (proved by Lurie and extended by Ayala–Francis–Tanaka) states that such functors are classified by fully dualizable objects equipped with additional structure: a boundary condition corresponds to a 1‑morphism from the unit to X, a line defect to a 2‑morphism, and so on. Concretely, for a 3‑dimensional Turaev‑Viro theory based on a spherical fusion category C, a boundary condition on a 2‑manifold is a module category over C; a line defect is a bimodule category; a point defect is a module functor. The state‑sum on a manifold with boundary is computed by placing the module category data on the boundary triangles, and the gluing axioms follow from the associativity of module categories. This framework also captures the folding trick: a boundary condition for a theory Z is equivalent to a domain wall between Z and the trivial theory. In condensed matter, gapped boundaries of a topological phase correspond to Lagrangian algebras in the modular tensor category Z(C), and the classification of such boundaries matches the classification of module categories over C. The hypothesis with singularities thus provides a unified mathematical language for bulk‑boundary correspondence, anyon condensation, and the classification of topological defects.

Modular Tensor Categories, the Verlinde Formula, and State Sum Invariants

A modular tensor category (MTC) is a braided fusion category with a non‑degenerate S‑matrix. The Verlinde formula expresses the fusion coefficients Nij^k in terms of the S‑matrix: Nij^k = Σ_m (Sim Sjm Skm^*) / S0m. This formula is a direct consequence of the fact that the MTC is the representation category of a rational conformal field theory, but it also follows purely from the categorical axioms. In the Reshetikhin‑Turaev construction, the invariant of a closed 3‑manifold presented by surgery on a framed link L is ZRT(M) = κ-σ(L) Σcolorings Πcomponents S0,color Πcrossings R‑symbols, where κ is a normalization factor and σ(L) is the signature of the linking matrix. The Turaev‑Viro invariant for a spherical fusion category C is defined by a state sum over triangulations: ZTV(M) = Σedge labelings Πtetrahedra |6j‑symbol|². When C is the Drinfeld center Z(D) of a spherical fusion category D, the MTC Z(D) is modular and the TV invariant of D equals |ZRT(Z(D))|². The Verlinde formula appears in the TV state sum because the 6j‑symbols of Z(D) are built from the S‑matrix of the MTC. Moreover, the dimension of the Hilbert space assigned to a genus‑g surface Σ_g in the RT theory is given by the Verlinde formula dim H(Σ_g) = Σ_i (S0i)2-2g. This dimension coincides with the number of conformal blocks in the associated CFT and with the ground‑state degeneracy of the topological phase on Σ_g. The interplay between the Verlinde formula, the S‑matrix, and state‑sum invariants is a central computational tool: it allows one to compute TV invariants from modular data without explicit triangulations, and to verify the equality |TV|² = RT for modular categories. In exam problems, you may be asked to compute the S‑matrix of a given MTC (e.g., SU(2)_k) and then use the Verlinde formula to find fusion rules or the genus‑g Hilbert space dimension.

Anomaly Inflow, Bulk‑Boundary Correspondence, and Invertible Phases

Anomalies in quantum field theory are obstructions to gauging a symmetry or to defining a theory on arbitrary manifolds. In the language of extended TQFT, an anomalous n‑dimensional theory corresponds to a fully extended TQFT with values in a target (∞,n)-category that has a non‑trivial invertible object in dimension n+1. The anomaly inflow mechanism states that the anomaly of the n‑dimensional theory is canceled by the bulk (n+1)-dimensional invertible TQFT evaluated on a manifold that bounds the n‑manifold. Formally, if Z_anom : Bordₙ → C is an anomalous theory, there exists an invertible (n+1)-dimensional theory Z_bulk : Bordn+1 → C such that the restriction of Z_bulk to the boundary reproduces Z_anom. In the cobordism hypothesis, invertible theories are classified by maps from the cobordism category to the Picard ∞‑groupoid of C, i.e., by cohomology classes (e.g., Hn+1(BG; U(1)) for a finite group symmetry G). For topological phases of matter, an invertible phase is a short‑range entangled state with no anyonic excitations; its partition function is a topological invariant of the manifold (e.g., the Arf invariant for a 2‑dimensional fermionic invertible phase). The bulk‑boundary correspondence then says that the boundary of an invertible (n+1)-dimensional phase hosts an anomalous n‑dimensional theory. In the context of the Turaev‑Viro and Reshetikhin‑Turaev theories, the framing anomaly of the RT theory (dependence on a choice of 2‑framing) is an invertible 3‑dimensional theory given by the signature of a 4‑manifold. The TV theory, being non‑anomalous, does not require a framing. When a spherical fusion category C is not modular, its Drinfeld center Z(C) is modular and the TV theory of C is the boundary of the invertible 4‑dimensional Crane‑Yetter theory based on Z(C). This perspective unifies the classification of symmetry‑protected topological phases (invertible theories) with the classification of topologically ordered phases (non‑invertible theories) under the cobordism hypothesis.

  • Confusing the level of dualizability required for a given dimension: n‑dimensional fully extended TQFTs need fully dualizable objects in an (∞,n)-category, not merely dualizable objects in a 1‑category.
  • Assuming every fusion category yields a Turaev‑Viro state sum; the category must be spherical (pivotal with equal left and right traces) for the state sum to be triangulation independent.
  • Thinking the Verlinde formula holds for any braided fusion category; it requires modularity (non‑degenerate S‑matrix).
  • Neglecting the framing anomaly when applying the cobordism hypothesis to oriented theories: oriented fully extended TQFTs correspond to SO(n)-fixed points, not just fully dualizable objects.
  • Treating factorization homology as only a computational tool; the theorem states that every fully extended TQFT arises from an Eₙ‑algebra, so factorization homology is a classification, not merely a method.
  • Believing that all modular tensor categories give universal braiding for quantum computation; Ising (SU(2)₂) and SO(3)₃ are counterexamples.
  • Overlooking that the equality |TV|² = RT holds only when the input category is a spherical fusion category and the RT theory is built from its Drinfeld center.

Extended TQFTs with Tangential Structures and G‑Structures

The cobordism hypothesis in its most general form classifies fully extended topological quantum field theories valued in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category 𝒞 not only for oriented manifolds but for manifolds equipped with a tangential structure. A tangential structure is a map of ∞‑groups ξ: G → O(n) (or GL(n)) that lifts the stable tangent bundle of an n‑manifold to a G‑bundle. When ξ is the identity O(n) → O(n) one recovers unoriented theories; when ξ is the inclusion SO(n) → O(n) one obtains oriented theories; when ξ is the map BSpin(n) → BO(n) one gets spin theories, and so on. The (∞,n)‑category Bordₙ^ξ of n‑dimensional cobordisms with ξ‑structure has objects (n‑1)‑manifolds with ξ‑structure, morphisms n‑manifolds with ξ‑structure on their tangent bundle, and higher morphisms given by diffeomorphisms preserving the ξ‑structure. The cobordism hypothesis states that the (∞,n)‑category of symmetric monoidal functors Fun^⊗(Bordₙ^ξ, 𝒞) is equivalent to the (∞,n)‑category of fully dualizable objects in 𝒞 equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the action of the ∞‑group G on the space of such objects. Concretely, a fully extended TQFT with ξ‑structure corresponds to a fully dualizable object X ∈ 𝒞 together with a trivialization of the G‑action on X induced by the map ξ. For example, an oriented 3‑dimensional TQFT corresponds to a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,3)‑category with an SO(3)‑homotopy fixed point structure; a spin 3‑dimensional TQFT requires a Spin(3)‑fixed point. This formulation explains why the Reshetikhin‑Turaev theory, built from a modular tensor category, naturally carries a framing anomaly: the relevant tangential structure is a 2‑framing, i.e. a lift to the 3‑connected cover of SO(3), and the anomaly is measured by the obstruction to extending the SO(3)‑fixed point to a 2‑framing fixed point. In practice, to construct a TQFT with a given G‑structure one first identifies the fully dualizable objects in the target category, then computes the homotopy fixed points of the G‑action. For finite groups G, this reduces to the classification of G‑equivariant fully dualizable objects, which underlies the construction of symmetry‑protected topological phases.

The (∞,n)-Category of Cobordisms with Corners and Its Generators

The (∞,n)‑category Bordₙ^ξ is not merely a formal gadget; it admits a concrete description in terms of manifolds with corners. An object of Bordₙ^ξ is a closed (n‑1)‑manifold with ξ‑structure. A 1‑morphism is an n‑manifold with corners of codimension ≤ 1 whose boundary is decomposed into an incoming and an outgoing (n‑1)‑manifold, each carrying the induced ξ‑structure. Higher morphisms are given by diffeomorphisms relative to the boundary, and by cobordisms between cobordisms, i.e. manifolds with corners of higher codimension. The Baez‑Dolan cobordism hypothesis with corners asserts that Bordₙ^ξ is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals generated by a single object: the positively framed point (or more generally the point equipped with a chosen ξ‑structure). In the language of (∞,n)‑categories, this means that every k‑morphism in Bordₙ^ξ can be built from the elementary k‑handle attachments. For n = 2, the generators are the 0‑handle (a disc), the 1‑handle (a pair of pants), and the 2‑handle (a cap), together with the duality data (cups and caps) that implement the evaluation and coevaluation maps. For general n, the generators correspond to the standard handlebodies D^k × Dn‑k for k = 0,…,n, each equipped with the canonical ξ‑structure induced from the trivial framing on ℝⁿ. The symmetric monoidal structure is given by disjoint union, and the duality is implemented by reversing the orientation of the handles. This handlebody presentation is crucial for computations: any fully extended TQFT Z: Bordₙ^ξ → 𝒞 is completely determined by the image of the positively framed point, which must be a fully dualizable object X ∈ 𝒞, together with the images of the handle attachments, which are forced to be the evaluation, coevaluation, and higher duality morphisms associated to X. Consequently, the classification of fully extended TQFTs reduces to the classification of fully dualizable objects with ξ‑fixed point structure in 𝒞.

Technique
Given a symmetric monoidal (∞,2)-category 𝒞 and a fully dualizable object X ∈ 𝒞, describe how to construct the 2‑dimensional oriented TQFT Z_X corresponding to X, and explain why the partition function of Z_X on a closed oriented surface Σ_g of genus g is given by the categorical trace of the identity on X⊗ g.
The oriented 2‑dimensional cobordism category Bord₂SO(2) is generated by the point with its standard SO(2)‑structure. A fully dualizable object X in 𝒞 carries a canonical SO(2)‑homotopy fixed point because the circle action on the space of fully dualizable objects factors through the Serre automorphism, which is trivial for oriented theories. The functor Z_X sends the point to X. The 1‑handle (pair of pants) maps to the multiplication morphism μ: X ⊗ X → X, the 0‑handle (disc) maps to the unit η: 1 → X, the 2‑handle (cap) maps to the counit ε: X → 1, and the duality data (cup and cap) are the evaluation ev: X* ⊗ X → 1 and coevaluation coev: 1 → X ⊗ X* coming from the dualizability of X. A closed surface Σ_g can be decomposed into g pairs of pants glued together with g caps. Under Z_X this yields the morphism (ε ∘ μ⊗ g) ∘ (η⊗ g) which is precisely the categorical trace of idX⊗ g. In a pivotal category this trace equals the quantum dimension of X raised to the power g, reproducing the Verlinde formula for the Hilbert space dimension when 𝒞 is the category of representations of a quantum group.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks for the partition function of an extended TQFT on a closed manifold, first identify the handle decomposition of the manifold, then translate each handle into the corresponding duality morphism of the fully dualizable object. The answer is almost always a categorical trace or a higher trace; write it in terms of evaluation, coevaluation, and the Serre automorphism if the theory is unoriented.

Higher Gauge Theory and Extended Topological Field Theories

Higher gauge theory provides a physical realisation of extended TQFTs in which the fields are higher‑degree differential forms or, more geometrically, connections on higher principal bundles (n‑gerbes). A 1‑gauge theory has a Lie group G and a connection 1‑form A; its action is the Chern‑Simons functional ∫ Tr(A ∧ dA + ⅔ A ∧ A ∧ A) in three dimensions, yielding the Reshetikhin‑Turaev TQFT after quantisation. A 2‑gauge theory involves a crossed module (H → G) or a 2‑group, with a 1‑form A valued in Lie(G) and a 2‑form B valued in Lie(H) satisfying a fake curvature condition. The action is a higher Chern‑Simons term ∫ ⟨B ∧ F_A⟩ + …, and its quantisation produces a 3‑dimensional extended TQFT whose value on a point is the 2‑category of representations of the 2‑group. In general, an n‑gauge theory is governed by an n‑group (a group object in (∞,n‑1)‑groupoids) and its connections are described by differential cohomology in degree n+1. The cobordism hypothesis predicts that the resulting extended TQFT is classified by a fully dualizable object in the (∞,n)‑category of n‑vector spaces (or more generally in the target (∞,n)‑category of the theory). For example, the 3‑dimensional Dijkgraaf‑Witten theory with finite gauge group G is the extended TQFT associated to the fully dualizable object given by the category of G‑graded vector spaces in 2‑Vect. Its higher gauge fields are flat G‑bundles, which are 1‑gerbes with connection. The partition function on a closed 3‑manifold M is |Hom(π₁(M), G)| / |G|, matching the state‑sum construction. Higher gauge theories also naturally incorporate defects: a codimension‑k defect corresponds to a boundary condition for the (n‑k)‑form field, which in the extended TQFT language is a k‑morphism in the target category. This perspective unifies the description of Wilson lines, surface operators, and higher‑codimension defects within a single categorical framework.

Symmetry-Protected Topological Phases and the Cobordism Classification

Symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phases are short‑range entangled phases of matter that are trivial without symmetry but become distinct when a global symmetry G is imposed. In the language of extended TQFT, an SPT phase in n spatial dimensions corresponds to an invertible (n+1)‑dimensional TQFT with a G‑symmetry, i.e. a symmetric monoidal functor Z: Bordn+1BG → 𝒞 where BG is the classifying space of G (viewed as a tangential structure via the trivial map BG → BO(n+1)). The invertibility means that Z(M) is an invertible object in 𝒞 for every closed (n+1)‑manifold M, and the partition function is a U(1)‑valued topological invariant. The cobordism hypothesis identifies such theories with homotopy classes of maps from the Madsen‑Tillmann spectrum MTG to the Picard spectrum of 𝒞. For finite G and 𝒞 = n‑Vect_ℂ, this reduces to group cohomology: invertible (n+1)‑dimensional TQFTs with G‑symmetry are classified by Hn+1(BG; U(1)). This matches the well‑known group‑cohomology classification of bosonic SPT phases. For fermionic systems, the relevant tangential structure is a spin or pin structure together with a G‑bundle, leading to classification by spin cobordism groups ΩSpinn+1(BG) or by the Anderson dual of the spin cobordism spectrum. The bulk‑boundary correspondence states that the boundary of an invertible (n+1)‑dimensional bulk theory is an anomalous n‑dimensional theory whose anomaly is precisely the bulk invariant. For instance, the 2+1‑dimensional integer quantum Hall effect (a U(1) SPT) has a 3+1‑dimensional bulk described by the Chern‑Simons term (k/4π) ∫ A ∧ dA; its boundary hosts a chiral anomaly captured by the same k. In the extended TQFT framework, the bulk theory is an invertible 4‑dimensional TQFT with U(1) symmetry, and the boundary theory is a non‑invertible 3‑dimensional TQFT (the Reshetikhin‑Turaev theory at level k) whose anomaly is cancelled by the bulk. This anomaly inflow mechanism is a direct consequence of the cobordism hypothesis with singularities, where the singularity is the boundary itself.

Hochschild Homology, Factorization Homology, and the Cobordism Hypothesis

Factorization homology provides a universal construction that assigns to each Eₙ‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)‑category 𝒞 a fully extended n‑dimensional TQFT ∫‑ A: Bordₙfr → 𝒞, where fr denotes the framing tangential structure. The value of this TQFT on a framed n‑manifold M is the factorization homology ∫_M A, which can be computed as a colimit over the Ran space of M of the factorization algebra built from A. When M = Sⁿ, the factorization homology ∫Sⁿ A is equivalent to the n‑fold Hochschild homology HH(n)(A), also known as the topological chiral homology of A on the n‑sphere. For n = 1, this recovers the ordinary Hochschild homology HH(A) = A ⊗A⊗Aop A, which is the trace of the identity bimodule. For n = 2, ∫S² A is the 2‑dimensional Hochschild homology, which computes the Drinfeld center Z(A) when A is a fusion category. The cobordism hypothesis asserts that every fully extended framed TQFT arises in this way from a unique Eₙ‑algebra. Consequently, the classification of framed TQFTs is equivalent to the classification of Eₙ‑algebras in 𝒞. The relationship with higher Morita theory is that the (∞,n)‑category of Eₙ‑algebras, bimodules, and higher morphisms is precisely the (∞,n)‑category of fully dualizable objects in the (∞,n)‑category Algₙ(𝒞) of Eₙ‑algebras. The factorization homology of a manifold with boundary computes the value of the TQFT on that manifold, and the gluing formula for factorization homology corresponds to the composition of morphisms in the cobordism category. This perspective also clarifies the appearance of the Verlinde formula: for a modular tensor category 𝒞 viewed as an E₂‑algebra in 2‑Vect, the factorization homology of a genus‑g surface Σ_g is the Hochschild homology of the E₂‑algebra, which evaluates to the space of conformal blocks, whose dimension is given by the Verlinde formula. Thus, factorization homology not only constructs TQFTs but also provides a computational bridge between algebraic data (Eₙ‑algebras) and topological invariants (partition functions, Hilbert spaces).

  • Confusing the tangential structure required for a given symmetry class: spin, pin⁺, pin⁻, and oriented structures are not interchangeable and lead to different cobordism groups.
  • Assuming that every fully dualizable object in an (∞,n)‑category automatically carries a G‑fixed point for any G; the fixed point condition is a non‑trivial homotopical constraint.
  • Treating higher gauge theory as merely a generalisation of Chern‑Simons theory without recognising that the gauge fields are higher gerbes whose classification involves differential cohomology.
  • Overlooking that the group‑cohomology classification Hn+1(BG; U(1)) applies only to bosonic SPT phases with finite G; fermionic phases require spin cobordism.
  • Believing that factorization homology is only a computational tool; the theorem states it is a bijection between Eₙ‑algebras and framed extended TQFTs.
  • Neglecting the role of the Serre automorphism in unoriented theories: the partition function on a non‑orientable manifold involves the Serre automorphism of the fully dualizable object.
  • Misidentifying the Drinfeld center of a fusion category with its Hochschild homology in degree 1; the center is the 2‑dimensional Hochschild homology (factorization homology on S²).

The advanced topics above complete the core theoretical toolkit needed to tackle research‑level problems in extended topological field theory.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Corners and Stratified Spaces

The original cobordism hypothesis of Baez and Dolan, proved in full generality by Lurie, concerns the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category Bordn of closed n‑manifolds and cobordisms between them. Many physical theories, however, involve manifolds with boundaries, corners, and embedded defects of various codimensions. Ayala and Francis (2015) constructed the (∞,n)-category Bordncorner whose k‑morphisms are k‑manifolds with corners, and showed that it is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object — the point. The generators are the standard cornered disks Dk for 0 ≤ k ≤ n, together with the gluing maps that implement composition. Stratified spaces enter when one allows singularities along prescribed strata; Gaiotto and Johnson‑Freyd (2019) introduced the (∞,n)-category Bordnstrat whose objects are manifolds equipped with a stratification by singularity type. The cobordism hypothesis with singularities then states that symmetric monoidal functors Bordnstrat → 𝒞 are classified by fully dualizable objects in 𝒞 equipped with a compatible system of singularity data — i.e. a choice of 1‑morphisms, 2‑morphisms, …, n‑morphisms for each stratum satisfying the appropriate adjunction and duality conditions. This framework underpins the modern classification of defect topological quantum field theories, where a defect of codimension k corresponds to a k‑morphism in the target (∞,n)-category. In particular, the condensation completion of a higher category, which freely adds all possible defects, is precisely the value of the extended TQFT on a point in the stratified setting.

Extended TQFTs and the Classification of Topological Orders via Higher Categories

Topological order in condensed matter physics is the phenomenon of long‑range entanglement that cannot be described by symmetry breaking. In 2+1 dimensions the classification of gapped topological orders is given by modular tensor categories (MTCs), a result due to Witten (1989) and mathematically formalized by Turaev and Viro (1992). In 3+1 dimensions the analogous classification uses braided fusion 2‑categories, as shown by Kapustin and Thorngren (2014) and later by Johnson‑Freyd (2020). The cobordism hypothesis provides a unifying language: a fully extended n‑dimensional TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Bordn → 𝒞, and by the hypothesis such functors are in bijection with fully dualizable objects of 𝒞. For n = 3, taking 𝒞 = 3Vect (the (∞,3)-category of 3‑vector spaces) the fully dualizable objects are precisely fusion categories, and the resulting TQFT is the Turaev‑Viro theory. For n = 4, the target 𝒞 must be a symmetric monoidal (∞,4)-category such as 4Vect; fully dualizable objects there are braided fusion 2‑categories, yielding the Crane‑Yetter‑type state sums. When a global symmetry group G is present, one works with the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds equipped with a G‑structure (a map to BG). The classification of symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phases then becomes the computation of the homotopy groups of the mapping space Map(BG, 𝕀𝕟𝕧(𝒞)), where 𝕀𝕟𝕧(𝒞) denotes the (∞,n)-category of invertible objects. This recovers the group‑cohomology classification Hn+1(BG; U(1)) for bosonic phases and the spin cobordism classification ΩSpinn+1(BG) for fermionic phases, as proved by Freed and Hopkins (2016). Higher Morita equivalence of fusion categories corresponds to the equivalence of the associated extended TQFTs, so the classification of topological orders up to Morita equivalence is exactly the classification of fully extended TQFTs up to invertible natural transformations.

Exam Technique: Matching Tangential Structures to Physical Symmetries
A 3+1‑dimensional fermionic SPT phase is protected by time‑reversal symmetry T with T² = (−1)F. Which cobordism group classifies such phases?
The symmetry group is ℤ₂T with the non‑trivial action on fermion parity. The appropriate tangential structure is a pin⁻ structure, because pin⁻ manifolds are precisely those that admit a time‑reversal symmetry squaring to (−1)F. The classification of invertible fermionic phases with this symmetry in 3+1 D is given by the pin⁻ cobordism group ΩPin⁻₄(Bℤ₂T). Using the known computation ΩPin⁻₄ ≅ ℤ₁₆ (generated by the Arf‑Brown‑Kervaire invariant) and the fact that the map to Bℤ₂T is trivial on the generator, one finds ΩPin⁻₄(Bℤ₂T) ≅ ℤ₁₆. Hence there are 16 distinct phases, labelled by an integer modulo 16.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the classification of a topological phase, first identify the symmetry group and the statistics (bosonic/fermionic). Then recall the relevant cobordism group: oriented for bosonic, spin for fermionic with no time‑reversal, pin⁺ or pin⁻ for time‑reversal. Write down the group name and dimension; the answer is often the cobordism group itself, not a numerical invariant.

The Relationship Between Extended TQFTs and Conformal Field Theory via the Cardy Condition

Two‑dimensional rational conformal field theories (RCFTs) provide a rich source of modular tensor categories. The Reshetikhin‑Turaev construction assigns to an MTC 𝒞 a 2+1‑dimensional TQFT ZRT that is once‑extended (it assigns vector spaces to circles and linear maps to cobordisms). The boundary conditions of the RCFT correspond to module categories over 𝒞. Fuchs, Runkel, and Schweigert (2002) showed that consistent boundary conditions are in bijection with special symmetric Frobenius algebras A in 𝒞. The Cardy condition — the requirement that the annulus partition function computed in the open‑string channel equals that in the closed‑string channel — translates into the statement that the category of A‑modules is a module category over 𝒞 and that the bulk‑boundary map is given by the induction functor. In the language of extended TQFTs, the bulk theory is a 2‑dimensional TQFT with values in the 2‑category of module categories over 𝒞, and the boundary theory is a 1‑dimensional TQFT valued in the category of A‑modules. Factorization homology extends this picture to higher dimensions: an E₂‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)-category 𝒞 gives a fully extended 2‑dimensional TQFT ∫ − A, and the value on a circle is the Hochschild homology HH(A), which for a modular tensor category recovers the space of conformal blocks. The Verlinde formula then computes the dimension of this space as the trace of the modular S‑matrix. Thus the Cardy condition is the 2‑dimensional shadow of the higher‑categorical statement that the bulk‑boundary system is a morphism in the (∞,2)-category of E₂‑algebras.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of 4D TQFTs: Crane‑Yetter and Beyond

The Crane‑Yetter model (1993) was the first state‑sum construction of a 4‑dimensional topological quantum field theory. It starts from a modular tensor category 𝒞 (or more generally a premodular category) and assigns a partition function to a closed 4‑manifold by labelling the 2‑simplices of a triangulation with simple objects of 𝒞 and the 3‑simplices with 6j‑symbols. Mathematically, the Crane‑Yetter invariant is the evaluation of the 4‑dimensional TQFT associated to the fully dualizable object 𝒞 in the symmetric monoidal (∞,4)-category 4Vect. Barrett and Westbury (1996) generalized the construction to spherical fusion 2‑categories, showing that the state sum depends only on the Morita equivalence class of the input 2‑category. Douglas and Schommer‑Pries (2018) proved that the (∞,4)-category of fully dualizable objects in 4Vect is equivalent to the (∞,4)-category of braided fusion 2‑categories, so every fully extended 4‑dimensional framed TQFT arises from a braided fusion 2‑category. The Crane‑Yetter theory corresponds to the special case where the braided fusion 2‑category is the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) of a fusion category 𝒞, viewed as a braided fusion 2‑category with one object. Recent work by Johnson‑Freyd (2021) constructs 4‑dimensional TQFTs with defects by allowing the input to be a fusion 2‑category with a chosen algebra object; the resulting theory assigns 2‑categories to surfaces and 1‑categories to 3‑manifolds with boundary. These developments illustrate how higher categorical algebra — in particular the theory of E₄‑algebras and their modules — provides the complete algebraic data for 4‑dimensional extended TQFTs, exactly as predicted by the cobordism hypothesis.

Fermionic Topological Phases and the Spin Cobordism Hypothesis

Fermionic systems require a spin or pin structure on spacetime to define the fermion path integral. The classification of fermionic symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phases in d+1 dimensions with symmetry group G is given by the spin cobordism groups ΩSpind+1(BG) when G does not involve time‑reversal, and by pin⁺ or pin⁻ cobordism groups when time‑reversal is present. Freed and Hopkins (2016) proved the spin cobordism hypothesis: the (∞,d+1)-category of invertible spin TQFTs with target the (∞,d+1)-category of complex vector spaces is equivalent to the Anderson dual of the spin cobordism spectrum MSpin. Concretely, the group of deformation classes of invertible spin TQFTs in dimension d+1 is the generalized cohomology group (MSpin∧Iℤ)−d−1(pt), which is isomorphic to Hom(ΩSpind+1, U(1)) ⊕ Ext(ΩSpind, ℤ). In 1+1 D the only non‑trivial invariant is the Arf invariant, giving a ℤ₂ classification of fermionic SPT phases (the Kitaev chain). In 2+1 D the spin cobordism group ΩSpin₃ = 0, so there are no non‑trivial fermionic SPT phases without additional symmetry; with a U(1) charge conservation symmetry one obtains ΩSpin₃(BU(1)) ≅ ℤ, corresponding to the integer quantum Hall effect. For time‑reversal symmetric fermions with T² = (−1)F the relevant structure is pin⁻, and ΩPin⁻₄ ≅ ℤ₁₆ classifies the 3+1 D topological superconductors. The Gu‑Wen classification of 2+1 D fermionic topological orders uses spin modular tensor categories, which are modular tensor categories equipped with a distinguished fermion ψ satisfying ψ⊗ψ ≅ 1 and a spin structure on the associated 3‑manifold invariants. The cobordism hypothesis with tangential structure spin thus provides a complete mathematical framework for the classification of fermionic topological phases, unifying the algebraic data (spin modular categories) with the topological invariants (spin cobordism groups).

  • Confusing the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with corners with the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with singularities; they are distinct but related.
  • Assuming that every modular tensor category yields a fully extended 3D TQFT; the Reshetikhin‑Turaev theory is only 2+1D extended to 3D, not fully extended to points.
  • Overlooking that the Cardy condition in higher dimensions involves higher Frobenius algebras in higher categories, not just ordinary Frobenius algebras.
  • Treating the Crane‑Yetter invariant as a fully extended 4D TQFT without specifying the target (∞,4)-category; it is a once‑extended theory.
  • Neglecting the distinction between spin and pin⁺ structures when classifying time‑reversal symmetric fermionic phases; they lead to different cobordism groups.
  • Forgetting that the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with corners requires the target (∞,n)-category to admit duals for all k‑morphisms up to n, not just objects.
  • Misapplying the Freed‑Hopkins classification to bosonic phases; it applies specifically to invertible fermionic phases with spin structure.

Fully Extended 3D TQFTs from Fusion 2‑Categories and the Cobordism Hypothesis

The cobordism hypothesis asserts that a fully extended topological quantum field theory in dimension n is determined by a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category. For n=3 the target is often taken to be the (∞,3)-category 3Vect of 2‑vector spaces, whose objects are finite semisimple tensor categories, 1‑morphisms are bimodule categories, 2‑morphisms are functors, and 3‑morphisms are natural transformations. Douglas, Schommer‑Pries, and Snyder (2018) proved that the fully dualizable objects in 3Vect are precisely fusion 2‑categories — semisimple monoidal 2‑categories with simple unit and duals for all 1‑ and 2‑morphisms. Consequently every fully extended 3‑dimensional framed TQFT arises from a fusion 2‑category 𝒞.

The construction proceeds by assigning to a point the fusion 2‑category 𝒞, to an interval the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) viewed as a braided monoidal 2‑category, to a surface the 2‑category of module 2‑categories over Z(𝒞), and to a 3‑manifold the vector space obtained by a state‑sum over a triangulation labelled by simple objects, 1‑morphisms, and 2‑morphisms of 𝒞. The pentagonator and higher coherence data of the fusion 2‑category guarantee invariance under Pachner moves. When 𝒞 is the 2‑category of module categories over a fusion category 𝒟, the resulting theory coincides with the Turaev‑Viro‑Barrett‑Westbury state sum built from 𝒟. In this sense the fusion 2‑category framework strictly generalizes the modular tensor category input of the Reshetikhin‑Turaev theory, which yields only a once‑extended 3D TQFT (assigning vector spaces to surfaces but not categories to points).

Recent work by Johnson‑Freyd (2021) and by Gaiotto‑Johnson‑Freyd (2022) classifies all fully dualizable objects in 3Vect up to Morita equivalence, showing that they correspond to braided fusion 2‑categories with a chosen Lagrangian algebra. This classification matches the physical expectation that gapped 3D topological orders are in bijection with modular tensor categories, while fully extended theories require the richer algebraic structure of fusion 2‑categories. The cobordism hypothesis thus provides a conceptual bridge between higher categorical algebra and the classification of topological phases of matter.

Non‑Invertible Symmetries, Higher Categories, and Anomaly Inflow in Extended TQFT

Traditional symmetry in quantum field theory is described by a group G acting on local operators. In recent years it has become clear that many topological and conformal field theories possess non‑invertible symmetries — symmetries implemented by topological defects that do not have inverses under fusion. These defects form a fusion category (or fusion 2‑category in higher dimensions) rather than a group. The mathematical framework for such symmetries is provided by higher categories: a non‑invertible 0‑form symmetry in a d-dimensional theory is a fusion (d‑1)-category of topological defects, while a non‑invertible 1‑form symmetry is a braided fusion (d‑2)-category.

Anomaly inflow for non‑invertible symmetries is captured by extending the bulk theory by one dimension and coupling it to a symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phase whose boundary realizes the anomalous symmetry. In the language of the cobordism hypothesis, an anomalous theory with non‑invertible symmetry 𝒮 in dimension d corresponds to a fully extended (d+1)-dimensional TQFT whose value on the point is a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,d+1)-category equipped with a map from the (∞,d+1)-category of 𝒮-structured cobordisms. The anomaly is then the obstruction to trivializing this bulk theory, measured by a class in the appropriate cobordism group with coefficients in the higher categorical symmetry.

Concrete examples appear in 2D rational conformal field theories, where the Verlinde lines generate a modular tensor category 𝒞 of topological defects. The Cardy condition for boundary conditions is precisely the statement that the category of boundary conditions is a module category over 𝒞. In 3D, the work of Gaiotto‑Kapustin‑Seiberg‑Willett (2015) and later of Thorngren‑Wang (2019) shows that non‑invertible 1‑form symmetries in 3D gauge theories are described by braided fusion 2‑categories, and their anomalies are classified by the 4D cobordism groups of the corresponding higher group. The cobordism hypothesis with singularities (as developed by Ayala‑Francis‑Tanaka) provides the precise mathematical formulation: the bulk is a fully extended 4D TQFT valued in the (∞,4)-category of braided fusion 2‑categories, and the boundary theory is a 3D theory with a non‑invertible symmetry given by the image of the bulk defect category under the boundary functor.

Worked Example: Anomaly of a Non‑Invertible 1‑Form Symmetry in 3D
Consider a 3D topological gauge theory with gauge group G = SU(2) at level k = 2. The Wilson lines form the modular tensor category 𝒞 = Rep(Uq(sl2)) at q = eπi/4. The theory has a non‑invertible 1‑form symmetry generated by the simple object of spin 1/2. Compute the anomaly of this symmetry as an element of the spin cobordism group ΩSpin4(B2𝒞).
The non‑invertible 1‑form symmetry is described by the braided fusion 2‑category 𝒞 viewed as a 2‑group. Its classifying space B2𝒞 fits into a fibration B2𝒞 → B3U(1) with fiber BSU(2). The anomaly is the image of the generator of ΩSpin4(B3U(1)) ≅ ℤ under the map induced by the fibration. For k=2 the generator maps to 2 ∈ ℤ ≅ ΩSpin4(B2𝒞). Hence the anomaly is the element 2, which is non‑trivial but becomes trivial after stacking two copies of the theory. This matches the known fact that the SU(2)2 theory has a ℤ2 anomaly for its non‑invertible 1‑form symmetry.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the anomaly of a non‑invertible symmetry, identify the higher categorical symmetry (fusion category, braided fusion 2‑category, etc.), write down the relevant cobordism group with coefficients in its classifying space, and use the known map from the invertible symmetry cobordism group. Remember that the anomaly is always a torsion element for finite higher groups; its order divides the dimension of the symmetry category.

Factorization Algebras, Chiral Algebras, and the Extended Cobordism Hypothesis

Factorization algebras, introduced by Beilinson‑Drinfeld and developed by Costello‑Gwilliam, provide a local‑to‑global description of quantum field theories that is naturally compatible with the cobordism hypothesis. A factorization algebra on a manifold M assigns to each open set U a cochain complex F(U) together with structure maps for disjoint unions, satisfying a factorization property. When M is a disk, the factorization algebra is equivalent to an En-algebra in chain complexes. The cobordism hypothesis for En-algebras (Lurie, 2009) states that the (∞,n)-category of En-algebras in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category 𝒞 is equivalent to the (∞,n)-category of fully extended n-dimensional TQFTs valued in 𝒞 that are locally constant.

Chiral algebras on a complex curve are a special case of factorization algebras on a 2‑dimensional real manifold equipped with a holomorphic structure. The Beilinson‑Drinfeld construction associates to a chiral algebra a factorization algebra on the Ran space of the curve. In the context of the cobordism hypothesis, a chiral algebra gives rise to a 2‑dimensional extended TQFT valued in the (∞,2)-category of presentable categories, where the value on a point is the category of modules over the chiral algebra. The work of Francis‑Gaitsgory (2012) shows that the factorization homology of a chiral algebra over a surface computes the conformal blocks of the associated 2D CFT, and the gluing axioms of the CFT are precisely the factorization property.

Recent advances by Ayala‑Francis‑Rozenblyum (2020) extend this picture to higher dimensions: an En-algebra in the (∞,n)-category of k-vector spaces defines a fully extended n-dimensional TQFT whose value on a point is the En-algebra itself. The factorization homology of this En-algebra over a framed n-manifold recovers the state‑sum invariants of the corresponding TQFT. This provides a uniform construction of extended TQFTs from algebraic data, encompassing the Turaev‑Viro, Reshetikhin‑Turaev, and Crane‑Yetter theories as special cases where the En-algebra arises from a fusion category, modular tensor category, or braided fusion 2‑category respectively.

The Baez‑Dolan Stabilization Hypothesis and the (∞,n)‑Category of Cobordisms

The Baez‑Dolan stabilization hypothesis (1995) predicts that the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms Bordn becomes equivalent to the (∞,n+1)-category of cobordisms after one suspension, i.e. Bordn ≃ ΩBordn+1. Lurie's proof of the cobordism hypothesis (2009) implicitly uses this stabilization: the (∞,n)-category Bordnfr of framed cobordisms is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals on a single object. The stabilization hypothesis implies that the k-morphisms of Bordn for k close to n are invertible, and that the only non‑invertible data lives in low dimensions.

Concretely, the generators of Bordnfr are the point (0‑morphism), the interval (1‑morphism), and the n-dimensional framed disks with corners. The relations are given by the Morse‑Cerf theory of handle decompositions. The stabilization hypothesis guarantees that the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with corners (as defined by Ayala‑Francis‑Tanaka) is equivalent to the (∞,n)-category obtained by iterated span construction from the (∞,1)-category of manifolds with corners. This equivalence underpins the classification of fully dualizable objects: an object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category is fully dualizable iff it admits duals for all k-morphisms up to n, which is exactly the condition that the corresponding TQFT extends to the point.

The hypothesis also clarifies the relationship between the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with singularities (stratified spaces) and the ordinary cobordism category. A singularity of codimension k corresponds to a k-morphism in the higher category. The stabilization hypothesis implies that the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with singularities of codimension ≤ n is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals on a collection of objects indexed by the singularity types. This perspective was used by Freed‑Hopkins (2016) to classify invertible topological phases with symmetry by mapping the singular cobordism category into the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum.

Extended TQFTs from Vertex Operator Algebras and the Geometric Langlands Program

Vertex operator algebras (VOAs) provide a rigorous algebraic framework for the chiral half of a 2D conformal field theory. The work of Huang‑Lepowsky (1995) and later of Beilinson‑Drinfeld shows that a VOA gives rise to a factorization algebra on the complex plane, and hence to an E2-algebra in the category of vector spaces. By the cobordism hypothesis for E2-algebras, this yields a fully extended 2‑dimensional framed TQFT valued in the (∞,2)-category of categories. The value on a point is the category of modules over the VOA, which is a modular tensor category when the VOA is rational and C2-cofinite.

The geometric Langlands program predicts a deep relationship between the category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles on a curve and the category of representations of the Langlands dual group LG. In the language of extended TQFT, this is a statement about a 4‑dimensional topological gauge theory (the Kapustin‑Witten twist of N=4 super‑Yang‑Mills) whose boundary conditions are given by categories of D-modules and categories of representations. The cobordism hypothesis with singularities predicts that the 4‑dimensional theory is determined by a fully dualizable object in the (∞,4)-category of braided fusion 2‑categories. Recent work by Gaiotto‑Moore‑Witten (2021) identifies this object with the 2‑category of module 2‑categories over the braided fusion 2‑category Rep(LG) (or its quantum deformation at a root of unity). The geometric Langlands correspondence then emerges as the equivalence of the two boundary 3‑dimensional theories obtained by compactifying the 4‑dimensional theory on an interval with different boundary conditions.

This perspective unifies several seemingly disparate constructions: the Turaev‑Viro state sum from a modular tensor category, the Reshetikhin‑Turaev invariant from a VOA, and the Kapustin‑Witten 4D TQFT from the geometric Langlands duality. All are instances of the same principle — the cobordism hypothesis — applied to different fully dualizable objects in higher categories. The classification of fully dualizable objects in the (∞,4)-category of 2‑vector spaces (Douglas‑Schommer‑Pries, 2018) shows that the only inputs are braided fusion 2‑categories, and the geometric Langlands program provides a rich supply of such objects via quantum groups at roots of unity.

  • Confusing the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with corners with the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with singularities; they are distinct but related.
  • Assuming that every modular tensor category yields a fully extended 3D TQFT; the Reshetikhin‑Turaev theory is only 2+1D extended to 3D, not fully extended to points.
  • Overlooking that the Cardy condition in higher dimensions involves higher Frobenius algebras in higher categories, not just ordinary Frobenius algebras.
  • Treating the Crane‑Yetter invariant as a fully extended 4D TQFT without specifying the target (∞,4)-category; it is a once‑extended theory.
  • Neglecting the distinction between spin and pin⁺ structures when classifying time‑reversal symmetric fermionic phases; they lead to different cobordism groups.
  • Forgetting that the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with corners requires the target (∞,n)-category to admit duals for all k-morphisms up to n, not just objects.
  • Misapplying the Freed‑Hopkins classification to bosonic phases; it applies specifically to invertible fermionic phases with spin structure.

The Atiyah–Segal Axioms for Fully Extended Topological Quantum Field Theories

The original Atiyah axioms (1988) define a topological quantum field theory as a symmetric monoidal functor from the category of cobordisms of dimension n to the category of finite‑dimensional vector spaces. This formulation captures the assignment of a vector space to each closed (n−1)‑manifold and a linear map to each n‑dimensional cobordism, together with compatibility under disjoint union and gluing. The fully extended version replaces the ordinary cobordism category by the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category Bordn of n‑dimensional manifolds with corners, where objects are 0‑manifolds, 1‑morphisms are 1‑manifolds with boundary, up to n‑morphisms given by n‑manifolds with corners. A fully extended TQFT is then a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑functor Z : Bordn → C, where C is a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category (for example, the (∞,n)‑category of n‑vector spaces).

The axioms require that Z preserves the symmetric monoidal structure (disjoint union maps to tensor product) and sends the generating duality data in Bordn to duality data in C. Concretely, the value on a point must be a fully dualizable object of C — an object admitting duals for all k‑morphisms up to n. The cobordism hypothesis (proved by Lurie, 2009) states that the (∞,n)‑category of such functors is equivalent to the (∞,n)‑groupoid of fully dualizable objects in C. This equivalence upgrades the Atiyah–Segal axioms from a definition to a classification theorem: constructing a fully extended TQFT amounts to choosing a fully dualizable object.

In practice, the axioms impose a hierarchy of constraints. At level 0, one picks an object X ∈ C. At level 1, one must specify evaluation and coevaluation 1‑morphisms exhibiting X as dualizable. At level 2, one must provide 2‑morphisms satisfying the swallowtail equations, and so on up to level n. The existence of these higher duals is highly non‑trivial; for instance, in the (∞,3)‑category of fusion categories, a fusion category is fully dualizable iff it is non‑degenerate (i.e., a modular tensor category). The extended axioms also clarify the role of anomalies: an anomalous theory corresponds to a functor defined only on a subcategory of Bordn with tangential structure, and the anomaly itself is an invertible field theory classified by the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum.

The Classification of Two‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Calabi–Yau Categories

Two‑dimensional TQFTs in the Atiyah sense are classified by commutative Frobenius algebras: the value on a circle is a finite‑dimensional commutative Frobenius algebra, and the pair‑of‑pants cobordism gives the multiplication. When one extends the theory down to points, the target becomes a symmetric monoidal (∞,2)‑category, and the cobordism hypothesis identifies fully extended 2D TQFTs with fully dualizable objects in that target. In the (∞,2)‑category of categories (with Deligne tensor product), the fully dualizable objects are precisely the smooth and proper Calabi–Yau A∞‑categories (or, equivalently, Calabi–Yau categories in the sense of Kontsevich). This result, due to Costello (2004) and later refined by Lurie (2009), shows that a 2D extended TQFT is uniquely determined by a Calabi–Yau category C assigned to a point.

The Calabi–Yau condition means that C carries a non‑degenerate trace map HH•(C) → k[−d] for some integer d, where HH• denotes Hochschild homology. The value of the TQFT on a circle is then the Hochschild homology HH•(C), which inherits a Frobenius algebra structure from the Calabi–Yau trace. The pair‑of‑pants product corresponds to the convolution product on Hochschild homology induced by the composition in C. Higher genus surfaces are computed by iterated traces, reproducing the Gromov–Witten invariants of the associated topological string theory. In the A‑model, C is the Fukaya category of a symplectic manifold; in the B‑model, it is the derived category of coherent sheaves on a Calabi–Yau variety. The extended framework also accommodates open‑closed TQFTs, where boundary conditions correspond to objects of C and boundary‑changing operators to morphisms.

Worked Example: Recovering the Frobenius Algebra from a Calabi–Yau Category
Let C be the category of finite‑dimensional representations of a semisimple Hopf algebra H over ℂ, equipped with the Calabi–Yau structure given by the integral on H. Compute the value of the associated 2D extended TQFT on the circle and verify that it yields a commutative Frobenius algebra isomorphic to the centre Z(H).
The Hochschild homology of C = Rep(H) is canonically isomorphic to the centre Z(H) as an algebra. The Calabi–Yau trace on C induces the Frobenius form on Z(H) via the integral on H. The pair‑of‑pants cobordism corresponds to the convolution product on HH•(C), which under the identification HH•(Rep(H)) ≅ Z(H) becomes the multiplication in the centre. Commutativity follows from the braiding in Rep(H) induced by the universal R‑matrix of H. Thus the circle value is the commutative Frobenius algebra Z(H) with the trace given by the integral, matching the known classification of 2D TQFTs from semisimple Hopf algebras.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the classification of fully extended 2D TQFTs, state the target (∞,2)‑category (e.g., categories with Deligne tensor product) and the fully dualizable objects (smooth proper Calabi–Yau categories). Do not confuse this with the non‑extended classification by commutative Frobenius algebras. Remember that the circle value is Hochschild homology, not the original category.

The Douglas–Reutter Construction of Four‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Braided Fusion 2‑Categories

In 2018, Christopher Douglas and David Reutter gave an explicit construction of fully extended 4‑dimensional TQFTs valued in the (∞,4)‑category of 2‑vector spaces, using a braided fusion 2‑category as input. A braided fusion 2‑category is a semisimple monoidal 2‑category with a braiding, finite simple objects, and duals for all 1‑ and 2‑morphisms. The classification of fully dualizable objects in the (∞,4)‑category of 2‑vector spaces (Douglas–Schommer‑Pries, 2018) shows that braided fusion 2‑categories are precisely the 3‑dualizable objects, hence they define fully extended 4D TQFTs by the cobordism hypothesis.

The construction proceeds by assigning the braided fusion 2‑category B to a point. The value on a circle is the Drinfeld centre Z(B), which is a braided fusion 1‑category (a modular tensor category).
