The value on a 2‑sphere is the category of modules over Z(B), and the value on a 3‑sphere is the vector space of endomorphisms of the unit in that module category, which recovers the Crane–Yetter invariant of the 4‑manifold. The gluing formulas are implemented via the higher categorical trace (the 4‑dimensional analogue of the Hochschild homology trace). This construction generalizes the Crane–Yetter model (which uses a modular tensor category as input) and the Walker–Wang models (which use a braided fusion 1‑category). It also provides a physical realization of the 4‑dimensional topological gauge theory with gauge 2‑group given by the automorphism 2‑group of B.

An important feature is that the Douglas–Reutter TQFT is fully local: it assigns data to manifolds of all dimensions 0 through 4, and the value on a closed 4‑manifold is computed by a state‑sum over a triangulation using the 6j‑symbols of the braided fusion 2‑category. The theory is anomaly‑free precisely when the braided fusion 2‑category is non‑degenerate (i.e., its Drinfeld centre is trivial). If the centre is non‑trivial, the theory has a 3‑dimensional anomaly captured by the modular tensor category Z(B), consistent with the anomaly inflow picture.

Topological Modular Forms, the Cobordism Hypothesis, and Anomalous Topological Phases

Topological modular forms (TMF) is a generalized cohomology theory built from the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves. Stolz and Teichner (2011) proposed a geometric model for TMF using 2‑dimensional supersymmetric Euclidean field theories, and later work (e.g., by Gwilliam–Scheimbauer) connected this to the cobordism hypothesis for (∞,2)‑categories with spin structure. The cobordism hypothesis identifies fully extended 2D spin TQFTs with fully dualizable objects in a symmetric monoidal (∞,2)‑category of spin‑structured bordisms. The partition function of such a theory on a torus yields a modular form, and the collection of all such partition functions assembles into a class in TMF.

Anomalous topological phases correspond to invertible field theories that are not fully defined on all manifolds but only on those equipped with a tangential structure (e.g., spin, pin+, or orientation). The Freed–Hopkins classification (2016) uses the cobordism hypothesis to show that invertible n‑dimensional phases with symmetry group G are classified by the Anderson dual of the spin cobordism spectrum MSpin∧(BG). In particular, the group of deformation classes of invertible 2D spin TQFTs is isomorphic to TMF−2(pt) ≅ ℤ/24, generated by the Arf invariant. The Witten genus of a spin manifold, which takes values in TMF, appears as the partition function of the 2D supersymmetric sigma model, and its anomaly is measured by the index of the Dirac operator on the loop space.

This interplay has concrete consequences for the classification of symmetry‑protected topological phases. For example, the 3‑dimensional bosonic topological insulator with time‑reversal symmetry is classified by the spin cobordism group ΩSpin3(Bℤ2) ≅ ℤ2, and its boundary anomaly is a 2D theory whose partition function is the Arf invariant. The TMF perspective also explains the appearance of the 24‑fold periodicity in the classification of 2D fermionic phases, linking it to the homotopy groups of the sphere spectrum.

The Geometry of Moduli Spaces of Flat Connections and Extended Chern–Simons Theory

Chern–Simons theory with compact gauge group G at level k is a 3‑dimensional TQFT whose classical phase space on a closed surface Σ is the moduli space Mflat(Σ,G) of flat G‑connections modulo gauge transformations. The extended version of this theory, constructed by Freed, Teleman, and others, assigns to a point the modular tensor category C = Rep(Uq(g)) of representations of the quantum group at a root of unity q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)). This category is fully dualizable in the (∞,3)‑category of tensor categories, so by the cobordism hypothesis it defines a fully extended 3D TQFT.

The value on a circle is the Drinfeld centre of C, which is equivalent to C itself (since C is modular). The value on a closed surface Σ is the vector space of conformal blocks of the associated Wess–Zumino–Witten model, which can be identified with the space of holomorphic sections of a determinant line bundle over Mflat(Σ,G). The mapping class group of Σ acts projectively on this space, giving the Verlinde formula for the dimension. The gluing of 3‑manifolds along surfaces corresponds to the factorization homology of the E2‑algebra given by C, and the partition function on a closed 3‑manifold is the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant.

This geometric picture clarifies the relationship between the quantum group construction and the classical symplectic geometry of the moduli space. The extended TQFT provides a functorial quantization of the moduli space: the category assigned to a point is the category of boundary conditions (Wilson lines), the category assigned to an interval is the category of line defects, and the vector space assigned to a surface is the Hilbert space obtained by geometric quantization. The anomaly of the theory (the framing dependence) is captured by the central charge of the modular tensor category, which equals the level k times the dual Coxeter number.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the non‑extended Atiyah axioms (vector spaces assigned to (n−1)‑manifolds) with the fully extended axioms (data assigned down to points); the latter require a fully dualizable object in a higher category.
  • Assuming that every Calabi–Yau category yields a 2D extended TQFT without checking smoothness and properness; only smooth and proper Calabi–Yau categories are fully dualizable.
  • Thinking that the Douglas–Reutter construction works for any braided fusion 2‑category; the theory is anomaly‑free only when the braided fusion 2‑category is non‑degenerate (trivial Drinfeld centre).
  • Mixing up TMF as a cohomology theory with the classification of 2D spin TQFTs; TMF classifies deformation classes of supersymmetric theories, not all topological theories.
  • Believing that the moduli space of flat connections is the value of extended Chern–Simons theory on a surface; the value is the vector space of conformal blocks, which is the geometric quantization of that moduli space.
  • Overlooking that the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with corners requires the target (∞,n)‑category to admit duals for all k‑morphisms up to n, not just for objects.
  • Neglecting the distinction between spin and pin+ structures when applying the Freed–Hopkins classification; they lead to different cobordism groups and different anomaly classifications.

Pin and Pin⁺ Structures in the Cobordism Hypothesis and Fermionic Phase Classification

The spin cobordism hypothesis classifies invertible fermionic phases that admit a spin structure on spacetime. Many physical systems, however, require only a pin or pin⁺ structure — for example, time‑reversal symmetric topological insulators in three dimensions are naturally described by pin⁺ manifolds. The Freed–Hopkins theorem extends the cobordism classification to any tangential structure encoded by a map BG → BO. For pin structures the relevant groups are ΩPinn and ΩPin⁺n. The Anderson dual of the corresponding Thom spectra yields the groups of deformation classes of invertible n‑dimensional TQFTs with that structure. In particular, ΩPin⁺3(Bℤ₂) ≅ ℤ₂ classifies the 3D bosonic topological insulator with time‑reversal symmetry, while ΩPin2 ≅ ℤ₈ captures the 2D fermionic phases with only a pin structure. The Arf invariant, which generates the ℤ₂₄ periodicity in spin 2D theories, is replaced by the Arf–Brown invariant in the pin⁺ case, giving a ℤ₈ classification. These results are obtained by applying the cobordism hypothesis to the (∞,n)‑category of cobordisms equipped with the chosen tangential structure; the fully dualizable object is the invertible TQFT itself, and its value on a point is the trivial algebra in the target symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category. The computation of the relevant cobordism groups uses the Adams spectral sequence for the Thom spectra MPin and MPin⁺, together with the known homotopy groups of spheres. The physical consequence is that anomaly inflow for pin⁺ theories is governed by the η‑invariant of the Dirac operator on pin⁺ manifolds, while for pin theories it is governed by the Arf–Brown invariant. This distinction is crucial when constructing lattice models of symmetry‑protected topological phases that do not admit a spin structure.

Five‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 3‑Categories and the Cobordism Hypothesis

The cobordism hypothesis asserts that fully extended n‑dimensional TQFTs with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category C are classified by fully dualizable objects of C. For n = 5 the target category must admit duals for all k‑morphisms up to k = 5. A natural algebraic source of such objects is the (∞,5)‑category whose objects are fusion 3‑categories (or, more precisely, sylleptic monoidal 3‑categories with duals), 1‑morphisms are bimodule 3‑categories, and higher morphisms are given by higher Morita equivalences. Douglas and Reutter have shown that a non‑degenerate braided fusion 2‑category yields a 4D TQFT; the next step is to use a non‑degenerate sylleptic fusion 3‑category to produce a 5D theory. The non‑degeneracy condition means that the Müger centre (the higher categorical analogue of the Drinfeld centre) is trivial. When this holds, the fusion 3‑category is fully dualizable in the (∞,5)‑category of 3‑categories, and the cobordism hypothesis produces a unique extended 5D TQFT. The value of this theory on a point is the fusion 3‑category itself; on a circle it is its 3‑category of modules; on a surface it is a 2‑category obtained by factorization homology of the associated E₃‑algebra; on a 3‑manifold it is a linear category; on a 4‑manifold it is a vector space; and on a closed 5‑manifold it yields a scalar invariant. The state‑sum construction generalizes the Crane–Yetter model: one triangulates the 5‑manifold, labels 4‑simplices with simple objects of the fusion 3‑category, 3‑simplices with 1‑morphisms, and so on, and contracts using the higher associators and braidings. The resulting invariant is expected to detect exotic smooth structures on 5‑manifolds, just as the Crane–Yetter invariant detects certain 4‑manifold invariants. Recent work by Johnson‑Freyd and Scheimbauer has identified the precise higher categorical axioms (syllepsis, duals, non‑degeneracy) that guarantee full dualizability, and has computed the first examples using the 3‑category of 2‑vector spaces over a spherical fusion category.

Technique
Explain how the non‑degeneracy condition on a sylleptic fusion 3‑category ensures that the corresponding 5D extended TQFT is anomaly‑free, and describe the role of the Müger centre in this context.
A sylleptic fusion 3‑category C is fully dualizable in the symmetric monoidal (∞,5)‑category of 3‑categories precisely when it admits duals for all objects, 1‑morphisms, 2‑morphisms, and 3‑morphisms, and when its Müger centre Z₂(C) — the 3‑category of objects that braid trivially with every object of C — is equivalent to the trivial 3‑category. The Müger centre measures the failure of the braiding to be non‑degenerate; if it is non‑trivial, the theory has a non‑invertible topological defect of codimension one, which manifests as an anomaly in the partition function on closed 5‑manifolds. When Z₂(C) ≃ 1, the higher categorical analogue of the modular condition holds, and the state‑sum construction yields a well‑defined scalar invariant independent of the triangulation. This is the direct higher‑dimensional generalization of the fact that a modular tensor category (non‑degenerate braided fusion 1‑category) gives an anomaly‑free 3D TQFT, while a merely braided fusion 1‑category gives a theory with a boundary anomaly. Thus non‑degeneracy of the sylleptic fusion 3‑category is the algebraic criterion for an anomaly‑free 5D extended TQFT.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the algebraic input that classifies an n‑dimensional extended TQFT, recall the pattern: 2D → Calabi–Yau category, 3D → modular tensor category, 4D → braided fusion 2‑category, 5D → sylleptic fusion 3‑category. The key property at each step is non‑degeneracy of the appropriate higher centre (Müger centre). Memorize this ladder and the corresponding dualizability conditions; it turns a long essay into a structured list.

Higher Categorical Representations of Loop Groups and Extended Geometric Langlands

The geometric Langlands program predicts an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on the moduli stack of G‑bundles on a curve and the category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of LG‑local systems, where LG is the Langlands dual group. In the topological setting, this equivalence is realized by a 2D extended TQFT constructed from the representation theory of the loop group LG at level k. The category assigned to a point is the category of positive energy representations of the affine Kac–Moody algebra ĝ at level k, which is a modular tensor category. The cobordism hypothesis then promotes this to a fully extended 2D TQFT. To access the higher categorical structure of geometric Langlands, one considers 2‑representations of the loop group — i.e., module categories over the modular tensor category Repk(LG). These 2‑representations form a fusion 2‑category, which by the cobordism hypothesis yields a 3D extended TQFT. The value of this 3D theory on a circle is the Drinfeld centre of the fusion 2‑category, which recovers the original modular tensor category. On a surface, the theory assigns the category of conformal blocks of the associated Wess–Zumino–Witten model, matching the geometric quantization of the moduli space of flat G‑connections. The 4D lift involves the 3‑category of 2‑representations of the loop group, which is expected to be a sylleptic fusion 3‑category. Its non‑degeneracy would give a 4D TQFT whose boundary is the 3D theory, providing a topological realization of the S‑duality of 4D N=4 super Yang–Mills. Recent work by Gaitsgory and Lurie on the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence constructs the required higher categorical structures using factorization homology of the affine Grassmannian. The extended cobordism hypothesis thus provides a unifying framework: each categorical level of loop group representation theory produces an extended TQFT in one higher dimension, and the Langlands duality appears as an equivalence of the resulting theories.

Defects of Codimension Two and the (∞,n)‑Category of Stratified Cobordisms

The cobordism hypothesis with singularities, developed by Ayala, Francis, and Rozenblyum, extends the classification to manifolds equipped with a stratification and prescribed singularity data. A defect of codimension two corresponds to a surface operator in the physical theory. In the (∞,n)‑category Bordnstrat of stratified cobordisms, objects are (n−1)‑manifolds with a codimension‑two stratification, 1‑morphisms are n‑manifolds with corners and a codimension‑two singular locus, and higher morphisms are given by stratified diffeomorphisms. The generators of this category are handlebodies with a single singular disk of the appropriate dimension. A fully extended TQFT with codimension‑two defects is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bordnstrat → C. By the singular cobordism hypothesis, such functors are classified by fully dualizable objects in C equipped with a chosen 1‑morphism (the defect line) and a 2‑morphism (the defect junction) satisfying certain adjunction conditions. Concretely, for n = 3 the data is a modular tensor category C (the bulk), a module category M over C (the surface defect), and a functor M ⊠ M → M (the fusion of defects). The partition function on a 3‑manifold with a network of surface defects is computed by the state‑sum of the Turaev–Viro type, where the defect network is labeled by objects and morphisms of M. For n = 4 the bulk is a braided fusion 2‑category, the codimension‑two defect is a module 2‑category, and the defect fusion is a 2‑functor. The classification of such defect data is equivalent to the classification of separable algebra objects in the higher Morita category of the bulk. This framework explains the appearance of non‑invertible symmetries in higher dimensional QFTs: the defect network itself carries a higher categorical symmetry, and the anomaly inflow is captured by the failure of the defect data to be fully dualizable.

Moduli Stacks of Local Systems and the Extended Cobordism Hypothesis

The moduli stack LocG(Σ) of G‑local systems on a surface Σ is a derived Artin stack whose quasi‑coherent sheaves form a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)‑category. Ben‑Zvi, Francis, and Nadler showed that the factorization homology of the E₂‑algebra given by the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(g) computes the category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on LocG(Σ). This identifies the value of the extended Chern–Simons theory on Σ with the geometric quantization of the moduli stack. The extended cobordism hypothesis provides a conceptual explanation: the 3D TQFT is the factorization homology of an E₂‑algebra A in the (∞,3)‑category of tensor categories. The algebra A is the modular tensor category Rep(Uq(g)), which is fully dualizable. The cobordism hypothesis then guarantees that the factorization homology functor ∫− A : Bord3 → Vect is the unique extended TQFT with value A on a point. The moduli stack enters because the factorization homology of A over Σ is equivalent to the category of sheaves on the mapping stack Map(Σ, BG) ≃ LocG(Σ). This equivalence is a manifestation of the higher categorical version of the Atiyah–Bott–Goldman symplectic structure on the moduli space. For higher genus surfaces, the mapping class group action on the category of sheaves recovers the Verlinde formula for the dimension of conformal blocks. The same pattern persists in higher dimensions: the 4D TQFT from a braided fusion 2‑category computes factorization homology over 3‑manifolds, which is expected to be equivalent to the category of sheaves on the moduli stack of flat 2‑connections (a higher stack). Thus the extended cobordism hypothesis, factorization homology, and the geometry of moduli stacks form a triad that classifies and constructs extended TQFTs across all dimensions.

  • Confusing pin and pin⁺ cobordism groups; they differ by a factor of two in low dimensions and lead to distinct anomaly classifications.
  • Assuming that any fusion 3‑category yields a 5D TQFT; full dualizability requires syllepsis and trivial Müger centre.
  • Overlooking that the loop group 2‑representations form a fusion 2‑category only at levels where the affine Kac–Moody algebra is rational.
  • Treating codimension‑two defects as ordinary line defects; they require a stratified cobordism category and a module 2‑category over the bulk.
  • Identifying the moduli stack of local systems with the value of the TQFT on a surface; the value is the category of sheaves on that stack, not the stack itself.
  • Neglecting the role of the Adams spectral sequence in computing pin⁺ cobordism groups; the ℤ₈ periodicity in 2D pin⁺ theories comes from the image of J.
  • Believing that the Crane–Yetter model directly generalizes to 5D without higher associator data; the 5D state sum needs the full sylleptic structure of a fusion 3‑category.

Higher Categorical Structure of the Drinfeld Center and Extended 3D TQFTs

The Drinfeld center Z(C) of a fusion category C is a braided fusion category that carries a canonical modular tensor category structure when C is spherical. In the language of extended topological quantum field theory, the assignment C ↦ Z(C) lifts to a symmetric monoidal functor from the (∞,3)-category of fusion categories to the (∞,3)-category of modular tensor categories. This functor is precisely the value on a point of the fully extended 3‑dimensional TQFT constructed via the Reshetikhin–Turaev state sum. The cobordism hypothesis identifies this TQFT with the unique symmetric monoidal functor Bord₃ → Vect that sends a point to the fully dualizable object Z(C) in the symmetric monoidal (∞,3)-category of tensor categories.

At the level of 1‑morphisms, the functor assigns to an interval the bimodule category Z(C) viewed as a Z(C)–Z(C) bimodule. The 2‑morphisms are given by module functors, and the 3‑morphisms by natural transformations. The pivotal structure of C ensures that the duality data required by the cobordism hypothesis — evaluation and coevaluation 1‑morphisms, and the associated 2‑ and 3‑morphisms — are satisfied. In particular, the spherical structure provides a canonical isomorphism between the left and right duals of the object Z(C), which is the higher categorical incarnation of the modular S‑matrix.

Concretely, the partition function of the extended theory on a closed 3‑manifold M with a triangulation is computed by the Turaev–Viro state sum built from the 6j‑symbols of C. The same invariant can be obtained from the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction using the modular data of Z(C). The equivalence of these two state sums is a manifestation of the fact that the Drinfeld center is the higher categorical center of C, and that the cobordism hypothesis forces the value of the theory on a point to be a fully dualizable object whose center is itself. This perspective explains why the Turaev–Viro model is anomaly‑free: the anomaly would be an obstruction to extending the theory to a point, but the center construction guarantees full dualizability.

Recent work by Douglas, Schommer‑Pries, and Snyder shows that the assignment C ↦ Z(C) extends to a functor on the (∞,3)-category of separable fusion categories, and that the resulting extended TQFTs are precisely those whose bulk modular tensor category is a Drinfeld center. This classification matches the physical expectation that any (2+1)‑dimensional topological order with a gapped boundary arises from a fusion category, and its bulk is the Drinfeld center of that category.

Extended TQFTs from Conformal Nets and the Cobordism Hypothesis

Conformal nets provide an operator‑algebraic model for chiral conformal field theory. A conformal net 𝒜 assigns to each interval I ⊂ S¹ a von Neumann algebra 𝒜(I) satisfying isotony, locality, Möbius covariance, and the existence of a vacuum vector. The category Rep(𝒜) of representations of a completely rational conformal net is a modular tensor category. By the cobordism hypothesis, this modular tensor category determines a unique fully extended 3‑dimensional TQFT Z_𝒜 : Bord₃ → Vect.

The construction of Z_𝒜 proceeds by first forming the (∞,3)-category of conformal nets, where 1‑morphisms are defects between nets, 2‑morphisms are sectors, and 3‑morphisms are intertwiners. The fully dualizable object corresponding to 𝒜 is the category Rep(𝒜) equipped with its braided monoidal structure. The cobordism hypothesis then guarantees that the factorization homology of the E₂-algebra Rep(𝒜) over a 3‑manifold M computes the partition function of the associated TQFT. This factorization homology can be identified with the space of conformal blocks of the net 𝒜 on the boundary of M.

An important consequence is that the mapping class group of a surface Σ acts projectively on the category Z_𝒜(Σ), and this action coincides with the modular transformation properties of the characters of 𝒜. The Verlinde formula for the dimension of conformal blocks emerges as the trace of the identity functor on Z_𝒜(Σ). Moreover, the extended theory assigns to a point the category Rep(𝒜), to a circle the Drinfeld center Z(Rep(𝒜)) (which is equivalent to Rep(𝒜) ⊠ Rep(𝒜)^rev), and to a surface the category of modules over the corresponding E₂-algebra.

Recent results by Bartels, Douglas, and Henriques establish that the (∞,3)-category of conformal nets is equivalent to the (∞,3)-category of fully dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal (∞,3)-category of tensor categories. This equivalence realizes the cobordism hypothesis for a large class of rational CFTs and provides a rigorous bridge between the operator‑algebraic and categorical approaches to extended TQFT.

Worked Example: Drinfeld Center of Vec(G)
Let G be a finite group and Vec(G) the fusion category of G‑graded vector spaces. Compute the Drinfeld center Z(Vec(G)) and describe the resulting extended 3D TQFT.
The simple objects of Vec(G) are δ_g for g ∈ G. The Drinfeld center Z(Vec(G)) has simple objects labelled by pairs (C, ρ) where C is a conjugacy class of G and ρ is an irreducible representation of the centralizer Z_G(g) for a chosen g ∈ C. The braiding is given by the conjugation action. This modular tensor category is equivalent to Rep(D(G)), the representation category of the Drinfeld double of G. The extended TQFT ZVec(G) assigns to a point the category Rep(D(G)), to a circle the category of D(G)‑bimodules, and to a closed 3‑manifold M the Dijkgraaf–Witten invariant with gauge group G. The partition function is |G|-χ(M) Σ[A] 1, where the sum runs over gauge equivalence classes of flat G‑bundles on M.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the value of an extended TQFT on a point, identify the fully dualizable object in the relevant symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category. For 3D theories from fusion categories, the answer is the Drinfeld center; for theories from conformal nets, it is the representation category of the net. Remember that the cobordism hypothesis guarantees uniqueness once the point value is fixed.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Non-Orientable Tangential Structures and Pin⁻ Phases

The cobordism hypothesis extends to manifolds equipped with a tangential structure ξ : B → BO(n). For non‑orientable theories the relevant structures are Pin⁻ and Pin⁺, which are lifts of the structure group from O(n) to Pin⁻(n) or Pin⁺(n). The (∞,n)-category Bord_nPin⁻ has objects (n−1)-manifolds with a Pin⁻ structure, and morphisms are n‑dimensional Pin⁻ cobordisms. A fully extended TQFT with Pin⁻ structure is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord_nPin⁻ → C.

The classification of such theories is given by the singular cobordism hypothesis: they correspond to fully dualizable objects in C equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the action of the Pin⁻ group on the core of C. In low dimensions, the Pin⁻ cobordism groups are known: Ω₁Pin⁻ = 0, Ω₂Pin⁻ = ℤ₂, Ω₃Pin⁻ = 0, Ω₄Pin⁻ = ℤ₁₆. The ℤ₁₆ in dimension four classifies (3+1)‑dimensional fermionic invertible phases with time‑reversal symmetry satisfying T² = (−1)^F. The generator is the Arf‑Brown‑Kervaire invariant, and the 16‑fold periodicity reflects the Bott periodicity of the Pin⁻ spectrum.

For a Pin⁻ TQFT in dimension three, the value on a point is a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,3)-category with a Pin⁻‑equivariant structure. Concretely, this means a fusion category C equipped with a braided monoidal equivalence C ≃ C^rev that squares to the identity, together with a compatible pivotal structure. The resulting state‑sum invariant on a closed Pin⁻ 3‑manifold is a generalization of the Turaev–Viro model where the 6j‑symbols satisfy a modified pentagon equation reflecting the non‑orientable gluing rules.

Recent computations by Gaiotto and Johnson‑Freyd show that the Pin⁻ cobordism hypothesis predicts a ℤ₈ classification of (2+1)‑dimensional fermionic topological orders with time‑reversal symmetry, matching the known classification of symmetry‑protected topological phases in the tenfold way. The anomaly inflow for these phases is captured by the non‑triviality of the Pin⁻ structure on the 4‑dimensional bulk, which is measured by the η‑invariant of the Dirac operator on the bounding Pin⁻ 4‑manifold.

Higher Categorical Representations of Mapping Class Groups in Extended TQFT

In a fully extended n‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord_n → C, the mapping class group Γ(Σ) of a closed (n−1)-manifold Σ acts on the object Z(Σ) by monoidal autoequivalences. This action lifts to a functor BΓ(Σ) → Aut⊗(Z(Σ)), where BΓ(Σ) is the classifying space of the mapping class group and Aut⊗(Z(Σ)) is the 2‑group of monoidal autoequivalences of the category Z(Σ). The cobordism hypothesis implies that this representation is determined entirely by the fully dualizable object Z(pt).

For n = 3 and Z(pt) = C a modular tensor category, Z(Σ) is the category of modules over the E₂-algebra given by the factorization homology of C over Σ. The mapping class group action recovers the projective representation on the space of conformal blocks, and the higher categorical structure encodes the pentagon and hexagon equations for the braiding and associativity isomorphisms. In particular, the Dehn twist along a simple closed curve acts by the spherical twist functor associated to the corresponding simple object in C.

When C is the Drinfeld center of a fusion category D, the mapping class group action on Z(Σ) can be described in terms of the tube algebra of D. The higher representation theory of Γ(Σ) on the 2‑category of module categories over C provides a categorification of the Verlinde formula: the dimension of the space of invariants under a subgroup H ⊂ Γ(Σ) equals the trace of the corresponding functor on the Hochschild homology of the tube algebra.

Recent work by Ben‑Zvi, Francis, and Nadler identifies the category Z(Σ) with the category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack Loc_G(Σ) of G‑local systems, where G is the compact Lie group associated to C via the quantum group at a root of unity. Under this equivalence, the mapping class group action becomes the geometric action on the moduli stack, and the higher categorical representation is realized by the Fourier‑Mukai transforms associated to the Hecke correspondences on Loc_G(Σ).

Six-Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 4-Categories and the Cobordism Hypothesis

The cobordism hypothesis in dimension six predicts that fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFTs with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,6)-category C are classified by fully dualizable objects in C. A natural source of such objects is the (∞,6)-category of fusion 4‑categories, whose objects are fusion 4‑categories, 1‑morphisms are bimodule 3‑categories, and so on up to 6‑morphisms. A fusion 4‑category is a semisimple rigid monoidal 4‑category with simple unit and finite‑dimensional Hom‑spaces.

To be fully dualizable, a fusion 4‑category must satisfy a hierarchy of dualizability conditions: it must have duals for objects, 1‑morphisms, 2‑morphisms, 3‑morphisms, and 4‑morphisms, together with coherent evaluation and coevaluation data at each level. The highest non‑trivial condition is the existence of a syllepsis — a symmetric braiding on the 3‑morphisms — and the vanishing of the Müger center at the level of 4‑morphisms. These conditions are the direct higher‑dimensional analogues of the spherical and modular conditions for fusion 1‑categories.

When a fusion 4‑category 𝒞 is fully dualizable, the cobordism hypothesis produces an extended TQFT Z_𝒞 : Bord₆ → C. The value on a point is 𝒞, on a circle the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) (a braided fusion 3‑category), on a surface the 2‑category of modules over Z(𝒞), and so on. The partition function on a closed 6‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum generalization of the Crane–Yetter model, where the local weights are given by the 10j‑symbols of 𝒞 associated to the 4‑simplices of a triangulation.

Explicit examples of fusion 4‑categories are scarce. The most studied candidate is the 4‑category of 3‑vector spaces over a finite group G, which yields a 6‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with gauge 3‑group B³G. Another source comes from the representation 4‑category of a finite 3‑group, or from the 4‑category of module 3‑categories over a braided fusion 3‑category. The classification of fully dualizable fusion 4‑categories remains an open problem, but the cobordism hypothesis provides a precise framework for its solution.

  • Confusing the Drinfeld center of a fusion category with its monoidal center; the Drinfeld center is braided and modular when the category is spherical, while the monoidal center is only monoidal.
  • Assuming that every conformal net yields a fully extended TQFT; complete rationality is required for the representation category to be modular and fully dualizable.
  • Overlooking the distinction between Pin⁻ and Pin⁺ structures in anomaly classification; they differ by a factor of two in the relevant cobordism groups and lead to distinct periodicities (ℤ₈ vs ℤ₁₆).
  • Treating the mapping class group action on the category assigned to a surface as an ordinary linear representation; it is a higher categorical action by monoidal autoequivalences, and its decategorification gives the projective representation on conformal blocks.
  • Believing that a fusion 4‑category automatically defines a 6D TQFT; full dualizability demands syllepsis and trivial Müger centre, which are non‑trivial constraints.
  • Identifying the value of an extended TQFT on a surface with the moduli stack itself; the value is the category of sheaves on that stack, not the stack.
  • Neglecting the role of the Adams spectral sequence in computing Pin⁻ cobordism groups; the ℤ₁₆ periodicity in 4D Pin⁻ theories originates from the image of J in the stable homotopy groups of spheres.

Extended TQFTs from Spherical Fusion Categories and the Turaev–Viro State Sum

A spherical fusion category C is a finite semisimple rigid monoidal category equipped with a pivotal structure for which left and right traces coincide. The Turaev–Viro state‑sum construction assigns to a closed oriented 3‑manifold M a scalar invariant ZTV(M) by summing over labelings of a triangulation by simple objects of C with weights given by quantum 6j‑symbols. When C is spherical, the invariant is independent of the triangulation and defines a 3‑dimensional topological quantum field theory in the Atiyah–Segal sense. The cobordism hypothesis lifts this to a fully extended TQFT ZC : Bord3 → 3Vect whose value on a point is the 2‑category of module categories over C, on a circle the Drinfeld center Z(C) (a modular tensor category), and on a surface the category of modules over Z(C). Full dualizability of C in the symmetric monoidal (∞,3)‑category of fusion categories is equivalent to the spherical condition together with non‑degeneracy of the S‑matrix of Z(C). The extended theory satisfies the gluing axiom at all codimensions: the vector space assigned to a surface with boundary is the space of conformal blocks of the associated rational CFT, and the linear map assigned to a 3‑manifold with corners is computed by the state sum on a relative triangulation. This construction provides a concrete realisation of the cobordism hypothesis for 3‑dimensional theories and underlies the classification of (2+1)‑dimensional topological orders with gapped boundaries.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Spinc Structures and Fermionic Phases

A Spinc structure on a manifold is a lift of the structure group of the tangent bundle from SO(n) to Spinc(n) = (Spin(n) × U(1)) / ℤ₂. Unlike a Spin structure, a Spinc structure always exists on any oriented manifold, but its isomorphism classes are a torsor for H²(M; ℤ). The cobordism hypothesis for manifolds equipped with a tangential structure given by a map B → BO(n) states that fully extended n‑dimensional TQFTs with that structure are classified by fully dualizable objects in the target (∞,n)‑category equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(n)‑action induced by the structure. For Spinc the relevant classifying map is BSpinc → BO. The (∞,n)‑category of Spinc cobordisms is generated by the point with its unique Spinc structure, and the homotopy fixed point condition translates into the requirement that the dualizability data be compatible with the U(1)‑gerbe representing the determinant line bundle. In physics, Spinc TQFTs describe fermionic symmetry‑protected topological phases with a conserved U(1) charge (e.g. topological insulators). The partition function on a closed 4‑manifold M is a quadratic refinement of the intersection form twisted by the first Chern class of the determinant line, and the anomaly is captured by the η‑invariant of the Dirac operator coupled to the U(1) connection. The classification of invertible Spinc phases in dimension 4 is ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ factor is the integer Chern–Simons level and the ℤ₂ factor is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant. This result follows from the computation of the Spinc cobordism groups ΩSpinc₄ ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ via the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence.

Worked Example: Computing the Spinc Partition Function on ℂℙ²
Let M = ℂℙ² with its canonical Spinc structure. The determinant line bundle is 𝒪(3) with first Chern class c₁ = 3h, where h generates H²(ℂℙ²; ℤ). For an invertible Spinc TQFT at level k ∈ ℤ, the partition function is exp(2πi k ∫M (c₁²)/4) times the Arf invariant of the quadratic refinement. Compute the value for k = 1.
On ℂℙ², ∫M h² = 1. Hence ∫M c₁² = 9. The exponent is 2πi × 1 × 9/4 = 2πi × 9/4 = 2πi × (2 + 1/4) = 2πi × 1/4 = πi/2. The Arf invariant for the canonical Spinc structure on ℂℙ² is 0 because the intersection form is even after twisting by c₁. Therefore the partition function equals exp(πi/2) = i.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the partition function of an invertible Spinc theory on a simply‑connected 4‑manifold, first compute the Chern class square, reduce modulo 4, and then multiply by the level. Remember that the Arf invariant vanishes for manifolds with even intersection form after the twist; this is a standard shortcut that avoids the full η‑invariant computation.

Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Extended 3D TQFTs

In a fully extended 3‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord3 → C the value on a circle is a braided monoidal category B = Z(S¹). The mapping class group of the annulus is the braid group Bn on n strands, and its action on the n‑fold tensor product B⊗n is implemented by the braiding isomorphisms. At the extended level this action lifts to a monoidal functor from the braided monoidal category of braids (the free braided monoidal category on one object) to the monoidal category of endofunctors of B⊗n. Concretely, each generator σi acts by the braiding cXi,Xi+1 : Xi ⊗ Xi+1 → Xi+1 ⊗ Xi, and the Yang–Baxter equation ensures the braid relations. When B is the Drinfeld center of a spherical fusion category C, the braid group representation on the space of conformal blocks of a punctured sphere is the same as the monodromy representation of the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov connection. The higher categorical viewpoint replaces the vector space of conformal blocks by the category of modules over the tensor product of the corresponding vertex operator algebras, and the braid group acts by monoidal autoequivalences. This categorification explains why the Verlinde formula computes dimensions of invariant subspaces: the trace of the braid group action on the Hochschild homology of the tube algebra of C equals the dimension of the space of invariants. Recent work shows that the full (∞,2)‑category of boundary conditions for the Turaev–Viro theory carries an action of the mapping class groupoid of surfaces, and the braid group appears as the automorphism group of the n‑punctured disk.

Factorization Homology and the Computation of Extended TQFT Invariants on Manifolds with Boundary

Factorization homology ∫M A of an En‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)‑category provides a systematic method to compute the value of an n‑dimensional extended TQFT on a manifold M possibly with boundary. When A is the En‑algebra corresponding to a fully dualizable object in the target (∞,n)‑category, the cobordism hypothesis identifies the TQFT with the factorization homology functor. For a manifold with boundary ∂M, factorization homology satisfies a higher excision property: ∫M A is the colimit of a diagram built from a handle decomposition of M, and the boundary condition is encoded by a module En‑algebra over A. In practice, one chooses a good cover of M by disks and computes the factorization homology as a homotopy colimit of the values on disks, which are just A. The gluing of handles corresponds to taking relative tensor products of A‑modules. For 3‑dimensional TQFTs from a modular tensor category C, the E₂‑algebra is the Drinfeld center Z(C) viewed as an E₂‑algebra in Vect. Factorization homology on a 3‑manifold with boundary then reproduces the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant with the specified boundary condition given by a module category over Z(C). This approach also works for non‑semisimple theories where the state‑sum definition fails, because factorization homology is defined for any En‑algebra in a presentable symmetric monoidal category. The computation reduces to a higher categorical version of the surgery formula: the invariant of a closed manifold obtained by gluing two manifolds with boundary along a common surface is the relative tensor product of the corresponding module categories over the E₂‑algebra assigned to the surface.

The Classification of Fully Dualizable Objects in the (∞,n)‑Category of Higher Vector Spaces

The (∞,n)‑category nVect of higher vector spaces (also called n‑vector spaces) is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals on one generator. Its objects are (∞,n−1)‑categories that are finite semisimple in the sense of having finitely many simple objects and finite‑dimensional Hom‑spaces at each level. An object V in nVect is fully dualizable if and only if it is a finite semisimple (∞,n−1)‑category. The proof proceeds by induction on n: for n = 1, 1Vect = Vectfd and full dualizability is finite dimensionality. Assuming the statement for n−1, an object in nVect is a finite semisimple (∞,n−1)‑category C. Its dual is the opposite category Cop, and the evaluation and coevaluation maps are given by the relative tensor product over C and the unit object. The higher dualizability conditions (existence of adjoints for 1‑morphisms, 2‑morphisms, …) translate precisely into the requirement that every k‑morphism in C have a two‑sided adjoint, which holds in a finite semisimple higher category because every morphism space is a finite semisimple (∞,n−1−k)‑category. Consequently, the cobordism hypothesis implies that fully extended n‑dimensional TQFTs with values in nVect are classified by finite semisimple (∞,n−1)‑categories. For n = 2 this recovers the classification of 2D extended TQFTs by separable Frobenius algebras (or equivalently finite semisimple categories). For n = 3 the classification is by finite semisimple 2‑categories, i.e. fusion 2‑categories, which matches the Douglas–Reutter construction. For n = 4 the objects are finite semisimple 3‑categories, also known as fusion 3‑categories, and the corresponding TQFTs are the Crane–Yetter models. This pattern continues, giving a uniform description of all fully extended TQFTs valued in higher vector spaces.

  • Confusing the spherical condition for a fusion category with the modular condition for its Drinfeld center; the former ensures the Turaev–Viro state sum is triangulation independent, while the latter is required for the Reshetikhin–Turaev theory to be non‑degenerate.
  • Assuming that every Spinc structure on a 4‑manifold yields the same partition function; the determinant line bundle’s Chern class enters quadratically and changes the phase by a factor of exp(πi ∫ c₁²/2).
  • Treating the braid group action on the category of line operators as an ordinary linear representation; it is a monoidal functor from the braid category, and decategorification loses the higher coherence data that distinguish different anyon models.
  • Believing that factorization homology on a manifold with boundary computes the same invariant as the state sum without specifying a boundary condition; the boundary condition is a module En‑algebra and different choices give different relative invariants.
  • Overlooking that full dualizability in nVect requires finite semisimplicity at every categorical level; an infinite semisimple category (e.g. the category of all finite‑dimensional representations of a compact Lie group) is not fully dualizable.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the E₂‑algebra structure on the Drinfeld center and its braided monoidal structure; factorization homology uses the E₂‑structure, which encodes the full higher braiding data needed for gluing along surfaces.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Group Cohomology and Dijkgraaf–Witten Theory

Dijkgraaf–Witten theory provides a canonical family of finite‑group gauge theories whose action is a group cohomology class. In the extended framework, a Dijkgraaf–Witten theory in dimension n is classified by a degree‑n cocycle ω ∈ Hⁿ(BG; U(1)) for a finite group G. The cobordism hypothesis with tangential structure BG assigns to a point the n‑category of G‑graded vector spaces twisted by ω. For n=3 the resulting fully extended TQFT is the 3‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten model; its state sum on a triangulated 3‑manifold evaluates the product of ω on each tetrahedron, reproducing the original partition function. When G is replaced by a higher group (a 2‑group, 3‑group, …) the classifying space becomes a higher stack and the relevant cohomology is higher group cohomology. The extended theory then takes values in higher vector spaces: a 2‑group Dijkgraaf–Witten theory in dimension 4 is classified by a class in H⁴(B𝔾; U(1)) where 𝔾 is a 2‑group, and its value on a point is a fusion 2‑category of 𝔾‑graded 2‑vector spaces. The gluing formulas are governed by the higher categorical analogue of the group multiplication, and the partition function on a closed 4‑manifold is the higher trace of the identity 1‑morphism in that fusion 2‑category. This construction realises the classification of symmetry‑protected topological phases with finite higher‑group symmetry: each phase corresponds to a distinct cohomology class, and the cobordism hypothesis guarantees that the resulting extended TQFT is uniquely determined by the fully dualizable object assigned to the point.

Concretely, for a finite 2‑group presented by a crossed module (H → G, α, t), the 4‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten invariant can be computed by a state sum on a triangulation where each 4‑simplex contributes a 4‑cocycle evaluated on the assignment of group elements to edges and 2‑group elements to triangles. The pentagonator coherence of the 2‑group ensures triangulation independence. The same pattern extends to 3‑groups and beyond, giving a uniform description of all finite higher‑group Dijkgraaf–Witten theories as fully extended TQFTs valued in higher vector spaces. The classification theorem states that fully extended n‑dimensional TQFTs with finite higher‑group symmetry 𝔾 are in bijection with Hⁿ(B𝔾; U(1)). This result is a direct consequence of the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds equipped with a map to B𝔾 and the identification of fully dualizable objects in nVect with finite semisimple (∞, n−1)‑categories.

Higher Categorical Traces and the Computation of Partition Functions in Extended TQFTs

The partition function of a fully extended TQFT on a closed n‑manifold M is the value of the symmetric monoidal functor Z: Bordₙfr → 𝒞 on M. By the cobordism hypothesis, Z is determined by a fully dualizable object X ∈ 𝒞. The value on M can be expressed as the n‑fold categorical trace of the identity n‑morphism on X. In an (∞, n)‑category, the trace of a k‑morphism is defined recursively: the trace of a 1‑morphism f: X → X is the composite 1 → X ⊗ X* → X ⊗ X* → 1 using evaluation and coevaluation; the trace of a 2‑morphism is the trace of the induced 1‑morphism on the Hom‑category, and so on. For 𝒞 = nVect, the fully dualizable object X is a finite semisimple (∞, n−1)‑category. The 0‑trace (ordinary trace) of the identity on a finite‑dimensional vector space is its dimension. The 1‑trace of the identity on a finite semisimple category is its categorical dimension, i.e. the sum of squares of dimensions of simple objects. The 2‑trace of the identity on a fusion 2‑category is the sum over simple objects of the squares of their 1‑traces, which reproduces the global dimension of the associated modular tensor category. In general, the n‑trace of the identity on a finite semisimple (∞, n−1)‑category yields the partition function of the corresponding TQFT on the n‑sphere; for other manifolds the trace is taken after applying the appropriate handle‑body decomposition, which translates into iterated relative tensor products of module categories.

Factorization homology provides a systematic algorithm for computing these higher traces. Given an Eₙ‑algebra A in a presentable symmetric monoidal category, the factorization homology ∫_M A on a manifold M with boundary computes the value of the extended TQFT determined by A. When M is closed, ∫_M A is the n‑fold trace of the unit of A. For a modular tensor category 𝒞, the E₂‑algebra is the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) viewed as an E₂‑algebra in Vect. Factorization homology on a closed 3‑manifold then reproduces the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant, which equals the 2‑trace of the identity in the fusion 2‑category 2Vect_𝒞. The same principle applies to 4‑dimensional Crane–Yetter models: the E₃‑algebra is the braided monoidal 2‑category of module 2‑categories over a braided fusion 2‑category, and its 3‑trace on a closed 4‑manifold gives the Crane–Yetter invariant. Thus higher categorical traces unify the computation of partition functions across dimensions and provide a conceptual explanation for the appearance of global dimensions, categorical dimensions, and higher analogues in state‑sum formulas.

Worked computation: 3D Dijkgraaf–Witten partition function on a lens space
Let G = ℤ/2 and let ω ∈ H³(Bℤ/2; U(1)) ≅ ℤ/2 be the non‑trivial class. Compute the partition function of the corresponding fully extended 3D TQFT on the lens space L(2,1) using the higher categorical trace.
The fully dualizable object assigned to the point is the fusion category Vecℤ/2^ω of ℤ/2‑graded vector spaces with associator twisted by ω. Its simple objects are the trivial grade 1 and the non‑trivial grade g, both of dimension 1. The categorical dimension (1‑trace of the identity) is dim(Vecℤ/2^ω) = 1² + 1² = 2. The lens space L(2,1) is obtained by gluing two solid tori along their boundary torus with a Dehn twist. In the extended TQFT, the value on a solid torus is the object Vecℤ/2^ω regarded as a module over itself; the gluing corresponds to the relative tensor product Vecℤ/2^ω ⊠Vecℤ/2^ω Vecℤ/2^ω, which is again Vecℤ/2^ω. The partition function is the 2‑trace of the identity on this module category, which equals the categorical dimension of Vecℤ/2^ω, i.e. 2. Hence Z(L(2,1)) = 2.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for a partition function on a closed manifold, first identify the fully dualizable object assigned to the point (often a fusion category, fusion 2‑category, etc.). Then recall that the partition function is the appropriate higher trace of the identity on that object. For 3D theories the trace is the categorical dimension; for 4D theories it is the 2‑trace (sum of squares of 1‑traces). Use the handle‑body decomposition of the manifold to reduce the computation to iterated relative tensor products of module categories, and remember that the trace of a relative tensor product is the trace of the identity on the resulting category.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Representations of Quantum Groups at Roots of Unity

Quantum groups U_q(𝔤) at a root of unity q = eπ i / ℓ have a finite semisimple representation category Rep^ℓ(𝔤) which is a modular tensor category. The Reshetikhin–Turaev construction yields a 3‑dimensional TQFT from this data. In the extended setting, one considers higher representations: a 2‑representation of U_q(𝔤) is a module category over Rep^ℓ(𝔤), and the collection of all such module categories forms a fusion 2‑category. The Douglas–Reutter construction shows that a fully extended 3‑dimensional TQFT is classified by a fusion 2‑category; the fusion 2‑category of module categories over Rep^ℓ(𝔤) provides exactly such a theory. Its value on a point is the fusion 2‑category Mod(Rep^ℓ(𝔤)), whose simple objects are the indecomposable module categories (classified by Lagrangian algebras in Rep^ℓ(𝔤)). The 1‑morphisms are module functors, and the 2‑morphisms are natural transformations. The categorical dimension of this fusion 2‑category reproduces the global dimension of Rep^ℓ(𝔤), and the partition function on a closed 3‑manifold coincides with the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant.

For 4‑dimensional theories one needs a braided fusion 2‑category. The Drinfeld center of Mod(Rep^ℓ(𝔤)) is a braided fusion 2‑category, and the Crane–Yetter model built from it yields a fully extended 4‑dimensional TQFT. More generally, higher representations of quantum groups at roots of unity give rise to a tower of extended TQFTs in all dimensions: the k‑fold categorification of Rep^ℓ(𝔤) produces a fusion k‑category, which by the cobordism hypothesis determines a fully extended k+1‑dimensional TQFT. The simple objects at each level are classified by higher Lagrangian algebras, and the higher traces compute the corresponding state‑sum invariants. This hierarchy explains the appearance of quantum group invariants in topological quantum computing: the anyon models used for fault‑tolerant quantum computation are precisely the simple objects of Rep^ℓ(𝔤), and their braiding is encoded in the modular structure. The extended TQFT framework makes the higher categorical structure of these anyon models explicit, allowing one to describe defects, boundaries, and symmetry actions in a uniform language.

Extended TQFTs and the Holographic Principle: Bulk–Boundary Correspondence in Higher Categories

The holographic principle asserts that a gravitational theory in a bulk spacetime is equivalent to a non‑gravitational theory on its boundary. Extended TQFTs provide a mathematically rigorous realisation of this idea in a topological setting. Given a fully extended n‑dimensional TQFT Z: Bordₙfr → 𝒞, a boundary condition is a morphism of extended TQFTs from the trivial (n−1)‑dimensional theory to the restriction of Z to the boundary. In the language of the cobordism hypothesis, a boundary condition corresponds to a module object over the fully dualizable object X ∈ 𝒞 assigned to the point. For a 3‑dimensional Chern–Simons theory with gauge group G at level k, the bulk theory is the Reshetikhin–Turaev TQFT built from the modular tensor category Rep_k(G). The boundary theory is the 2‑dimensional Wess–Zumino–Witten conformal field theory, whose category of representations is precisely Rep_k(G). The bulk‑boundary correspondence is the statement that the factorization homology of the bulk E₂‑algebra Z(Rep_k(G)) on a 3‑manifold with boundary computes the space of conformal blocks of the WZW model on the boundary surface, with the boundary condition given by a module category over Z(Rep_k(G)).

In higher dimensions, the same pattern holds. A 4‑dimensional Crane–Yetter theory built from a braided fusion 2‑category ℬ has as boundary a 3‑dimensional TQFT whose value on a point is a module 2‑category over ℬ. The bulk‑boundary correspondence is implemented by factorization homology of the E₃‑algebra associated to ℬ on a 4‑manifold with boundary. Anomalies in the boundary theory are captured by invertible bulk phases: if the boundary theory is anomalous, it cannot exist as a standalone TQFT but only as the boundary of a bulk invertible TQFT. The classification of invertible phases via the cobordism hypothesis (e.g. Hⁿ(BG; U(1)) for finite group symmetries) then matches the anomaly classification of the boundary theory. This higher categorical formulation of holography makes precise the idea that bulk topological order is the “center” of the boundary theory, and that the Drinfeld center construction is the mathematical avatar of the bulk‑boundary correspondence.

  • Confusing the degree of the cohomology class that classifies a Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with the spacetime dimension; the class lives in degree n for an n‑dimensional theory.
  • Assuming that the categorical dimension of a fusion category equals the partition function on every closed 3‑manifold; it only gives the value on the 3‑sphere, while other manifolds require the full state‑sum or factorization homology.
  • Treating higher representations of a quantum group as ordinary representations of a larger group; they are module categories over the representation category, not representations of a group.
  • Neglecting that a boundary condition in an extended TQFT is a module object, not merely a choice of boundary values for fields; different module structures give distinct boundary theories.
  • Believing that the Drinfeld center of a fusion 2‑category is automatically a braided fusion 2‑category without checking the coherence of the braiding at the 2‑morphism level.
  • Overlooking that the anomaly of a boundary theory is classified by an invertible bulk phase, and that the bulk phase must be trivial for the boundary theory to exist independently.
  • Misidentifying the trace level: the partition function of a 4D TQFT is the 3‑trace, not the 2‑trace, of the identity on the assigned fusion 3‑category.

The Construction of Modular Tensor Categories from Group Data and Quantum Groups

Modular Tensor Categories (MTCs) serve as the fundamental algebraic input for constructing 3-dimensional Reshetikhin–Turaev Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs). Understanding their origin and structure is crucial for grasping the mechanisms behind many quantum invariants. Two primary sources for MTCs are finite group data and quantum groups at roots of unity.

One prominent construction involves the Drinfeld double of a finite group G, denoted D(G). Given a finite group G, its representation category Rep(G) is a finite tensor category. The Drinfeld double D(G) is a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra, and its representation category Rep(D(G)) is a braided tensor category. Furthermore, Rep(D(G)) possesses a non-degenerate braiding, making it a modular tensor category. The objects of Rep(D(G)) can be understood as pairs (C, ρ), where C is a conjugacy class of G and ρ is an irreducible representation of the centralizer of an element in C. The fusion rules and braiding operations in Rep(D(G)) are derived from the group structure and its representations. This construction directly yields the input for Dijkgraaf–Witten TQFTs, which are state-sum models based on finite group cohomology. The partition function of a Dijkgraaf–Witten theory on a 3-manifold is given by a sum over group elements, weighted by a 3-cocycle, and can be equivalently formulated using the MTC Rep(D(G)).

A second, and perhaps more general, source of MTCs comes from quantum groups at roots of unity. Let 𝔤 be a complex simple Lie algebra (e.g., sl₂). The associated quantum group Uq(𝔤) is a deformation of the universal enveloping algebra U(𝔤), where q is a complex parameter. When q is a root of unity (e.g., q = exp(2πi/k) for some integer k), the category of representations of Uq(𝔤) becomes highly degenerate. By taking a suitable subquotient category, often called the "restricted" or "physical" representation category, one obtains a finite ribbon category. For specific choices of k (the "level"), this category becomes a modular tensor category, denoted Repk(𝔤). These MTCs are the algebraic foundation for the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev (WRT) TQFTs, which are generalizations of Chern–Simons theory. The objects of Repk(𝔤) are typically labeled by integrable highest weight representations of 𝔤 at level k. The braiding and fusion rules are derived from the quantum group's R-matrix and Clebsch–Gordan coefficients. The S-matrix, a key component of an MTC that encodes the modular transformation properties, is explicitly computable for these categories and is crucial for the Verlinde formula, which relates the dimensions of the spaces of conformal blocks in 2D Conformal Field Theories to the fusion coefficients of the MTC.

The existence of a non-degenerate braiding is what distinguishes a modular tensor category from a mere braided tensor category. This non-degeneracy ensures that the S-matrix, which describes the action of the modular transformation τ ↦ -1/τ on the characters of the category, is invertible. This property is essential for the construction of consistent 3D TQFTs and for the definition of topological invariants of 3-manifolds and knots. The connection between these algebraic structures and TQFTs highlights how abstract categorical properties directly translate into concrete topological and quantum field theoretic phenomena.

Eₙ-Algebras and Factorization Homology: The Engine of Fully Extended TQFTs

Fully extended Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs) assign data not just to manifolds of a fixed dimension, but to manifolds with boundaries, corners, and even points. This hierarchical structure is elegantly captured by the concept of Eₙ-algebras and the powerful machinery of factorization homology. An Eₙ-algebra is an algebraic structure that encodes n-fold commutativity, meaning its multiplication operation is commutative "up to n homotopies." More formally, an Eₙ-algebra is an algebra over the Eₙ-operad, which is an operad whose spaces of operations are equivalent to the configuration spaces of n-disks in ℝⁿ. The higher the 'n', the more commutative the algebra is in a homotopical sense.

In the context of extended TQFTs, an n-dimensional fully extended TQFT is determined by its value on a point, which must be a fully dualizable object in the target (∞,n)-category. This fully dualizable object is endowed with the structure of an Eₙ-algebra. For example, in a 2-dimensional fully extended TQFT, the value on a point is an E₂-algebra, which is a commutative algebra whose multiplication is commutative up to a coherent homotopy. For a 3-dimensional fully extended TQFT, the value on a point is an E₃-algebra, and so on. This Eₙ-algebra structure is the "seed" from which the entire TQFT is generated.

The mechanism for this generation is factorization homology. Factorization homology is a homology theory for manifolds with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category 𝒞. Given an Eₙ-algebra A in 𝒞 and an n-manifold M, factorization homology computes an object in 𝒞, denoted ∫M A. This object is constructed by "integrating" the Eₙ-algebra A over the manifold M. Intuitively, it assigns to each small region of M a copy of A, and then glues these copies together in a way that respects the algebraic structure of A and the topology of M. The power of factorization homology lies in its ability to automatically encode the gluing laws of a TQFT: if M is decomposed into smaller pieces, the factorization homology of M can be computed from the factorization homologies of its pieces, reflecting the cut-and-paste property of TQFTs.

Specifically, for an n-dimensional fully extended TQFT Z, the value Z(M) for an n-manifold M is given by the factorization homology ∫M A, where A is the Eₙ-algebra assigned to a point. For example, in a 2D TQFT, an E₂-algebra A (a commutative algebra) is assigned to a point. The TQFT then assigns to a closed 2-manifold Σ (a surface) the Hochschild homology HH*(A), which is a specific instance of factorization homology ∫Σ A. For a 3D TQFT, an E₃-algebra A is assigned to a point, and the TQFT assigns to a closed 3-manifold M the factorization homology ∫M A, which computes an object in the target category (e.g., a vector space). This framework provides a rigorous and constructive approach to defining fully extended TQFTs from minimal algebraic data, making the Atiyah–Segal axioms concrete and computable.

Examiner's trap
A common misconception is that an Eₙ-algebra is simply an n-fold tensor product of an ordinary algebra, or that its commutativity is strict. Explain why this is incorrect and what "n-fold commutativity" truly implies in the context of Eₙ-algebras.
An Eₙ-algebra is not merely an n-fold tensor product of an ordinary algebra. While an E₁-algebra is an associative algebra, and an E₂-algebra is a commutative associative algebra, the commutativity for n ≥ 2 is not strict but rather "up to coherent homotopy." This means that while the product a × b is equivalent to b × a, there is a specified higher-order operation (a 2-morphism in a higher category sense) that witnesses this equivalence, and these equivalences must satisfy further coherence conditions. For n > 2, the Eₙ-algebra structure encodes operations that are commutative in higher dimensions. For instance, an E₃-algebra has operations that are commutative in a 3-dimensional sense, allowing for "braiding" of operations in higher dimensions that would be trivial in a strictly commutative setting. This homotopical commutativity is crucial for the algebra to be defined on configuration spaces of n-disks, which are topologically non-trivial for n > 1. The "n-fold" refers to the dimension of the operad that governs the algebraic structure, not a simple iteration of tensor products.
Exam Day Strategy: Connecting Concepts
When faced with questions about advanced TQFT concepts like Eₙ-algebras or factorization homology, always try to link them back to the core principles: the Cobordism Hypothesis and the Atiyah–Segal axioms. Remember that Eₙ-algebras provide the algebraic input for the "value on a point" in a fully extended TQFT, and factorization homology is the mechanism by which this point-data is extended to higher-dimensional manifolds. This conceptual framework helps in understanding the 'why' and 'how' of these complex constructions, rather than just memorizing definitions.

Categorical Dimensions, Higher Traces, and the Computation of TQFT Invariants

In the realm of higher categories and extended TQFTs, the concepts of dimension and trace extend far beyond their familiar linear algebraic counterparts, providing powerful tools for computing topological invariants. The notion of categorical dimension, specifically the Frobenius-Perron dimension, is fundamental for objects and categories within a monoidal category. For an object X in a fusion category 𝒞, its Frobenius-Perron dimension dim(X) is a positive real number derived from the eigenvalues of its fusion matrix. For the category 𝒞 itself, its categorical dimension dim(𝒞) is the sum of the squares of the dimensions of its simple objects, divided by the dimension of the trivial object. This dimension is a crucial invariant of the category and plays a direct role in the partition functions of TQFTs.

For a 3-dimensional TQFT built from a modular tensor category 𝒞, the partition function on the 3-sphere (S³) is precisely the categorical dimension of 𝒞. However, for other closed 3-manifolds, the partition function is a more complex invariant that depends on the full state-sum construction or factorization homology. The distinction is vital: the categorical dimension of the input category provides a specific value for the simplest closed manifold, but not for all manifolds. This is often a point of confusion, as the full TQFT provides a richer set of invariants.

The concept of higher traces is essential for fully extended TQFTs. In an (∞,n)-category, a k-trace (or k-categorical trace) is a generalization of the ordinary trace of an endomorphism. For an endomorphism f: X → X of an object X in a 1-category, the trace is a scalar. In a 2-category, for a 1-morphism f: X → X, its 1-trace is an object in the target category (e.g., a vector space). For a fully extended n-dimensional TQFT, the partition function on a closed n-manifold M is computed by taking the (n-1)-trace of the identity 1-morphism on the object assigned to the (n-1)-manifold obtained by cutting M. More generally, the partition function Z(M) of an n-dimensional TQFT on a closed n-manifold M is given by the (n-1)-trace of the identity on the (n-1)-category assigned by the TQFT to the empty (n-1)-manifold. This is a highly abstract concept, but it provides a uniform way to compute invariants across different dimensions.

For instance, in a 2D fully extended TQFT, the value assigned to a point is an E₂-algebra A. The TQFT assigns to a circle S¹ the center of A, and to a closed surface Σ, its partition function Z(Σ) is the 1-trace (or categorical trace) of the identity on the category of A-modules, which corresponds to the Hochschild homology of A. In a 3D fully extended TQFT, the value assigned to a point is an E₃-algebra. The TQFT assigns to a closed 3-manifold M its partition function Z(M) as the 2-trace of the identity on the 2-category assigned to the empty 2-manifold. These higher traces are intimately connected to the factorization homology computations, providing the numerical invariants that characterize the topological phases of matter or quantum gravity models.

Understanding the interplay between categorical dimensions and higher traces is crucial for correctly interpreting the output of extended TQFTs. The categorical dimension of the input algebraic structure (e.g., a modular tensor category) provides a specific invariant, but the full power of the TQFT lies in its ability to compute higher traces for arbitrary closed manifolds, yielding a complete set of topological invariants.

Topological Invariants of Knots and 3-Manifolds via Extended TQFTs

Extended Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs) provide a powerful and unified framework for constructing and understanding topological invariants of low-dimensional manifolds, particularly knots and 3-manifolds. The connection stems from the ability of TQFTs to assign algebraic data to manifolds of various dimensions and their boundaries, allowing for a systematic computation of invariants through cutting and pasting operations.

The most celebrated examples are the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev (WRT) invariants of 3-manifolds and framed links, which originate from 3-dimensional Chern–Simons theory. Given a modular tensor category 𝒞 (often derived from quantum groups at roots of unity, as discussed previously), a 3D TQFT can be constructed. This TQFT assigns a complex number (the partition function) to every closed 3-manifold and a vector space to every closed surface. Crucially, it also assigns an element of a vector space to a framed link embedded in a 3-manifold. A framed link is a collection of knots, each with a specified normal vector field, which can be thought of as a 1-manifold with a trivialized normal bundle. The TQFT interprets such a framed link as a 1-dimensional object within the 3-dimensional bulk, and its invariant is computed by evaluating the TQFT on the 3-manifold with the link embedded.

The computation of 3-manifold invariants often relies on the surgery presentation of 3-manifolds. Any closed, orientable 3-manifold can be obtained by performing Dehn surgery on a framed link in the 3-sphere (S³). This means cutting out a tubular neighborhood of each component of the link and gluing it back in a different way, specified by the framing. An extended TQFT provides a systematic way to compute the invariant of the resulting 3-manifold: the TQFT assigns to the solid torus (the tubular neighborhood) a specific object (a module) in the target category, and the gluing operation corresponds to taking a trace or contraction of these objects according to the surgery coefficients. The WRT invariant of a 3-manifold M is then obtained by summing over contributions from the simple objects of the underlying modular tensor category, weighted by the linking numbers and framings of the surgery link components. These invariants generalize classical knot polynomials like the Jones polynomial, which can be recovered as specific values of WRT invariants for knots in S³.

The Turaev–Viro (TV) invariants provide an alternative, state-sum approach to 3-manifold invariants, also derived from a spherical fusion category. While the WRT invariants are generally complex-valued and sensitive to orientation, the TV invariants are real-valued and typically defined for triangulated 3-manifolds. There is a deep relationship between WRT and TV theories: for a given modular tensor category 𝒞, the TV theory constructed from 𝒞 is equivalent to the WRT theory constructed from the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) of 𝒞. This equivalence highlights the consistency and interconnectedness of different TQFT constructions.

In the fully extended TQFT framework, the assignments become even richer. For example, a 4D TQFT can assign a 3D TQFT to a 3-manifold boundary. This allows for the study of invariants of 4-manifolds with boundaries, where the boundary itself carries a topological field theory. This hierarchical structure provides a powerful tool for classifying and understanding topological phases of matter and for exploring connections to quantum gravity, where topological invariants play a crucial role in characterizing spacetime structures.

The Universal Property of the Cobordism Category and its Implications

The Cobordism Hypothesis, a profound statement in higher category theory, asserts a universal property for the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms, Bordₙ. This universal property is not merely an abstract mathematical curiosity; it has deep implications for the classification and construction of fully extended Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs). The hypothesis states that the (∞,n)-category Bordₙ is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with a fully dualizable object. More precisely, any symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-functor from Bordₙ to another symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category 𝒞 is uniquely determined by the image of a point, provided that this image is a fully dualizable object in 𝒞.

Let's unpack this. Bordₙ is the (∞,n)-category whose objects are (n-1)-manifolds, 1-morphisms are n-cobordisms between (n-1)-manifolds, 2-morphisms are (n+1)-cobordisms between n-cobordisms, and so on, up to n-morphisms. The "point" in this context is the 0-dimensional manifold, which is the initial object in Bordₙ. The hypothesis states that to define a fully extended n-dimensional TQFT, which is an (∞,n)-functor Z: Bordₙ → 𝒞, one only needs to specify the object X = Z(pt) ∈ 𝒞. However, this object X must satisfy a crucial condition: it must be fully dualizable. An object X in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category 𝒞 is fully dualizable if it possesses dual objects at all levels from 0 to n-1. For instance, in a 1-category, a fully dualizable object is one that has a dual (e.g., a finite-dimensional vector space has a dual space). In a 2-category, a fully dualizable object X must have a dual X* such that the evaluation and coevaluation 1-morphisms have their own duals (adjoints) as 2-morphisms, and so on, up to n levels. This hierarchy of duals encodes the ability to "turn manifolds around" and to define consistent gluing operations for cobordisms of all codimensions.

The implications of this universal property are far-reaching. Firstly, it provides a powerful classification theorem for TQFTs. Instead of trying to define a functor on all possible cobordisms, one only needs to classify fully dualizable objects in the target category 𝒞. For example, if 𝒞 is the category of vector spaces (Vect), then a fully dualizable object is a finite-dimensional vector space. A 1-dimensional TQFT (assigning a vector space to a point) is then classified by a finite-dimensional vector space. For higher dimensions, 𝒞 itself must be a higher category, and the fully dualizable objects become more complex algebraic structures, such as Eₙ-algebras in the (∞,1)-category of chain complexes or module categories over a fusion category.

Secondly, the Cobordism Hypothesis provides a constructive blueprint for building TQFTs. Once the Eₙ-algebra (or more generally, the fully dualizable object) assigned to a point is chosen, the entire TQFT is automatically determined. The functor Z then assigns to any k-manifold (k < n) an object in 𝒞 that is the (n-k)-fold "center" of the Eₙ-algebra. For example, a 3D TQFT is determined by an E₃-algebra A. It assigns to a surface Σ the factorization homology ∫Σ A, and to a circle S¹ the 2-center of A, and to a point the E₃-algebra A itself. This demonstrates how the local algebraic data at a point propagates through the higher categorical structure to define the global topological invariants of manifolds.

This universal property is a cornerstone of modern topological quantum field theory, connecting deep results in algebraic topology and higher category theory to the classification of topological phases of matter and the foundations of quantum gravity.

Common Traps

  • Confusing Categorical Dimension with Partition Function: Assuming the categorical dimension of a fusion category (e.g., Repk(𝔤)) directly gives the partition function for *any* closed 3-manifold. It only gives the partition function on the 3-sphere (S³); other manifolds require the full state-sum or factorization homology computation.
  • Misinterpreting Eₙ-Algebra Commutativity: Believing that "n-fold commutativity" in an Eₙ-algebra implies strict commutativity. For n ≥ 2, this commutativity is homotopical, meaning it holds up to coherent higher-order operations (2-morphisms, etc.), not as a strict equality.
  • Overlooking the Role of Duality in the Cobordism Hypothesis: Neglecting the crucial requirement that the object assigned to a point by a fully extended TQFT must be *fully dualizable*. Without this, the universal property of the cobordism category does not hold, and a consistent TQFT cannot be constructed.
  • Simplifying Higher Traces: Treating higher categorical traces as simple scalar traces. A k-trace in an (∞,n)-category is an object in a lower-level category (an (n-k)-category), not necessarily a scalar, and its computation involves complex categorical operations.
  • Ignoring the Origin of Modular Tensor Categories: Assuming MTCs are arbitrary algebraic structures without understanding their specific constructions from group data (Drinfeld doubles) or quantum groups at roots of unity, which are essential for their physical relevance in TQFTs.
  • Confusing WRT and Turaev–Viro Invariants: While related, assuming WRT and Turaev–Viro invariants are identical in construction or properties. WRT invariants arise from Chern–Simons theory and quantum groups, while TV invariants are state-sum models, though they can be equivalent under specific conditions (e.g., TV from 𝒞 is WRT from Z(𝒞)).

Extended TQFTs and the Algebraic Theory of Anyon Condensation

Anyon condensation provides a physical mechanism by which a topological phase transitions to another phase through the condensation of a subset of anyons into the vacuum. In the language of extended topological quantum field theory, this process is encoded as a domain wall — a codimension‑one defect — separating two distinct fully extended TQFTs. Algebraically, a condensation is described by a Lagrangian algebra (also called a connected étale algebra) in a braided fusion category C that represents the anyon data of the parent phase. The work of Bais and Slingerland (2009) showed that the condensed phase is equivalent to the category of local modules over this algebra, which inherits a braided fusion structure. Kong and Wen (2014) generalized this to the setting of higher categories, proving that a condensation in a 3‑dimensional TQFT corresponds to a fully dualizable 1‑morphism in the symmetric monoidal (∞,3)-category of cobordisms with defects. The universal property of the cobordism hypothesis then guarantees that specifying the Lagrangian algebra at the point level determines the entire domain wall theory, including its action on line and surface operators.
