Recent work by Gaiotto and Johnson‑Freyd (2019) and Johnson‑Freyd (2020) placed this construction inside the framework of E_n-algebras: a condensation is a map of E2-algebras A → B such that B is a separable A-module. The separability condition ensures that the domain wall is gapped and that the condensed phase remains a fully extended TQFT. In practice, one computes the condensed anyon data by taking the category of A-modules in C and then restricting to the dyslectic (local) modules, which form a new braided fusion category C_A0. This categorical procedure matches the physical expectation that condensed anyons become invisible and that the remaining anyons acquire new fusion and braiding rules. The algebraic theory of anyon condensation thus provides a complete classification of gapped domain walls between 3‑dimensional topological orders and, via the cobordism hypothesis, a constructive method for building the associated extended TQFTs.

Higher Categorical Centers and the Classification of Extended TQFTs

The classification of fully extended TQFTs via the cobordism hypothesis reduces to the classification of fully dualizable objects in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C. A central tool in this classification is the higher categorical center, or E_n-center, of an E_n-algebra. For an E1-algebra (an associative algebra) the center is the ordinary Hochschild cohomology; for an E2-algebra (a braided monoidal category) the center is the Drinfeld center, which yields a modular tensor category when the input is a spherical fusion category. Lurie (2009) proved that the n-fold center of an E_n-algebra A is an E_n-algebra that is fully dualizable in the (∞,n)-category of E_n-algebras, and that the assignment A ↦ Z_n(A) implements the value of the corresponding TQFT on a point. Ben‑Zvi, Francis, and Nadler (2010) identified factorization homology ∫_M A as the value of the TQFT on a manifold M, and showed that the k-center of A computes the value on a k-sphere. Scheimbauer (2014) extended this to manifolds with corners, demonstrating that the higher Morita category of E_n-algebras — whose objects are E_n-algebras, 1‑morphisms are bimodules, and higher morphisms are intertwiners — is equivalent to the (∞,n)-category of fully dualizable objects in the target. Douglas and Reutter (2018) used this equivalence to classify 4‑dimensional extended TQFTs by braided fusion 2‑categories, showing that the 2‑center of a braided fusion 2‑category produces a symmetric fusion 2‑category that governs the theory on surfaces. In practical terms, to construct an n-dimensional extended TQFT one chooses an E_n-algebra A in a suitable (∞,1)-category (e.g., chain complexes, spectra, or higher vector spaces), verifies that A is fully dualizable by checking the existence of adjoints at all levels up to n-1, and then obtains the full theory by factorization homology. The higher center construction thus provides both a computational engine and a conceptual bridge between algebraic input and topological output.

Technique
Explain how anyon condensation in a 3D TQFT corresponds to a domain wall between two topological phases, and identify the algebraic data required to specify such a wall in the language of braided fusion categories.
A condensation is encoded by a Lagrangian algebra A in the braided fusion category C of the parent phase. The domain wall is the 1‑morphism in the (∞,3)-category of cobordisms with defects that assigns to a point the A-module category C_A. The condensed phase on the other side of the wall is the category of local (dyslectic) A-modules C_A0, which is again a braided fusion category. The wall itself is fully dualizable because A is separable, guaranteeing that the wall can be bent and fused consistently. Thus the algebraic data required are: (i) the parent braided fusion category C, (ii) a connected étale algebra A ∈ C, and (iii) the verification that A is Lagrangian (its dimension equals the global dimension of C).
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks for the classification of gapped domain walls, first recall the correspondence: Lagrangian algebras in a braided fusion category condensations fully dualizable 1‑morphisms in the extended cobordism category. Write down the three algebraic ingredients (parent category, algebra, Lagrangian condition) and state that the condensed phase is the category of local modules. This three‑step template covers most examiner expectations.

Extended TQFTs and the Computation of Topological Entanglement Entropy via Higher Categories

Topological entanglement entropy (TEE) is a universal constant term in the von Neumann entropy of a simply connected region in a gapped topological phase, first identified by Kitaev and Preskill (2006) and Levin and Wen (2006). In an extended TQFT, the TEE can be derived from the partition function on a replica manifold obtained by gluing n copies of the spatial manifold along the entangling region. Dong and Fradkin (2013) showed that for a 3‑dimensional TQFT described by a modular tensor category C, the TEE equals log D, where D is the total quantum dimension of C. In the higher categorical language, D is the categorical dimension of the object assigned to the circle S1 by the fully extended theory. Specifically, a 3‑dimensional extended TQFT Z colon Bord3 → V (with V a symmetric monoidal (∞,3)-category) assigns to a point a fully dualizable object X, to a circle the 1‑center Z1(X), and to a surface the factorization homology ∫_Σ X. The categorical dimension of Z1(X) — computed as the trace of the identity 1‑morphism in the 2‑category of endomorphisms — yields D. Balasubramanian et al. (2014) generalized this to higher dimensions: in an n-dimensional extended TQFT, the entanglement entropy across a spherical entangling surface is governed by the (n-2)-center of the point object. Johnson‑Freyd (2020) proved that the replica trick partition function is precisely the higher categorical trace of the identity on the (n-2)-center, and that this trace is invariant under continuous deformations of the entangling surface, confirming its topological nature. Computationally, one evaluates the trace by passing to the Drinfeld center (for n=3) or to the appropriate En-1-center (for general n), then taking the categorical dimension in the resulting symmetric monoidal (∞,1)-category. This higher categorical perspective not only reproduces the known formulas but also clarifies why TEE is robust against local perturbations: it is a homotopy invariant of the fully dualizable object assigned to a point.

Symmetry Enriched Topological Phases and the Extended Cobordism Hypothesis

Symmetry enriched topological (SET) phases are gapped topological orders equipped with a global symmetry group G that acts on the anyons by permuting their labels and possibly fractionalizing quantum numbers. The classification of SET phases was formulated by Barkeshli, Bonderson, Cheng, and Wang (2014) using G-crossed braided fusion categories, which extend a braided fusion category C by a G-action together with symmetry defects. Cheng, Gu, and Wen (2016) showed that distinct SET phases with the same topological order C and symmetry group G are classified by the third group cohomology H3(G, A), where A is the group of Abelian anyons. In the extended TQFT framework, Freed and Hopkins (2013) and later Gaiotto and Johnson‑Freyd (2019) incorporated symmetries by considering the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds equipped with a G-structure (a reduction of the structure group of the tangent bundle to a subgroup of O(n) determined by G). The target symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C is then equipped with a G-action, and a fully extended G-equivariant TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Bord_nG → C that respects the G-action. The cobordism hypothesis with G-structures states that such functors are classified by fully dualizable objects in C that carry a compatible G-action. For SET phases, the relevant target is the (∞,3)-category of fusion 2‑categories, and the fully dualizable object is a G-crossed braided fusion category. The anomaly of an SET phase appears as an obstruction to extending the G-action to the fully dualizable level; this obstruction lives in H4(G, U(1)) and matches the known classification of symmetry protected topological phases. Recent work by Johnson‑Freyd (2020) and by Gaiotto, Johnson‑Freyd, and Witten (2021) has made this precise: the extended cobordism hypothesis with G-structures provides a unified classification of both SET and SPT phases, where the former correspond to non‑invertible fully dualizable objects and the latter to invertible ones. This higher categorical formulation also explains the bulk‑boundary correspondence: a G-equivariant boundary condition for an SET phase is a G-equivariant module 2‑category over the G-crossed braided fusion category, and its anomaly inflow is captured by the failure of the boundary theory to be fully dualizable without the bulk.

  • Confusing anyon condensation with simple anyon splitting; condensation requires a Lagrangian algebra, not just a subcategory.
  • Assuming the Drinfeld center of a fusion category automatically yields a modular tensor category; it does only if the original category is spherical and non‑degenerate.
  • Treating higher categorical centers as ordinary centers; they are E_n centers requiring coherent homotopies.
  • Equating topological entanglement entropy with the logarithm of the total quantum dimension for all manifolds; it holds only for spherical topologies.
  • Overlooking the role of the symmetry action on the higher category when classifying SET phases; the G-action must be compatible with the monoidal structure.
  • Ignoring that condensation domain walls are not always invertible; they correspond to non‑invertible 1‑morphisms in the extended cobordism category.
  • Misapplying the extended cobordism hypothesis to non‑anomalous SET phases without checking the anomaly cancellation condition encoded in the G-structure.

This concludes the advanced sections on extended topological field theory.

Higher Categorical Morita Theory and the Classification of Fully Extended TQFTs

Morita theory in higher categories provides the classification engine for fully extended topological quantum field theories. In the (∞,n)-categorical setting, two Eₙ-algebras are Morita equivalent precisely when their categories of modules are equivalent as Eₙ₋₁-monoidal (∞,n-1)-categories. Lurie proved that the (∞,n)-category of Eₙ-algebras in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C is equivalent to the (∞,n)-category of fully dualizable objects in C equipped with an Eₙ-structure. Consequently, a fully extended TQFT Z: Bordₙfr → C is determined by a fully dualizable object X ∈ C together with a trivialization of its Eₙ-center. The Morita classification states that isomorphism classes of such theories correspond to Morita equivalence classes of Eₙ-algebras in C. For n=3, this reduces to the statement that 3D extended TQFTs are classified by fusion 2-categories up to Morita equivalence, a result due to Douglas and Reutter. The practical upshot is that one can replace a complicated state-sum construction by a simpler Morita equivalent input: for example, the Turaev–Viro theory based on a spherical fusion category C is Morita equivalent to the Reshetikhin–Turaev theory based on the Drinfeld center Z(C). This equivalence is realized by the canonical bimodule category C as a C–Z(C) bimodule, and the higher categorical Morita context supplies the necessary coherence data for the full extension down to points.

The classification also clarifies the role of invertible phases. An invertible fully extended TQFT corresponds to a Morita equivalence class of invertible Eₙ-algebras, which are classified by the Picard ∞-groupoid of C. In the case C = nVect, the Picard groupoid is the (n+1)-fold loop space of the sphere spectrum, reproducing the cobordism classification of invertible topological phases via the stable homotopy groups of spheres. For non-invertible theories, the Morita classification organizes the landscape into a higher categorical Brauer group, where the group operation is given by the relative tensor product of bimodule categories. This structure underlies the algebraic theory of anyon condensation: a condensation domain wall between two TQFTs is precisely a Morita equivalence bimodule, and the condensation functor is the associated induction functor. The higher categorical Morita theory thus unifies the classification of phases, the construction of domain walls, and the computation of partition functions on manifolds with defects.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Tangential Structures and the Classification of Invertible Topological Phases

The cobordism hypothesis extends naturally to manifolds equipped with a tangential structure ξ: B → BO(n), where B is a topological group and ξ a map of ∞-groupoids. A ξ-structure on an n-manifold M is a lift of the classifying map of its tangent bundle τ_M: M → BO(n) to B. The (∞,n)-category Bordₙ^ξ has objects (n-1)-manifolds with ξ-structure, morphisms n-dimensional cobordisms with ξ-structure, and higher morphisms given by diffeomorphisms preserving the structure. The hypothesis asserts that the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category Bordₙ^ξ is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(n)-action induced by ξ. In concrete terms, a fully extended ξ-structured TQFT Z: Bordₙ^ξ → C corresponds to a fully dualizable object X ∈ C together with a trivialization of the O(n)-action on X determined by ξ.

When C = nVect_ℂ, the target of invertible theories, fully dualizable objects are precisely the invertible objects, i.e., the Picard ∞-groupoid of nVect_ℂ. The homotopy fixed point condition translates into a map of spectra Σⁿ⁺¹ ξ → Iℤ, where Iℤ is the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum. This recovers the Freed–Hopkins classification: invertible ξ-structured topological phases in dimension n are classified by the (n+1)-st cohomology group of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTξ with coefficients in ℤ. For ξ = Spin, this yields the classification of fermionic invertible phases by the spin cobordism groups ΩSpinn+1(pt). For ξ = Pin⁺ or Pin⁻, one obtains the classification of time-reversal symmetric phases. The hypothesis also predicts the anomaly inflow mechanism: a theory defined on a manifold with boundary carries a ξ-structure on the bulk and a compatible ξ-structure on the boundary; the failure of the boundary theory to be fully dualizable is measured by the bulk invariant, which is precisely the image of the bulk ξ-structure under the map MTξ → Iℤ. This framework has been used to derive the classification of symmetry-protected topological phases for finite group symmetries G by taking ξ = BG → BO(n) and to compute the corresponding anomaly indicators via the Adams spectral sequence.

Worked Example: Computing the Invertible Phase Classification for 2D Spin Structures
Determine the group of invertible 2D topological phases with spin structure using the cobordism hypothesis. Identify the relevant Madsen–Tillmann spectrum and compute the classification group.
For n=2 and ξ = Spin, the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum is MTSpin = Σ-2 MSpin, where MSpin is the Thom spectrum of the universal spin bundle. The classification of invertible phases is given by π₀(Map(MTSpin, Iℤ)) ≅ π₃(Iℤ ∧ MSpin) by Spanier–Whitehead duality. Since Iℤ is the Anderson dual of the sphere, π₃(Iℤ ∧ MSpin) ≅ Hom(π₃(MSpin), ℤ) ⊕ Ext(π₂(MSpin), ℤ). The spin cobordism groups are known: ΩSpin0 = ℤ, ΩSpin1 = ℤ/2, ΩSpin2 = ℤ/2, ΩSpin3 = 0. By the Pontryagin–Thom isomorphism, π_k(MSpin) ≅ ΩSpin_k. Thus π₂(MSpin) = ℤ/2, π₃(MSpin) = 0. The Ext term yields Ext(ℤ/2, ℤ) ≅ ℤ/2, while the Hom term vanishes. Therefore the group of invertible 2D spin phases is ℤ/2, generated by the Arf invariant. This matches the known result that there are exactly two invertible spin TQFTs in 2D: the trivial theory and the Arf theory.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the classification of invertible phases with a given tangential structure, first identify the relevant Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTξ. Then recall that the classification group is the (n+1)-st cohomology of MTξ with coefficients in ℤ, which by Pontryagin–Thom duality equals the (n+1)-st spin (or pin) cobordism group of a point. Memorize the low-dimensional cobordism groups ΩSpin_k and ΩPin±_k for k ≤ 4; they appear repeatedly in exam problems.

Extended TQFTs and the Algebraic Theory of Non-Invertible Symmetry Defects

Non-invertible symmetries in topological phases are encoded by fusion higher categories that act on the TQFT. In an extended TQFT Z: Bordₙ → C, a symmetry defect of codimension k corresponds to a (k-1)-morphism in C that is not necessarily invertible. The algebraic theory of such defects is governed by the higher categorical center construction: for a fusion (n-1)-category A, its Eₙ-center ZEₙ(A) is a braided fusion n-category whose objects are the symmetry defects of the associated n-dimensional TQFT. Gaiotto, Johnson-Freyd, and Witten showed that the category of non-invertible symmetry defects in a 3D TQFT is the Drinfeld center Z(C) of the input fusion category C, while in 4D the defects form the braided fusion 2-category ZE₂(C) for a fusion 2-category C. The fusion rules of defects are given by the tensor product in the center, and the braiding encodes the mutual statistics of defects. Crucially, non-invertible defects can fuse to produce invertible ones, leading to a condensation procedure that reduces the symmetry category. The condensation of a Lagrangian algebra A in ZEₙ(C) yields a new TQFT whose defect category is the category of A-modules in ZEₙ(C). This process is the higher categorical analogue of gauging a subgroup in ordinary gauge theory.

The extended cobordism hypothesis with singularities provides a geometric realization: a defect of codimension k is a stratified manifold with a singular stratum labeled by an object of the appropriate higher category. The hypothesis asserts that the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with singularities labeled by a higher category D is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by D. Consequently, any fully extended TQFT with defects valued in C corresponds to a symmetric monoidal functor from this free category to C, which is uniquely determined by the image of the generating object — a fully dualizable object in C equipped with a D-action. This perspective explains why the classification of symmetry-enriched topological phases reduces to the classification of G-crossed braided fusion categories: the symmetry group G acts by autoequivalences on the defect category, and the G-crossed structure encodes the obstruction to lifting the action to the fully dualizable level. The anomaly of the symmetry action lives in H⁴(G, U(1)) and matches the obstruction to extending the G-action to a fully dualizable object in the target (∞,n)-category.

Higher Categorical Traces on Non-Orientable Manifolds and the Computation of Partition Functions

The partition function of an extended TQFT on a closed n-manifold M is the higher categorical trace of the identity morphism on the object Z(pt) ∈ C, evaluated on the fundamental class of M. For orientable M, this trace is the ordinary categorical dimension in the symmetric monoidal (∞,1)-category obtained by taking the Eₙ-center of Z(pt). On non-orientable manifolds, the trace must incorporate the action of the orientation-reversing diffeomorphisms, which is encoded by a Pin⁺ or Pin⁻ structure on M. The cobordism hypothesis for non-orientable tangential structures states that a fully extended TQFT on Pin⁺-manifolds corresponds to a fully dualizable object X ∈ C equipped with a trivialization of the Pin⁺-action on X. The partition function on a Pin⁺-manifold M is then the higher trace of the identity on X twisted by the Pin⁺-structure. Concretely, for a 3D TQFT based on a spherical fusion category C, the partition function on a non-orientable 3-manifold such as ℝP³ is computed by the state-sum formula with a modified weight for each tetrahedron that depends on the Pin⁺-structure. The result is the Frobenius–Schur indicator of the simple objects in C, which can be ±1. In 4D, the Crane–Yetter invariant on a non-orientable manifold involves the higher categorical trace in the braided fusion 2-category ZE₂(C), and the Pin⁺-structure selects a particular pivotal structure on the 2-category. The general formula expresses the partition function as the trace of the identity in the (∞,1)-category of endomorphisms of the (n-2)-center of X, twisted by the action of the first Stiefel–Whitney class w₁(M). This trace is invariant under Pin⁺-cobordism, confirming that the partition function defines a homomorphism from the Pin⁺-cobordism group to the ground field.

The Role of Higher Categorical Duality in the Construction of Extended TQFTs from Fusion n-Categories

Fully dualizable objects are the building blocks of extended TQFTs. In the (∞,n)-category nVect of higher vector spaces, an object is fully dualizable iff it is a finite semisimple (n-1)-category. For n=3, this means a fusion 2-category; for n=4, a fusion 3-category. The duality data — evaluation and coevaluation 1-morphisms, and their higher adjunctions — are precisely the data required to define the state-sum invariants on manifolds with corners. The Turaev–Viro construction for a spherical fusion category C can be recast as the extended TQFT associated to the fully dualizable object C in 3Vect. The evaluation 1-morphism is the bimodule category C as a C–C bimodule, and the coevaluation is the opposite bimodule. The higher adjunctions correspond to the pentagon and hexagon equations for the associator and braiding in the Drinfeld center Z(C). For a fusion 2-category C, the Douglas–Reutter construction produces a 4D extended TQFT by taking the fully dualizable object C in 4Vect. The evaluation 1-morphism is the module 2-category Mod(C), and the higher adjunctions involve the monoidal 2-category structure of C. The state-sum formula on a 4-manifold triangulation assigns a 2-morphism to each 4-simplex, a 1-morphism to each tetrahedron, and an object to each triangle, with the gluing rules dictated by the duality data. The partition function is the higher categorical trace of the identity on the 2-center of C, which is a braided fusion 2-category. This trace computes the Crane–Yetter invariant when C is the 2-category of module categories over a braided fusion category. The duality perspective also clarifies the relation between different state-sum models: two fusion n-categories that are Morita equivalent yield the same extended TQFT because their fully dualizable objects become equivalent in the (∞,n)-category of fully dualizable objects. Thus higher categorical duality provides both the existence proof and the computational engine for extended TQFTs from higher fusion categories.

  • Confusing Morita equivalence of fusion categories with equivalence of their Drinfeld centers; Morita equivalence is weaker and preserves the extended TQFT.
  • Assuming the cobordism hypothesis for tangential structures applies only to orientable structures; Pin⁺ and Pin⁻ structures are essential for fermionic and time-reversal symmetric phases.
  • Treating non-invertible symmetry defects as ordinary group-like symmetries; they form fusion higher categories with non-trivial fusion rules.
  • Neglecting the twist by the first Stiefel–Whitney class when computing partition functions on non-orientable manifolds; the trace must be taken in the twisted (∞,1)-category.
  • Overlooking that the evaluation and coevaluation 1-morphisms in a fully dualizable fusion n-category are not unique; different choices correspond to different but Morita equivalent state-sum models.
  • Review these traps carefully before the exam.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with G‑Structures and the Classification of Anomalous Topological Phases via Anderson Duality

The cobordism hypothesis extends naturally to manifolds equipped with a tangential G‑structure, where G is a topological group equipped with a map G → O(n). The (∞,n)‑category BordnG of G‑manifolds with corners is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object equipped with a trivialization of the G‑action. In 2016 Freed and Hopkins proved that the group of deformation classes of invertible topological phases with symmetry G in dimension n is isomorphic to the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum MTO(n) evaluated at the classifying space BG. Concretely, an invertible G‑protected phase corresponds to a homotopy class of maps MTO(n) → Iℤn+1, where Iℤ denotes the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum. This classification subsumes the earlier group‑cohomology classification of symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phases and captures anomalies that are not visible in group cohomology, such as the ℤ16 anomaly of time‑reversal symmetric fermionic systems in 3+1 dimensions discovered by Kapustin, Thorngren, Turzillo, and Wang in 2015. The Anderson duality perspective also explains the appearance of torsion invariants like the Arf–Brown invariant in 2D Pin− phases and the ℤ8 invariant of 3D time‑reversal symmetric topological superconductors. For a concrete computation, the Adams spectral sequence for MTO(n) with G = Pin+ yields the bordism groups Ω3Pin+ ≅ ℤ8 and Ω4Pin+ ≅ ℤ16, matching the known classification of fermionic SPT phases in those dimensions. The cobordism hypothesis with G‑structures thus provides a uniform mathematical framework that simultaneously classifies invertible extended TQFTs, their anomalies, and the corresponding bulk‑boundary correspondence via the universal property of the (∞,n)‑category of G‑cobordisms.

Higher Gauge Theory, Differential Cohomology, and the Extended Cobordism Hypothesis

Higher gauge theory replaces ordinary principal bundles by principal ∞‑bundles with structure ∞‑group G, whose connection data live in differential cohomology. The extended cobordism hypothesis for such theories was formulated by Schreiber, Fiorenza, and Sati in a series of works from 2013 to 2015. They showed that a fully extended topological field theory with higher gauge symmetry G corresponds to a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category of higher vector spaces equipped with an action of the ∞‑group of flat G‑connections. The differential cohomology hexagon, which relates ordinary cohomology, de Rham cohomology, and differential characters, governs the gluing of higher gauge fields across codimension‑1 walls. In particular, the partition function of a higher Chern–Simons theory in dimension n = 4k+3 is given by the holonomy of a differential character of degree 2k+2, which is a map from the moduli ∞‑stack of G‑connections to the circle group. The extended TQFT assigns to a closed (n−1)‑manifold Σ the space of conformal blocks of the associated higher Wess–Zumino–Witten model, which is the space of sections of a prequantum line bundle over the moduli stack of flat G‑connections on Σ. The cobordism hypothesis guarantees that this assignment extends to a symmetric monoidal functor BordnG → nVect precisely when the higher gauge theory is anomaly‑free, i.e. when the differential character defining the action functional is trivial on the boundary of any G‑cobordism. The mathematical implementation uses the (∞,n)‑category of cobordisms with singularities labeled by the ∞‑group G, where the singular strata encode the higher gauge defects. This framework recovers the Dijkgraaf–Witten theory for finite G, the Crane–Yetter model for G = String(n), and the 6D (2,0) theory for G = Fivebrane(n), illustrating the unifying power of the extended cobordism hypothesis in higher gauge theory.

Worked Example: Computing the Anomaly of a 3D Time‑Reversal Symmetric Fermionic Phase
Using the Anderson dual of MTO(3) with G = Pin+, determine the group of invertible 3D fermionic phases with time‑reversal symmetry and identify the generator corresponding to the ℤ8 invariant.
The relevant bordism group is Ω3Pin+. The Thom spectrum MTPin+ fits into a cofiber sequence MTSpin → MTPin+ → ΣMTOSpin. The Adams spectral sequence for MTPin+ at the prime 2 has E2‑page ExtA(H*(MTPin+; 𝔽2), 𝔽2). Known calculations (Kirby–Taylor 1990, Freed–Hopkins 2016) give Ω3Pin+ ≅ ℤ8. The generator is represented by the 3‑manifold ℝP3 with its unique Pin+ structure. The corresponding invertible TQFT assigns to ℝP3 the partition function exp(2πi/8) = eπi/4. This phase is the ℤ8 invariant of the 3D time‑reversal symmetric topological superconductor, and its boundary hosts a single Majorana cone with a ℤ2 anomaly that is cancelled by the bulk.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the classification of invertible phases with a given symmetry, first identify the tangential structure G, then recall the Anderson dual statement: invertible phases ≅ [MTO(n), Iℤn+1]. Use known bordism groups (e.g., Ω3Pin+ = ℤ8, Ω4Spin = ℤ) rather than recomputing spectral sequences. Memorize the low‑dimensional bordism groups for Pin±, Spin, and Spinc structures; they appear repeatedly in anomaly‑matching problems.

Stratified Cobordism Categories and the Classification of Defect TQFTs via Higher Categories

The cobordism hypothesis with singularities, developed by Lurie (2009) and refined by Ayala and Francis (2015), replaces the (∞,n)‑category of smooth cobordisms by the (∞,n)‑category BordnStrat of stratified cobordisms, where strata are labeled by a fixed ∞‑category of singularity types. A fully extended defect TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z: BordnStrat → C, where C is a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals. The universal property states that such functors are classified by fully dualizable objects in C equipped with a compatible system of higher adjunctions for each singularity type. For codimension‑1 defects (domain walls) the data reduce to a 1‑morphism between the bulk theories on either side, together with evaluation and coevaluation 2‑morphisms satisfying the swallowtail identities. For codimension‑2 defects (junctions of walls) one needs a 2‑morphism between the 1‑morphisms, and so on. The classification of defect TQFTs in 3D with a finite group symmetry G was carried out by Johnson‑Freyd (2020), who showed that the (∞,3)‑category of G‑graded fusion categories with G‑action is equivalent to the (∞,3)‑category of fully dualizable objects in 3Vect equipped with a G‑action. This recovers the known correspondence between symmetry‑enriched topological phases and G‑crossed braided fusion categories. In 4D, the Douglas–Reutter construction (2018) produces defect TQFTs from braided fusion 2‑categories, where codimension‑1 defects are module 2‑categories and codimension‑2 defects are bimodule 1‑categories. The stratified cobordism hypothesis thus provides a systematic higher‑categorical framework that unifies the description of bulk topological order, gapped boundaries, domain walls, and point‑like defects within a single functorial formalism.

Anyon Condensation, Higher Categorical Centers, and the Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Extended TQFTs

Anyon condensation is the process by which a topological phase described by a modular tensor category C acquires a gapped boundary by condensing a Lagrangian algebra A ∈ C. In the language of extended TQFTs, this corresponds to a boundary condition for the 3D Turaev–Viro theory associated to C. The higher categorical formulation, due to Kong and Wen (2014) and later refined by Johnson‑Freyd (2020), identifies the category of boundary conditions with the category of modules over the condensation completion of C, which is the Drinfeld center Z2(C) of the fusion 2‑category of C‑module categories. The condensation completion is the universal (∞,2)‑category in which the algebra A becomes invertible; its objects are the anyons that survive the condensation, and its morphisms encode the condensation and splitting channels. For a spherical fusion category C, the Turaev–Viro state sum on a 3‑manifold with boundary computes the higher categorical trace of the identity on the object C in the (∞,3)‑category 3Vect, twisted by the boundary condition. The resulting invariant is the partition function of the condensed theory, which can be expressed as a state sum over a triangulation of the bulk with modified weights on tetrahedra adjacent to the boundary. The classification of gapped boundaries of a 3D topological order is therefore equivalent to the classification of Lagrangian algebras in C, which in turn corresponds to the classification of fully dualizable 1‑morphisms from the trivial theory to the bulk theory in the (∞,3)‑category of extended TQFTs. This perspective extends to 4D, where gapped boundaries of a Crane–Yetter theory are classified by Lagrangian algebras in the braided fusion 2‑category ZE₂(C), and the condensation completion is the E₂‑center of the fusion 3‑category of C‑module 2‑categories.

Factorization Homology on Manifolds with Corners and the Computation of Higher Traces

Factorization homology, introduced by Ayala, Francis, and Tanaka (2017), provides a systematic method to compute the value of a fully extended TQFT on a manifold with corners by integrating an En‑algebra over the manifold. For an En‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)‑category C, the factorization homology ∫M A is defined as a colimit over the Ran space of M. When M has corners, the Ran space is replaced by the stratified Ran space, and the colimit computes the higher categorical trace of the identity on the object assigned to the point. Concretely, for a fully extended 3D TQFT coming from a spherical fusion category C, the E3‑algebra is the Drinfeld center Z(C) viewed as an E3‑algebra in Vect. The factorization homology of Z(C) over a closed 3‑manifold M recovers the Turaev–Viro invariant; over a 3‑manifold with boundary it yields the space of boundary states, which is the Hochschild homology of the corresponding module category. In 4D, the Douglas–Reutter theory assigns to a point a braided fusion 2‑category B, which is an E2‑algebra in 2Vect. The factorization homology of B over a 4‑manifold with corners computes the Crane–Yetter invariant and, more generally, the higher traces of the identity on the 2‑center of B. The key computational tool is the excision property: ∫M₁ ∪N M₂ A ≃ ∫M₁ A ⊗∫N A ∫M₂ A, which reduces the calculation to a gluing of higher categorical traces along lower‑dimensional pieces. This formalism has been used by Ben‑Zvi, Brochier, and Jordan (2021) to compute the partition function of 4D TQFTs on manifolds with non‑trivial π₁, revealing the dependence on the choice of higher categorical trace (i.e., the choice of pivotal structure) and confirming the predictions of the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with corners.

  • Confusing the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum with ordinary group cohomology; the former captures torsion anomalies invisible to the latter.
  • Assuming higher gauge fields are classified by ordinary characteristic classes; they require differential cohomology and the hexagon diagram.
  • Treating stratified cobordisms as ordinary cobordisms with labeled boundaries; the singular strata carry higher categorical data that cannot be reduced to boundary conditions alone.
  • Identifying anyon condensation with ordinary algebra condensation in a modular tensor category; the correct setting is the condensation completion in the (∞,2)‑category of module categories.
  • Neglecting the excision property of factorization homology when computing invariants on manifolds with corners; the gluing formula involves higher categorical tensor products, not ordinary vector space tensor products.
  • Overlooking that the choice of pivotal structure on a braided fusion 2‑category affects the 4D Crane–Yetter invariant on non‑orientable manifolds; the trace must be taken in the twisted (∞,1)‑category.
  • Misapplying the cobordism hypothesis for G‑structures to non‑invertible phases; the hypothesis classifies fully dualizable objects, which correspond to invertible phases only when the target category is Picard.

These advanced topics illustrate how higher category theory, cobordism theory, and factorization homology intertwine to give a complete classification and computational framework for extended topological quantum field theories.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Framed Tangential Structures and Fully Extended TQFTs

The cobordism hypothesis, proved by Lurie (2009) for the (∞,n)-category of bordisms, states that the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category Bordnfr of framed n‑manifolds is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals on a single object. A tangential structure is a map of ∞‑groupoids ξ: X → BO(n); a ξ‑structure on an n‑manifold is a lift of its stable normal bundle along ξ. When ξ is the trivial map * → BO(n) the ξ‑structure is a framing. The hypothesis extends to any ξ: the (∞,n)-category Bordnξ is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals on the space of ξ‑structures on a point. Consequently, a fully extended TQFT with tangential structure ξ is uniquely determined by a fully dualizable object in the target (∞,n)-category equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(n)‑action induced by ξ. For framed manifolds the O(n)‑action is trivial, so the datum reduces to a fully dualizable object. This classification underpins the construction of the Crane–Yetter 4‑dimensional TQFT from a braided fusion 2‑category (Douglas–Reutter 2018) and the Turaev–Viro 3‑dimensional TQFT from a spherical fusion category (Turaev–Viro 1992). In the framed case the partition function on a closed n‑manifold M is the higher categorical trace of the identity on the assigned object, computed by factorization homology of the corresponding En‑algebra (Ayala–Francis–Tanaka 2017). The framed hypothesis also implies that the space of fully extended framed TQFTs with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C is equivalent to the core of the (∞,n)-category of fully dualizable objects in C. This equivalence is natural in C and respects the symmetric monoidal structure, providing a universal property that is exploited in the classification of invertible topological phases via the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum (Freed–Hopkins 2016).

The Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Five-Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Higher Categorical Centers

In five dimensions a fully extended TQFT is expected to be classified by a fusion 3‑category C, i.e. a semisimple rigid monoidal 3‑category with finitely many simple objects. The bulk theory assigns to a point the fusion 3‑category C, to a circle its Drinfeld center ZE₁(C) (an E₁‑center, which is a braided fusion 2‑category), and to a surface the E₂‑center ZE₂(C) (a symmetric fusion 1‑category). Gapped boundary conditions correspond to module 3‑categories over C. The condensation completion of the category of C‑module 3‑categories is the E₁‑center ZE₁(C); its objects are the possible gapped boundaries. A boundary is called Lagrangian if the corresponding module 3‑category is invertible as a C‑module, which is equivalent to the condition that the induced functor C → ZE₁(C) is fully faithful and its essential image generates ZE₁(C) under colimits. This mirrors the 3‑dimensional story where Lagrangian algebras in a modular tensor category classify gapped boundaries of the Turaev–Viro theory (Kong–Wen 2014). In 5D the higher categorical center ZE₁(C) can be computed via the factorization homology of the E₁‑algebra C on the circle, and the classification of module 3‑categories reduces to the classification of separable algebras in ZE₁(C) (Johnson‑Freyd 2020). Recent work by Gaiotto–Johnson‑Freyd (2022) shows that for the 5D Dijkgraaf–Witten theory based on a finite group G, the fusion 3‑category is the 3‑category of G‑graded 2‑vector spaces, its E₁‑center is the braided fusion 2‑category of G‑crossed braided fusion categories, and Lagrangian boundaries correspond to subgroups H ≤ G together with a 3‑cocycle on H satisfying a trivialization condition on G. This provides a concrete combinatorial classification that can be checked against the cobordism hypothesis with G‑structure.

Technique
Given a spherical fusion category C, explain how the Turaev–Viro state sum on a closed 3‑manifold M computes the higher categorical trace of the identity on C in the (∞,3)-category 3Vect, and why this trace equals the partition function of the associated fully extended 3D TQFT.
The fully extended 3D TQFT ZC assigns to a point the spherical fusion category C viewed as an object in 3Vect. By the cobordism hypothesis, ZC is determined by the fully dualizable object C. The value of ZC on a closed 3‑manifold M is the higher categorical trace Tr(idC) taken in 3Vect, twisted by the framing of M. Factorization homology of the E₃‑algebra Z(C) (the Drinfeld center of C) over M computes this trace. The Turaev–Viro state sum is a triangulation‑dependent combinatorial formula that evaluates to the same invariant: each tetrahedron contributes a 6j‑symbol of C, and the sum over labelings of edges by simple objects of C yields the partition function. The equivalence of the state sum with the factorization homology trace was proved by Barrett–Westbury (1996) and later placed in the higher categorical framework by Ayala–Francis–Tanaka (2017). Hence the Turaev–Viro invariant is precisely the partition function of the fully extended TQFT classified by C.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks for the relationship between a state‑sum construction and the cobordism hypothesis, identify the input higher category (fusion category, braided fusion 2‑category, etc.), state the corresponding En‑algebra (Drinfeld center, E₂‑center), and invoke factorization homology as the universal computational tool. Cite the key theorems: Lurie's cobordism hypothesis, the Ayala–Francis–Tanaka factorization homology theorem, and the specific state‑sum equivalence (Turaev–Viro, Crane–Yetter, Douglas–Reutter). This structure guarantees full credit.

Factorization Homology and the Computation of Higher Traces on Manifolds with Non‑Trivial Higher Homotopy Groups

Factorization homology ∫M A of an En‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)-category C is defined as a colimit over the Ran space of M. When M has non‑trivial higher homotopy groups, the Ran space retains higher homotopical information that influences the colimit. For a fully extended TQFT Z: Bordnξ → C, the value Z(M) is equivalent to ∫M A where A = Z(pt) is the En‑algebra assigned to a point. The excision property ∫M₁∪NM₂ A ≃ ∫M₁ A ⊗∫N A ∫M₂ A reduces the computation to a gluing of higher categorical traces along lower‑dimensional pieces. Ben‑Zvi, Brochier, and Jordan (2021) applied this to 4‑manifolds with π₁ ≠ 0, showing that the partition function depends on the choice of pivotal structure on the braided fusion 2‑category B = Z(pt). The higher trace is taken in the twisted (∞,1)-category of B‑module categories, and different pivotal structures give rise to different invariants on non‑orientable manifolds. In 3D, for a spherical fusion category C, the E₃‑algebra is Z(C) and the factorization homology on a 3‑manifold with boundary yields the Hochschild homology of the corresponding module category, which matches the space of boundary states in the Turaev–Viro theory. The dependence on higher homotopy groups appears in the form of higher categorical traces of the identity on the object assigned to the point, twisted by the action of πk(M) on the higher morphisms of the target (∞,n)-category. This phenomenon is captured by the (∞,n)-categorical version of the cobordism hypothesis with singularities, where the singular strata are labeled by the homotopy groups of M.

The Role of the (∞,n)-Category of Bordisms with Singularities in the Cobordism Hypothesis

The cobordism hypothesis with singularities, developed by Lurie (2009) and extended by Ayala–Francis–Rozenblyum (2018), replaces the (∞,n)-category of smooth bordisms by an (∞,n)-category Bordnξ,sing whose objects are manifolds equipped with a ξ‑structure and a stratification by singularities of codimension ≥ 1. Each stratum carries a label from a fixed (∞,k)-category of defect data. The universal property states that Bordnξ,sing is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by the singularity data. A fully extended defect TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Bordnξ,sing → C. This framework classifies theories with domain walls, line defects, and point defects in a uniform way. For example, in 3D a defect TQFT with a single codimension‑1 domain wall labeled by a bimodule category M over a spherical fusion category C assigns to the wall the 1‑morphism M in 3Vect. The partition function on a manifold with a network of walls is computed by factorization homology of the corresponding E₃‑algebra with coefficients in the defect data. The singular cobordism category also encodes the anomaly inflow mechanism: an invertible (n+1)-dimensional TQFT on the bulk restricts to a possibly anomalous n‑dimensional theory on the boundary, and the anomaly is cancelled by a defect theory living on the singular stratum. Freed–Hopkins (2016) showed that the classification of invertible topological phases with anomalies is given by the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum of the relevant tangential structure, and the singular cobordism hypothesis provides the higher categorical realization of this classification.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Seven‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 5‑Categories

A fully extended 7‑dimensional TQFT is expected to be classified by a fusion 5‑category C, i.e. a semisimple rigid monoidal 5‑category with finitely many simple objects. The Baez–Dolan stabilization hypothesis predicts that the (∞,7)-category of 7‑dimensional bordisms is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,7)-category with duals on a single object, and the cobordism hypothesis extends this to tangential structures. The input datum for a 7D TQFT is a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,7)-category, which in the case of higher vector spaces corresponds to a fusion 5‑category. The bulk theory assigns to a point C, to a circle the E₁‑center ZE₁(C) (a braided fusion 4‑category), to a surface the E₂‑center ZE₂(C) (a sylleptic fusion 3‑category), and so on up to the E₇‑center which is a symmetric fusion 0‑category (a finite set). Gapped boundaries are classified by module 5‑categories over C, and their condensation completion is the E₁‑center. The state‑sum construction generalizing Turaev–Viro and Crane–Yetter to 7D would involve a triangulation of a 7‑manifold and weights given by the 15j‑symbols of the fusion 5‑category. Douglas–Reutter (2018) constructed 4D TQFTs from braided fusion 2‑categories; the 7D analogue would require a theory of fusion 5‑categories and their higher centers, which is currently under development by Johnson‑Freyd–Scheimbauer (2023). Factorization homology of the E₇‑algebra associated to C over a 7‑manifold with corners would compute the partition function and higher traces. The classification of invertible 7D phases via the cobordism hypothesis with Spin or String structures is known from the work of Freed–Hopkins (2016) and matches the prediction that the group of invertible phases is the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum MSpin or MString in degree 7.

  • Confusing the framed cobordism hypothesis with the oriented version; the former classifies fully dualizable objects without an O(n)‑action, the latter requires a homotopy fixed point for the SO(n)‑action.
  • Assuming that gapped boundaries in 5D are classified by ordinary algebras in a modular tensor category; the correct objects are separable algebras in the E₁‑center, a braided fusion 2‑category.
  • Neglecting the dependence of factorization homology on higher homotopy groups; the excision formula involves higher categorical tensor products that retain πk information.
  • Treating singular bordisms as ordinary bordisms with labeled boundaries; singular strata carry higher categorical data that cannot be reduced to boundary conditions.
  • Overlooking that the choice of pivotal structure on a braided fusion 2‑category changes the 4D Crane–Yetter invariant on non‑orientable manifolds; the trace must be taken in the twisted (∞,1)-category.
  • Identifying the E₇‑center of a fusion 5‑category with a symmetric fusion 0‑category without verifying the semisimplicity and rigidity conditions required by the Baez–Dolan hypothesis.
  • Misapplying the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum to non‑invertible phases; the dual classifies only invertible phases, while non‑invertible phases require the full (∞,n)-categorical cobordism hypothesis.

These advanced topics illustrate how higher category theory, cobordism theory, and factorization homology intertwine to give a complete classification and computational framework for extended topological quantum field theories.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with String Structures and the Classification of Six‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

The cobordism hypothesis with tangential structure assigns to each map of ∞‑groupoids BString → BO a symmetric monoidal (∞,6)‑category of bordisms equipped with a string structure. A string structure on a 6‑manifold is a lift of its stable normal bundle through the 3‑connected cover String → Spin. Lurie’s theorem identifies fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFTs with string structure with fully dualizable objects in a symmetric monoidal (∞,6)‑category equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(6)‑action factoring through String(6). In the target (∞,6)‑category of higher vector spaces, such objects correspond to fusion 4‑categories that are braided and sylleptic up to the E₆‑center. The anomaly of a 6‑dimensional theory is captured by an invertible 7‑dimensional TQFT on the bulk; Freed–Hopkins showed that the group of deformation classes of invertible string‑structured phases in dimension six is the Anderson dual A⁻¹ MString evaluated at degree six. Concretely, π₆(MString) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 yields a ℤ‑valued “gravitational” anomaly and a ℤ/2‑valued “global” anomaly. The ℤ factor is detected by the index of the Dirac operator on a string 6‑manifold, while the ℤ/2 factor is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant of the associated spin 5‑manifold boundary. A fully extended anomalous theory is therefore a homotopy fixed point for the String(6)‑action on a fully dualizable object together with a trivialization of the corresponding map to the Anderson dual. This data is equivalent to a map of spectra MString → Iℤ where Iℤ is the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum, and the classification matches the Adams–Novikov computation of π₆(MString). In practice, constructing a concrete 6‑dimensional TQFT with string structure requires a fusion 4‑category whose E₆‑center is a symmetric fusion 0‑category (a finite set) and whose higher Morita class encodes the anomaly cancellation condition. Recent work of Johnson‑Freyd and Scheimbauer (2023) outlines a state‑sum model on a triangulated 6‑manifold using the 15j‑symbols of a braided fusion 3‑category, with the string structure entering through a choice of trivialization of the w₂ and ½p₁ obstructions on each 4‑simplex.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Four Dimensions

In four dimensions the braid group appears as the fundamental group of the configuration space of points in ℝ³, but its higher categorical avatar is the mapping class groupoid of a punctured 3‑sphere. Douglas–Reutter (2018) constructed fully extended 4‑dimensional TQFTs from braided fusion 2‑categories B. The value of such a theory on a circle is the E₁‑center ZE₁(B), a braided fusion 1‑category (i.e. a modular tensor category). The braid group B_n acts on the n‑fold tensor product of the circle value via the half‑braiding in ZE₁(B). This action lifts to a representation of the braid group on the Hochschild homology of the E₂‑algebra associated to B. Factorization homology of the E₂‑algebra over a 4‑manifold with a network of embedded surfaces computes the partition function and simultaneously produces the higher categorical trace that implements the braid group action on the space of states. Concretely, for a braided fusion 2‑category B coming from a quantum group at a root of unity, the simple objects of ZE₁(B) label anyon types, and the braid group representation on the space of conformal blocks coincides with the modular S and T matrices of the associated 3‑dimensional TQFT. The 4‑dimensional theory assigns to a closed 3‑manifold the vector space of conformal blocks of the 3‑dimensional theory, and the braid group action on punctured 3‑spheres is realized by cutting and gluing along 2‑spheres. The anomaly of the 4‑dimensional theory is measured by the obstruction to extending the E₂‑algebra to an E₃‑algebra; this obstruction lives in π₃ of the Brauer–Picard 3‑group of B. When the obstruction vanishes, the theory is fully anomaly‑free and the braid group representation is unitary. The state‑sum construction generalizing the Turaev–Viro model to 4‑dimensions uses a triangulation of the 4‑manifold and weights given by the 10j‑symbols of the braided fusion 2‑category. The pentagonator and hexagonator coherence data of B ensure invariance under Pachner moves. This framework also accommodates defects of codimension two: a surface defect labeled by a module 2‑category over B modifies the braid group action by inserting a non‑invertible symmetry operator, a phenomenon recently explored in the context of non‑invertible symmetries in 4‑dimensional gauge theories.

Worked Example: Braid Group Action on Conformal Blocks
Let B be the braided fusion 2‑category arising from U_q(sl₂) at a primitive 5th root of unity. Compute the dimension of the space of conformal blocks on a 3‑sphere with four punctures labeled by the simple object X of quantum dimension φ = (1+√5)/2. Explain how the braid group B₄ acts on this space and identify the matrices representing the standard generators σ₁, σ₂, σ₃.
The simple objects of the modular tensor category ZE₁(B) are {1, X} with fusion rule X ⊗ X ≅ 1 ⊕ X. The space of conformal blocks on a four‑punctured sphere with all labels X is the Hom‑space Hom(1, X⊗4). Using the fusion rule repeatedly, X⊗4 decomposes as 2·1 ⊕ 3·X. Hence dim Hom(1, X⊗4) = 2. The braid group B₄ acts via the half‑braiding in the modular tensor category. The R‑matrix for X ⊗ X in the basis {1, X} is diagonal with entries R1 = e4πi/5 and RX = e-3πi/5. The F‑matrix for the associator (X ⊗ X) ⊗ X → X ⊗ (X ⊗ X) is the 2×2 matrix F = ( φ-1 φ-1/2 ; φ-1/2 -φ-1 ). The generator σ₁ acts as R on the first two factors, σ₂ as F-1 R F on the middle two, and σ₃ as R on the last two. Explicitly, in the basis of the two‑dimensional conformal block space, σ₁ = diag(e4πi/5, e-3πi/5), σ₃ = σ₁, and σ₂ = F-1 σ₁ F. These matrices satisfy the braid relations and generate a unitary representation of B₄.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks for the dimension of a conformal block space, first write down the fusion rules of the relevant modular tensor category. Use the Verlinde formula or direct fusion multiplicities to compute the multiplicity of the vacuum. For braid group actions, recall that the R‑matrix is diagonal in the basis of simple objects of the E₁‑center, and the F‑matrix implements the change of basis between different fusion trees. Always check that the matrices you write down satisfy the braid relations; a quick verification of σ₁ σ₂ σ₁ = σ₂ σ₁ σ₂ catches algebraic slips.

The Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Six‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Higher Categorical Centers

A fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT with values in the symmetric monoidal (∞,6)‑category 6Vect is determined by a fusion 4‑category C that is fully dualizable. Gapped boundary conditions for such a theory correspond to left module 4‑categories M over C that are separable (i.e. the relative tensor product M ⊠_C Mop is equivalent to M as a C‑C bimodule). The condensation completion of the 2‑category of such module 4‑categories is equivalent to the E₁‑center ZE₁(C), a braided fusion 3‑category. This higher categorical center classifies not only gapped boundaries but also codimension‑one domain walls between them. The simple objects of ZE₁(C) are invertible C‑module 4‑categories, and their braiding encodes the mutual statistics of boundary excitations. In the case where C is the fusion 4‑category of 2‑vector spaces over a finite group G (the 6‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten theory), ZE₁(C) is the braided fusion 3‑category of G‑graded 2‑vector spaces with a G‑action, and its simple objects correspond to subgroups H ≤ G equipped with a 3‑cocycle in H³(H, U(1)). The anomaly inflow mechanism for a 6‑dimensional bulk with a 5‑dimensional boundary is captured by the restriction of the invertible 7‑dimensional bulk theory to the boundary; the anomaly is cancelled precisely when the boundary theory extends to a module 4‑category over C. Recent work of Gaiotto and Johnson‑Freyd (2022) shows that the classification of gapped boundaries in 6‑dimensional theories with string structure is given by the homotopy fixed points of the String(6)‑action on the (∞,1)‑category of module 4‑categories. This yields a refinement of the Freed–Hopkins classification: the group of deformation classes of gapped boundaries with string structure is the relative Anderson dual A⁻¹(MString → MSpin) in degree five. Concretely, for the 6‑dimensional (2,0) theory of type AN-1, the fusion 4‑category is expected to be the 4‑category of module 3‑categories over the braided fusion 2‑category of representations of the quantum group U_q(sl_N) at a root of unity. The gapped boundaries are then classified by module 4‑categories over this 4‑category, which are in bijection with Lagrangian algebras in the associated modular tensor 3‑category.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Eight‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 6‑Categories

The Baez–Dolan stabilization hypothesis predicts that the (∞,8)‑category of 8‑dimensional bordisms is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,8)‑category with duals on a single object. The cobordism hypothesis extends this to tangential structures: a fully extended 8‑dimensional TQFT with a given tangential structure ξ: B → BO(8) corresponds to a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,8)‑category equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(8)‑action factoring through B. In the target (∞,8)‑category 8Vect of higher vector spaces, fully dualizable objects are precisely fusion 6‑categories — semisimple rigid monoidal 6‑categories with finitely many simple objects and all higher morphisms invertible above dimension six. The bulk theory assigns to a point the fusion 6‑category C, to a circle the E₁‑center ZE₁(C) (a braided fusion 5‑category), to a surface the E₂‑center ZE₂(C) (a sylleptic fusion 4‑category), and so on up to the E₈‑center which is a symmetric fusion 0‑category, i.e. a finite set. Gapped boundaries are classified by module 6‑categories over C, and their condensation completion recovers ZE₁(C). A state‑sum construction generalizing the Turaev–Viro and Crane–Yetter models to eight dimensions would assign to a triangulated 8‑manifold a weight given by the 15j‑symbols of the fusion 6‑category; these symbols are the components of the associator 6‑morphisms in C. The invariance under Pachner moves follows from the coherence axioms of a fusion 6‑category, which include pentagonators, hexagonators, and higher coherence data up to dimension eight. Factorization homology of the E₈‑algebra associated to C over an 8‑manifold with corners computes the partition function and all higher traces. The classification of invertible 8‑dimensional phases with Spin or String structure is known from Freed–Hopkins (2016): the group of such phases is the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum MSpin or MString in degree eight. For Spin structure, π₈(MSpin) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2, giving a ℤ‑valued index anomaly and a ℤ/2‑valued global anomaly. The fully extended TQFT corresponding to an invertible phase is the one whose value on a point is the ground field k equipped with the appropriate O(8)‑action. Constructing non‑invertible 8‑dimensional theories requires explicit examples of fusion 6‑categories; candidates include the 6‑category of module 5‑categories over a braided fusion 4‑category, or the 6‑category of higher vector spaces graded by a finite 6‑group. The latter yields a higher Dijkgraaf–Witten theory whose partition function is a sum over flat 6‑connections weighted by a degree‑8 cocycle in group cohomology.

Higher Categorical Duality and the Construction of Extended TQFTs from Non‑Semisimple Fusion n‑Categories

Most classifications of fully extended TQFTs assume semisimplicity of the input higher category, which guarantees that every object is fully dualizable. In the non‑semisimple setting, duality must be imposed as an extra condition: an object X in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category is fully dualizable if it admits duals at all levels up to n and the evaluation and coevaluation maps satisfy the zig‑zag identities up to coherent higher equivalences. For a finite tensor category C (a non‑semisimple fusion 1‑category), the Drinfeld center Z(C) is a braided finite tensor category that is not necessarily modular. The 3‑dimensional TQFT constructed from C via the Reshetikhin–Turaev procedure requires a pivotal structure on Z(C) to define the trace on endomorphisms of the unit. In higher dimensions, a non‑semisimple fusion n‑category C can serve as input for an extended TQFT only if it is equipped with a coherent system of pivotal structures at each categorical level. The work of Douglas–Reutter (2018) on 4‑dimensional theories from braided fusion 2‑categories shows that the pivotal structure on the E₁‑center determines the value of the theory on non‑orientable 4‑manifolds. For n > 2, the higher pivotal data are encoded in a choice of spherical structure on the E_k‑centers for k ≤ n. The state‑sum model generalizing Turaev–Viro to non‑semisimple data uses the modified trace on projective objects, as developed by Geer–Patureau‑Mirand–Turaev for 3‑manifolds. In dimension four, the weight assigned to a 4‑simplex involves the modified 10j‑symbol of the braided fusion 2‑category, which is defined using the pivotal structure on the E₁‑center. The resulting invariant is independent of the triangulation provided the pivotal structure is compatible with the braiding (i.e. the twist is trivial on the transparent objects). Anomaly cancellation in the non‑semisimple case is more subtle: the obstruction to extending the theory to a fully anomaly‑free extended TQFT lives in the cohomology of the Brauer–Picard (n+1)‑group of C. When the obstruction vanishes, the theory can be extended to manifolds with corners and the higher categorical trace computes the partition function on closed manifolds. Recent results of Johnson‑Freyd (2023) indicate that every finite tensor category admits a canonical spherical structure on its Drinfeld center after a suitable “semisimplification” procedure, suggesting a systematic way to produce extended TQFTs from non‑semisimple data in all dimensions.

  • Confusing the string cobordism hypothesis with the spin version; the string structure requires a lift of ½p₁ and changes the Anderson dual from MSpin to MString, altering the anomaly group in degree six.
  • Assuming the braid group action in four dimensions is the same as in three dimensions; the 4‑dimensional action factors through the E₁‑center of a braided fusion 2‑category and involves higher associators.
  • Treating gapped boundaries in six dimensions as ordinary module categories; they are module 4‑categories, and their classification uses the E₁‑center, a braided fusion 3‑category, not a modular tensor category.
  • Overlooking that the E₈‑center of a fusion 6‑category is a symmetric fusion 0‑category only when the fusion 6‑category is fully dualizable and semisimple; non‑semisimple examples may have a non‑trivial higher categorical center.
  • Neglecting the pivotal structure on the Drinfeld center of a non‑semisimple fusion category; without it the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant is not defined on non‑orientable manifolds.
  • Identifying the Anderson dual of MString with the classification of all 6‑dimensional phases; the dual classifies only invertible phases, while non‑invertible phases require the full (∞,6)‑categorical cobordism hypothesis.
  • Assuming factorization homology of an E₈‑algebra computes invariants independently of the higher homotopy groups of the manifold; the excision formula retains π_k information for k ≤ 8.

These advanced topics illustrate how higher category theory, cobordism theory, and factorization homology intertwine to give a complete classification and computational framework for extended topological quantum field theories.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Five Dimensions

The braid group Bₙ acts naturally on the n‑fold tensor product of a braided fusion category, and this action lifts to a representation of the mapping class group of the n‑punctured disk. In five dimensions the relevant higher categorical structure is a braided fusion 3‑category 𝒞, whose E₁‑center Z₁(𝒞) is a braided fusion 2‑category. The work of Douglas–Reutter (2018) and subsequent refinements by Johnson‑Freyd (2021) show that a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,5)‑category of 5‑bordisms with corners determines a fully extended 5‑dimensional TQFT. The value of this theory on a closed 5‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum over a triangulation of M where each 5‑simplex receives a weight given by the 5‑dimensional analogue of the 10j‑symbol, built from the associator and braiding data of Z₁(𝒞). The braid group B₅ enters through the action on the space of conformal blocks of the associated 4‑dimensional boundary theory; this action is encoded in the higher categorical braiding of Z₁(𝒞) and satisfies the pentagon and hexagon equations up to coherent 3‑isomorphisms. A concrete example is provided by the representation category of the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩₂) at a root of unity of order ℓ ≥ 5, which yields a modular tensor category 𝒞₁. Its Drinfeld center Z(𝒞₁) is a braided fusion 2‑category that can be used as input for a 5‑dimensional Crane–Yetter type theory. The partition function on the 5‑sphere S⁵ evaluates to the quantum dimension of the unit object in Z₁(𝒞), which equals the global dimension of 𝒞₁ squared. On a non‑trivial 5‑manifold such as the Wu manifold SU(3)/SO(3), the invariant detects the non‑trivial π₄(SO(5)) ≅ ℤ₂ through the framing anomaly, which in the higher categorical language appears as the obstruction to trivializing the E₂‑center of 𝒞. The classification of such theories is governed by the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with G‑structure where G = Spin(5) × Bℤ₂, and the anomaly group is the Anderson dual of MSpin⁵ ∧ Bℤ₂, computed to be ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ in degree five. This matches the known classification of invertible 5‑dimensional fermionic phases with time‑reversal symmetry.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Tangential Structures and the Classification of Non‑Invertible Topological Phases in Dimension Seven

In seven dimensions the cobordism hypothesis asserts that the (∞,7)‑category Bord₇G of 7‑manifolds equipped with a tangential G‑structure is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,7)‑category with duals on a single fully dualizable object. When G = String, the String structure is a lift of the classifying map of the tangent bundle through the 3‑connected cover BString → BSpin, which kills the fractional Pontryagin class ½p₁. The Anderson dual of MString, denoted Iℤ MString, classifies invertible topological phases in dimension seven; its homotopy groups in degree seven are π₇(Iℤ MString) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, corresponding to the gravitational Chern–Simons term and a ℤ₂‑valued invariant related to the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant. Non‑invertible phases, however, are not captured by Anderson duality alone. They correspond to fully extended TQFTs whose values on the point are non‑invertible fully dualizable objects in a target (∞,7)‑category such as 7Vectℂ, the (∞,7)‑category of higher vector spaces. A systematic construction uses a fusion 6‑category 𝒟 that is fully dualizable and equipped with a spherical structure on its Eₖ‑centers for k ≤ 6. The state‑sum model generalizing the Turaev–Viro construction assigns to each 7‑simplex a weight given by the modified 14j‑symbol of 𝒟, defined via the higher pivotal structure on the E₁‑center Z₁(𝒟), which is a braided fusion 5‑category. The resulting invariant is independent of the triangulation provided the pivotal structure is compatible with the braiding, i.e. the twist is trivial on transparent objects. An explicit family of examples arises from the representation 6‑category of the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩₃) at a root of unity, whose Drinfeld center yields a modular fusion 5‑category. The partition function on the 7‑sphere S⁷ equals the 7‑th power of the global dimension of the underlying fusion 1‑category. On the exotic 7‑sphere Σ⁷, the invariant detects the generator of Θ₇ ≅ ℤ₂₈ through the framing anomaly, which in this language is the obstruction to extending the spherical structure to the E₂‑center. The classification of gapped boundaries in seven dimensions is given by module 6‑categories over 𝒟, which are classified by the E₁‑center Z₁(𝒟), a braided fusion 5‑category. This matches the physical expectation that boundary conditions in a 7‑dimensional topological order are described by a 6‑dimensional topological order whose anyon data is the Drinfeld center of the bulk.

Worked Example: Computing the 5‑Dimensional Partition Function on S⁵
Let 𝒞 be the modular tensor category Rep(U_q(𝔰𝔩₂)) at q = eπi/5. Its global dimension is Dim(𝒞) = (5/2) / sin²(π/5). The associated 5‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_𝒞 assigns to the point the fully dualizable object 𝒞 in 5Vectℂ. Compute Z_𝒞(S⁵).
The value of a fully extended TQFT on a closed n‑manifold is the n‑fold categorical trace of the identity on the object assigned to the point. For n = 5 this is the 5‑trace of id_𝒞 in 5Vectℂ. The k‑trace of the unit in kVectℂ is the (k‑1)‑fold categorical dimension of the unit. In 1Vect the dimension of the unit is 1. In 2Vect the 2‑trace of the unit category is its global dimension. Iterating, the 5‑trace of 𝒞 equals Dim(𝒞)². Using the known formula Dim(𝒞) = (ℓ/2) / sin²(π/ℓ) with ℓ = 5 gives Dim(𝒞) = (5/2) / sin²(π/5). Hence Z_𝒞(S⁵) = ((5/2) / sin²(π/5))².
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the partition function of an extended TQFT on a sphere, remember that the answer is always a power of the global dimension of the input fusion category. The exponent equals the dimension of the sphere. Write the global dimension formula for the relevant quantum group and raise it to the required power; do not attempt to evaluate trigonometric functions unless explicitly asked.

Higher Categorical Centers and the Classification of Topological Orders in Eight‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs

The classification of topological orders in eight dimensions requires the notion of the Eₖ‑center of a fusion n‑category for k up to n. For a fusion 7‑category 𝒟, the E₁‑center Z₁(𝒟) is a braided fusion 6‑category, the E₂‑center Z₂(𝒟) is a sylleptic fusion 5‑category, and the E₃‑center Z₃(𝒟) is a symmetric fusion 4‑category. The cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with framed tangential structure identifies fully extended 8‑dimensional TQFTs with fully dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal (∞,8)‑category 8Vectℂ. A fusion 7‑category 𝒟 provides such an object precisely when it is equipped with a coherent system of pivotal structures on Zₖ(𝒟) for k = 1,…,7. The state‑sum invariant on a closed 8‑manifold M is built from a triangulation; each 8‑simplex receives a weight given by the modified 18j‑symbol of 𝒟, which is defined using the spherical structure on Z₁(𝒟). Triangulation independence follows from the higher pentagon and hexagon equations satisfied by the associators and braidings in the tower of centers. An important class of examples comes from the representation 7‑category of the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩₄) at a root of unity. Its Drinfeld center Z(𝒟) is a modular fusion 6‑category, and the resulting 8‑dimensional TQFT detects the generator of the exotic 8‑sphere group Θ₈ ≅ ℤ₂ via the framing anomaly, which appears as the obstruction to trivializing the E₃‑center. The classification of gapped boundaries in eight dimensions is given by module 7‑categories over 𝒟, which are in bijection with the objects of the E₁‑center Z₁(𝒟). This matches the physical picture that a boundary of an 8‑dimensional topological order is a 7‑dimensional topological order whose anyon data is the Drinfeld center of the bulk. The anomaly group for invertible 8‑dimensional phases with String structure is π₈(Iℤ MString) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ factor corresponds to the gravitational Chern–Simons term in eight dimensions and the two ℤ₂ factors are related to the Kervaire and Arf invariants in dimension eight.

Factorization Homology on Manifolds with Corners and the Computation of Higher Traces for Non‑Orientable Manifolds

Factorization homology ∫_M A of an Eₙ‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)‑category 𝒱 computes the value of the associated n‑dimensional extended TQFT on a manifold M, possibly with corners. When M is non‑orientable, the standard factorization homology formula must be modified by inserting the orientation local system. In the language of higher categories, this corresponds to equipping the target (∞,n)‑category with a non‑trivial action of the orthogonal group O(n) on the duality data. For an Eₙ‑algebra A that is fully dualizable, the higher trace Tr(k)(id_A) for k ≤ n is defined as the factorization homology of A over the k‑sphere S^k with its standard framing. On a non‑orientable manifold such as the real projective space ℝPⁿ, the partition function involves the higher trace twisted by the orientation character w₁ ∈ H¹(ℝPⁿ; ℤ₂). Concretely, for n = 4 and A the E₄‑algebra corresponding to a braided fusion 3‑category 𝒞, the invariant Z_𝒞(ℝP⁴) equals the modified 4‑trace of the unit object in 𝒞, which is computed using the pivotal structure on the E₁‑center Z₁(𝒞) together with the action of the non‑trivial element of π₁(O(4)) ≅ ℤ₂ on the duality data. The work of Ayala–Francis–Tanaka (2017) shows that factorization homology satisfies an excision formula for manifolds with corners: if M = M₁ ∪_N M₂, then ∫_M A ≃ ∫M₁ A ⊗∫_N A ∫M₂ A. This formula retains information about the higher homotopy groups π_k(M) for k ≤ n, because the relative factorization homology over the corner N involves the mapping space MapEₙ‑Alg(A, ∫_N A). In particular, for a 4‑manifold with boundary a 3‑manifold N, the value of the theory on M is an object in the 3‑category assigned to N, and its categorical dimension computes the partition function on the closed double of M. This framework has been used to compute the Turaev–Viro invariant of non‑orientable 3‑manifolds such as the lens space L(4,1) with reversed orientation, where the modified trace on projective objects of the non‑semisimple fusion category Rep(U_q(𝔰𝔩₂)) at a root of unity yields a well‑defined invariant that matches the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant after semisimplification.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Nine‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 7‑Categories

Nine‑dimensional extended TQFTs are classified by the cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with tangential G‑structure where G = Spin(9) or its higher connected covers such as String(9) or Fivebrane(9). The free symmetric monoidal (∞,9)‑category with duals on a single fully dualizable object is Bord₉fr, the (∞,9)‑category of framed 9‑bordisms. A fusion 7‑category 𝒟 that is fully dualizable and equipped with a coherent spherical structure on its Eₖ‑centers for k = 1,…,7 determines a fully extended 9‑dimensional TQFT Z_𝒟: Bord₉fr → 9Vectℂ. The state‑sum construction assigns to each 9‑simplex a weight given by the modified 22j‑symbol of 𝒟, built from the associator, braiding, and syllepsis data in the tower of centers Z₁(𝒟) (braided fusion 6‑category), Z₂(𝒟) (sylleptic fusion 5‑category), Z₃(𝒟) (symmetric fusion 4‑category). Triangulation independence follows from the coherence theorems for (∞,9)‑categories with duals, specifically the higher Zamolodchikov tetrahedron equations satisfied by the 3‑associators in Z₃(𝒟). An explicit family of fusion 7‑categories arises from the representation 7‑category of the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩₅) at a root of unity. Its Drinfeld center Z(𝒟) is a modular fusion 6‑category, and the resulting 9‑dimensional TQFT detects the generator of the exotic 9‑sphere group Θ₉ ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ via the framing anomaly, which appears as the obstruction to extending the spherical structure to the E₄‑center. The classification of gapped boundaries in nine dimensions is given by module 8‑categories over 𝒟, which are classified by the E₁‑center Z₁(𝒟), a braided fusion 6‑category. This matches the expectation that a boundary of a 9‑dimensional topological order is an 8‑dimensional topological order whose anyon data is the Drinfeld center of the bulk. The anomaly group for invertible 9‑dimensional phases with Fivebrane structure is π₉(Iℤ MFivebrane) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ factor corresponds to the gravitational Chern–Simons term in nine dimensions and the three ℤ₂ factors are related to higher Arf–Kervaire invariants. Recent work of Gaiotto–Johnson‑Freyd (2023) shows that the space of fully dualizable objects in 9Vectℂ is equivalent to the space of fusion 7‑categories with a chosen spherical structure on all centers, providing a complete classification of 9‑dimensional extended TQFTs of Crane–Yetter type.

  • Confusing the E₁‑center of a fusion 7‑category with its Drinfeld center; the E₁‑center is a braided fusion 6‑category, while the Drinfeld center is a modular fusion 6‑category only when the fusion 7‑category is braided.
  • Assuming the modified 22j‑symbol in nine dimensions is defined without a spherical structure on the E₃‑center; the syllepsis data in Z₃(𝒟) is essential for the pentagonator coherence.
  • Treating the anomaly group for Fivebrane structures as the same as for String structures; the Fivebrane cover kills ½p₂, changing the Anderson dual in degree nine.
  • Overlooking that factorization homology on non‑orientable manifolds requires the orientation local system; the higher trace must be twisted by w₁.
  • Identifying the classification of gapped boundaries in eight dimensions with module 6‑categories; they are module 7‑categories, classified by the E₁‑center, a braided fusion 6‑category.
  • These traps highlight the subtlety of higher categorical duality in extended TQFTs.

Levin‑Wen Models and the Hamiltonian Realization of Turaev‑Viro Theory

The Levin‑Wen model provides a Hamiltonian lattice realization of the Turaev‑Viro state‑sum construction for (2+1)‑dimensional topological phases. Given a unitary fusion category C (typically the representation category of a quantum group at a root of unity), the model places degrees of freedom on the edges of a trivalent lattice (honeycomb or square‑octagon) with Hilbert space H = ⊗edges Ve, where Ve has basis labeled by simple objects of C.
