The Hamiltonian is a sum of commuting projectors: H = −∑v Qv − ∑p Bp, where the vertex term Qv enforces the fusion rules at each vertex (the tensor product of incident edge labels must contain the trivial object), and the plaquette term Bp = ∑s∈Irr(C) (ds/D) Bps creates a closed string of type s around the plaquette, with ds the quantum dimension and D = √∑ di2 the total quantum dimension. The ground‑state subspace on a closed surface Σg of genus g has dimension equal to the Turaev‑Viro invariant TV(Σg × S1), which for a modular tensor category equals the number of anyon types raised to the genus. Excitations are created by violating Qv (electric charges, labeled by objects of C) or Bp (magnetic fluxes, labeled by objects of the Drinfeld center Z(C)). The braiding statistics of these anyons are exactly those of Z(C), and the S‑ and T‑matrices computed from the lattice model match the modular data of the Turaev‑Viro TQFT. The model is exactly solvable because all terms commute, and the ground‑state wavefunction can be written as a tensor network (a projected entangled‑pair state) whose tensors are the F‑symbols of C. This construction generalizes to higher dimensions: the (3+1)‑D Walker‑Wang model uses a braided fusion category, and the (4+1)‑D Crane‑Yetter model uses a modular tensor category, but only in (2+1)‑D does the Hamiltonian commuting‑projector construction yield a fully gapped phase for arbitrary unitary fusion categories.

Walker‑Wang Models and the Classification of (3+1)‑D Topological Orders via Braided Fusion Categories

The Walker‑Wang model extends the commuting‑projector Hamiltonian approach to (3+1) dimensions. The input data is a braided fusion category B (not necessarily modular). Degrees of freedom live on the edges of a trivalent spatial lattice (e.g., the diamond lattice), with Hilbert space labeled by simple objects of B. The Hamiltonian again consists of commuting vertex and plaquette terms: Qv enforces fusion rules at vertices, while Bp = ∑s∈Irr(B) (ds/D) Bps creates a loop of anyon s around a plaquette, using the braiding of B to define the action on the four edges of the plaquette. The crucial distinction from (2+1)‑D is that the bulk excitations are not fully gapped unless B is modular (i.e., the braiding is non‑degenerate). If B is modular, the Walker‑Wang model realizes a trivial bulk with a (2+1)‑D topological boundary whose anyon theory is B — this is the bulk‑boundary correspondence for (3+1)‑D SPT phases protected by the categorical symmetry of B. If B is not modular (e.g., Rep(G) for a finite group G with trivial braiding), the bulk hosts deconfined loop excitations (flux loops) whose braiding statistics are governed by the symmetric center Z2(B). The classification of (3+1)‑D topological orders with gapped boundaries is thus: bulk = Drinfeld center Z(B) (a modular tensor category), boundary = B. The Walker‑Wang Hamiltonian provides a microscopic realization of the Crane‑Yetter TQFT when B is modular, and of Dijkgraaf‑Witten theory when B = VecGω. Recent work by Lan‑Kong‑Wen (2018) shows that the condensation completion of B (the Karoubi envelope of the category of B‑modules in the symmetric monoidal category of vector spaces) classifies all gapped boundaries of the Walker‑Wang bulk, and that the resulting higher category of boundaries is equivalent to the (∞,3)‑category of module 2‑categories over B.

Worked Example: Ground‑State Degeneracy of the Toric Code on T3
The toric code is the Walker‑Wang model with input braided fusion category B = Vecℤ₂ (the category of ℤ₂‑graded vector spaces with trivial braiding). Compute the ground‑state degeneracy on the 3‑torus T3 and explain why the bulk is not a fully gapped topological order.
The toric code in (3+1)‑D has vertex terms Qv = ∏e∋v Xe and plaquette terms Bp = ∏e∈∂p Ze on a cubic lattice. On T3 with L3 vertices, there are 3L2 independent plaquette constraints and 3L2 independent vertex constraints, but the product of all vertex operators on each of the three families of parallel planes equals identity, giving 3 relations. The number of qubits is 3L3. The ground‑state degeneracy is 23L3 − 6L2 + 3? No — the correct count uses homology: the ground‑state subspace is isomorphic to H1(T3, ℤ₂) ⊕ H2(T3, ℤ₂) ≅ (ℤ₂)3 ⊕ (ℤ₂)3, so degeneracy = 26 = 64. The bulk excitations are point‑like electric charges (violations of Qv) and loop‑like magnetic fluxes (violations of Bp). The magnetic flux loops have trivial self‑braiding but non‑trivial mutual braiding with electric charges. Because B = Vecℤ₂ is symmetric (not modular), the bulk is not a fully gapped topological order; it is a (3+1)‑D ℤ₂ gauge theory with deconfined loops. The gapped boundary is the (2+1)‑D toric code with anyon theory Z(Vecℤ₂) ≅ Rep(D(ℤ₂)), the quantum double of ℤ₂.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Bulk vs Boundary in Walker‑Wang
When a question asks for the topological order of a Walker‑Wang model, first check if the input braided fusion category is modular. If yes (e.g., Fibonacci, SU(2)k), the bulk is trivial (invertible) and the boundary carries the topological order. If not modular (e.g., Rep(G), VecGω), the bulk is a gauge theory with loop excitations classified by the symmetric center, and the boundary is a (2+1)‑D topological order with anyons given by the Drinfeld center. Always compute the symmetric center Z2(B) = {X ∈ B | cX,YcY,X = id for all Y} to determine bulk loop braiding.

The Freed‑Hopkins Theorem and the Classification of Anomalies via Cobordism Groups

The Freed‑Hopkins theorem (2016) provides a complete classification of invertible topological phases (including anomalies) in terms of cobordism groups of manifolds with tangential structure. For a given symmetry type G (a topological group with a map G → O(n)), the group of deformation classes of reflection‑positive invertible n‑dimensional topological phases with symmetry G is isomorphic to the Anderson dual of the Madsen‑Tillmann spectrum: Invn(G) ≅ π−n(Iℤ MG). Here MG = MG(n) is the Thom spectrum of the virtual vector bundle over BG induced by the map G → O(n), and Iℤ denotes the Anderson dual with respect to the sphere spectrum. Concretely, this means there is a short exact sequence 0 → Extℤ1(Ωn−1G, ℤ) → Invn(G) → Homℤ(ΩnG, ℤ) → 0, where ΩkG = πk(MG) is the cobordism group of k‑manifolds with G‑structure. The Hom term classifies "free" anomalies (partition functions valued in U(1) that are homomorphisms on the cobordism group), while the Ext term classifies "torsion" anomalies (phases that are not determined by their values on closed manifolds but require manifolds with boundary). For example, for G = Spin (oriented fermionic phases), Ω3Spin = 0, Ω4Spin = ℤ (generated by the K3 surface), so Inv4(Spin) = ℤ, corresponding to the gravitational Chern‑Simons term (the signature/16). For G = Pin+ (time‑reversal symmetric fermions with T2 = (−1)F), Ω2Pin+ = ℤ₂, Ω3Pin+ = 0, giving Inv3(Pin+) = ℤ₂, the Arf invariant anomaly. The theorem also incorporates crystalline symmetries by taking G to be a space group extension of O(n), and it matches the classification of SPT phases via group cohomology Hn+1(G, U(1)) for finite groups G (where ΩnG ≅ Hn(G, ℤ) for n ≤ 3). The proof uses the cobordism hypothesis for invertible theories: the space of fully dualizable objects in the (∞,n)‑category of n‑vector spaces is the Picard ∞‑groupoid, and the mapping space from BordnG to nVect is the Anderson dual of the Thom spectrum.

Tensor Network Representations of Extended TQFTs and the Multi‑Scale Entanglement Renormalization Ansatz

Tensor networks provide a concrete computational framework for extended TQFTs, realizing the higher categorical structure as a network of tensors with indices labeled by objects and morphisms in a fusion n‑category. The Multi‑Scale Entanglement Renormalization Ansatz (MERA) is a tensor network with a hierarchical structure that implements a real‑space renormalization group flow. For a (1+1)‑D critical system described by a CFT, the MERA consists of disentanglers (unitaries) and isometries arranged in layers, each layer coarse‑graining the lattice by a factor of 2. The tensors in a MERA for a topological phase are not arbitrary: they are the F‑symbols and R‑symbols of a fusion category (for (2+1)‑D) or a braided fusion category (for (3+1)‑D). In the language of extended TQFTs, a MERA tensor network on a spatial manifold M computes the factorization homology ∫M A of an En‑algebra A (the fusion category) by decomposing M into n‑simplices and assigning tensors to each simplex. The causal cone structure of MERA corresponds to the factorization homology excision property: the value on a region depends only on the algebra A and the topology of the region. For (2+1)‑D Turaev‑Viro theory, the MERA tensors are the 6j‑symbols of the spherical fusion category, and the network exactly reproduces the state‑sum on a triangulation. For (3+1)‑D Walker‑Wang models, the MERA uses the 10j‑symbols of a braided fusion category. The key advantage of MERA over PEPS (Projected Entangled Pair States) is that MERA captures the logarithmic scaling of entanglement entropy in critical systems, while PEPS gives area law. For gapped topological phases, both give area law, but MERA's hierarchical structure makes the anyon condensation and renormalization group flow manifest: each layer of MERA implements a condensation of anyons described by a Lagrangian algebra in the fusion category. The work of Evenbly‑Vidal (2015) and Williamson‑Cheng‑Brennen (2017) shows that the MERA for a topological phase can be constructed from the data of a unitary fusion category, and that the fixed‑point MERA (where all layers are identical) corresponds to the exactly solvable Levin‑Wen Hamiltonian. The entanglement spectrum of a MERA block is given by the quantum dimensions of the anyons, and the modular S‑matrix can be extracted from the overlap of MERA states with different anyon fluxes inserted.

Condensation Completion and the Higher Categorical Classification of Gapped Phases

The condensation completion (also called the Karoubi envelope or idempotent completion) of a fusion n‑category C is the universal higher category in which all condensation processes (i.e., all separable Frobenius algebras) have been realized as objects. For a fusion 1‑category C, the condensation completion is the 2‑category of C‑modules, Mod(C), whose objects are module categories over C, 1‑morphisms are module functors, and 2‑morphisms are natural transformations. This 2‑category is equivalent to the 2‑category of separable Frobenius algebras in C (the anyon condensation data). For a fusion 2‑category C, the condensation completion is a 3‑category whose objects are module 2‑categories, and so on. The fundamental theorem (Gaiotto‑Johnson‑Freyd, 2019; Douglas‑Reutter, 2018) states that the condensation completion of a fusion n‑category C is equivalent to the (∞,n+1)‑category of boundary conditions for the (n+1)‑dimensional topological order described by C. In particular, the (∞,n+1)‑category of gapped boundaries of the (n+1)‑D bulk with anyon data C is the condensation completion of C. This provides a purely algebraic classification of gapped phases: an (n+1)‑D topological order is a fusion n‑category C; its gapped boundaries are module n‑categories over C; domain walls between boundaries are module (n−1)‑categories, etc. The condensation completion also classifies symmetry defects: a symmetry of the topological order is an autoequivalence of C, and the category of symmetry defects is the condensation completion of the crossed product C ⋊ G. For non‑invertible symmetries (fusion categories acting on C), the defect category is the relative Deligne product C ⊠C C. The condensation completion is computable: for C = VecGω (Dijkgraaf‑Witten theory), Mod(C) is the 2‑category of G‑graded vector spaces with associator ω, whose objects are classified by subgroups H ⊂ G and 2‑cocycles on H (the condensation of the H‑symmetry). For C = Rep(Uq(𝔰𝔩2)) at a root of unity, the condensation completion recovers the classification of gapped boundaries of the SU(2)k Chern‑Simons theory, which are labeled by ADE Dynkin diagrams (the Cappelli‑Itzykson‑Zuber classification). The higher condensation completion (iterating the construction) yields the full (∞,n)‑category of defects of all codimensions, which is the target of the fully extended TQFT by the cobordism hypothesis.

The Role of Higher Categorical Traces in the Computation of Partition Functions on Non‑Orientable Manifolds

Computing the partition function of an extended TQFT on a non‑orientable manifold requires a refinement of the categorical trace that incorporates the orientation local system. For a fully extended n‑dimensional TQFT Z: BordnG → nVect, where G is a tangential structure (e.g., Pin+, Pin−, or no structure for unoriented manifolds), the value on a closed n‑manifold M is the higher categorical trace of the identity morphism on the object Z(∅) in the (∞,n)‑category nVect. When M is non‑orientable, the trace must be twisted by the first Stiefel‑Whitney class w1(M) ∈ H1(M, ℤ₂). Concretely, for a 2‑dimensional TQFT corresponding to a Calabi‑Yau category C, the partition function on the Klein bottle K2 is the trace of the Nakayama automorphism (the "twisted trace") rather than the ordinary trace. For a 3‑dimensional Turaev‑Viro theory based on a spherical fusion category C, the partition function on a non‑orientable 3‑manifold (e.g., ℝP3 or the mapping torus of an orientation‑reversing diffeomorphism) is computed by the modified trace on projective objects in the non‑semisimple category C (if C is not semisimple) or by the state‑sum with a sign factor (−1)w1⋅w2 for each tetrahedron, where w2 is the second Stiefel‑Whitney class of the triangulation. The general formula for the higher trace on a non‑orientable manifold is given by factorization homology with coefficients in the orientation‑twisted En‑algebra: ∫M Aw1, where Aw1 is the En‑algebra in the category of local systems on M twisted by w1. This twisted factorization homology satisfies an excision formula that involves the relative trace on the corner strata. For example, the partition function of the (3+1)‑D ℤ₂ gauge theory (Dijkgraaf‑Witten with ω = 0) on ℝP4 is 1/|G| ∑[P]∈H1(ℝP4,ℤ₂) (−1)∫ w1∪w2(P) = 1/2 (1 + (−1)∫ w1∪w2) = 0, because w1∪w2 is the generator of H3(ℝP4, ℤ₂) ≅ ℤ₂. The higher trace also appears in the computation of topological entanglement entropy on non‑orientable surfaces: the Rényi entropy Sn = (1/(1−n)) log Tr(ρAn) involves the n‑th higher trace of the density matrix, which for a topological phase is the quantum dimension of the anyon type raised to the Euler characteristic of the replica manifold.

  • Confusing the Levin‑Wen Hamiltonian (which requires a unitary fusion category) with the Turaev‑Viro state sum (which works for any spherical fusion category, including non‑unitary ones); the Hamiltonian is only gapped and local for unitary categories.
  • Assuming the Walker‑Wang model always has a gapped bulk; the bulk is gapped only when the input braided fusion category is modular, otherwise it hosts deconfined loop excitations.
  • Misidentifying the Freed‑Hopkins classification group: it is the Anderson dual of the Madsen‑Tillmann spectrum, not the cobordism group itself; the Ext term captures torsion anomalies invisible to homomorphisms on ΩnG.
  • Treating MERA as a generic tensor network without recognizing that its tensors are constrained to be the F‑ and R‑symbols of a fusion category for topological phases; arbitrary MERA tensors do not yield a topological fixed point.
  • Overlooking that the condensation completion of a fusion n‑category is an (n+1)‑category, not an n‑category; the objects are module n‑categories, which are one categorical level higher.
  • Forgetting that the higher trace on non‑orientable manifolds requires twisting by the orientation local system w1; the ordinary trace computes the partition function on the orientation double cover, not on the manifold itself.
  • Identifying the symmetric center Z2(B) of a braided fusion category with its Drinfeld center Z(B); the symmetric center is the subcategory of objects that braid trivially with everything, while the Drinfeld center is the category of pairs (X, half‑braiding) and is always modular.

These advanced topics complete the theoretical toolkit needed to analyze extended TQFTs from Hamiltonian, categorical, and computational perspectives.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Fivebrane Structures and the Classification of Seven‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

A fivebrane structure on a manifold is a lift of the classifying map of the tangent bundle through the homotopy fiber of the map BString → BFivebrane that kills the fractional Pontryagin class p₂/6. In the language of tangential structures, this is a map G → BO where G is the 7‑connected cover of BO often denoted BFivebrane. The cobordism hypothesis for manifolds equipped with a G‑structure states that the symmetric monoidal (∞,7)‑category of fully extended 7‑dimensional TQFTs with G‑structure is equivalent to the (∞,7)‑category of fully dualizable objects in the target (∞,7)‑category. For invertible theories the classification reduces to the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG. The group of deformation classes of invertible 7‑dimensional phases with fivebrane structure is therefore π₀(Iℤ MTFivebrane). Computations using the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence show that this group contains a ℤ summand generated by the 7‑dimensional Chern–Simons term ∫ C₃ ∧ G₄ ∧ G₄ (the M‑theory anomaly polynomial) and a torsion summand ℤ₂₄ detected by the ν‑invariant of the fivebrane worldvolume theory. The ℤ₂₄ torsion matches the known classification of anomalies of the six‑dimensional (2,0) superconformal theory on a fivebrane. When the fivebrane structure is further refined to a Fivebrane⁺ structure (including a choice of orientation on the determinant line of the Dirac operator), the Anderson dual acquires an extra ℤ₂ factor corresponding to the Arf invariant of the self‑dual three‑form field. These results are the seven‑dimensional analogues of the Freed–Hopkins classification for lower dimensions and provide the mathematical underpinning for the Green–Schwarz anomaly cancellation mechanism in M‑theory on manifolds with boundary.

Key Computational Steps

  1. Identify the tangential structure G = BFivebrane and compute its Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTFivebrane as the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −γ over BFivebrane.
  2. Run the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MTFivebrane using the known BP‑homology of BString and the fiber sequence BFivebrane → BString → K(ℤ,8).
  3. Extract the Anderson dual Iℤ MTFivebrane and read off π₀ and π₋₁ which classify invertible phases and their anomalies respectively.
  4. Match the resulting groups to physical anomaly polynomials: the ℤ generator corresponds to the M‑theory Chern–Simons term, the ℤ₂₄ to the fivebrane worldvolume anomaly, and the ℤ₂ (if present) to the Arf invariant.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Six Dimensions

The braid group Bₙ admits a natural action on the n‑fold tensor product of any object in a braided monoidal category. In the extended setting, one replaces the braided monoidal category by a braided fusion 5‑category 𝒞 (a semisimple rigid monoidal 5‑category with braiding and duals). The higher categorical representation of Bₙ is then a monoidal functor Bₙ → Aut(𝒞⊠ n) where Aut denotes the 6‑category of auto‑equivalences. By the cobordism hypothesis, a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,6)‑category 6Vect of 6‑vector spaces determines a fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT. The construction proceeds by first forming the 6‑category 𝒞‑Mod of left module 5‑categories over 𝒞; this 6‑category is symmetric monoidal under the relative tensor product ⊠_𝒞. The braid group action lifts to an action by monoidal auto‑equivalences of 𝒞‑Mod, and the homotopy fixed points of this action produce a 6‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_𝒞 : Bord₆fr → 6Vect. The value of Z_𝒞 on a point is the 5‑category 𝒞 itself, on a circle it is the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) (now a braided fusion 4‑category), and on a closed 6‑manifold M it computes a state‑sum invariant built from the 6‑j symbols of 𝒞. When 𝒞 is the 5‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of the six‑dimensional (2,0) theory of type Ak-1. The partition function on S⁶ reproduces the Witten index of the (2,0) theory, while the invariant of S³ × S³ yields the modular S‑matrix of the associated 4‑dimensional TQFT obtained by dimensional reduction. This construction generalizes the 3‑dimensional Reshetikhin–Turaev and 4‑dimensional Crane–Yetter models and provides a categorical framework for the classification of 6‑dimensional topological orders with non‑invertible symmetries.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Explain why the braid group representation on a braided fusion 5‑category yields a fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT, and identify the categorical level at which the Drinfeld center appears."
The braid group Bₙ acts by monoidal auto‑equivalences on the 6‑category 𝒞‑Mod of module 5‑categories over a braided fusion 5‑category 𝒞. Taking homotopy fixed points of this action produces a symmetric monoidal functor Bord₆fr → 6Vect, i.e. a fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT. The Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) is a braided fusion 4‑category and appears as the value of the TQFT on the circle S¹, because the circle is the 1‑dimensional manifold whose bordism category encodes the center construction. Thus the center appears one categorical level below the input 5‑category.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the categorical level of a center or a trace, remember the rule: an n‑dimensional extended TQFT assigns a k‑category to a (n−k)‑manifold. The Drinfeld center of the input (n−1)‑category lives on the circle, which is (n−(n−1)) = 1‑dimensional, so it is an (n−2)‑category. For n=6 this gives a 4‑category.

The Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Seven‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Higher Categorical Centers

In a fully extended 7‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord₇G → 7Vect constructed from a fusion 6‑category 𝒟, gapped boundary conditions correspond to left module 6‑categories over 𝒟. The classification of such boundaries up to equivalence is governed by the higher categorical center Z₇(𝒟), defined as the E₇‑center of 𝒟 in the sense of higher Morita theory. Concretely, Z₇(𝒟) is the 7‑category of endomorphisms of the unit object in the symmetric monoidal 7‑category 𝒟‑Mod of module 6‑categories. Its objects are pairs (M, γ) where M is a 𝒟‑module 6‑category and γ is a half‑braiding with every other module. The condensation completion of Z₇(𝒟) yields the 8‑category of all defects of codimension ≤ 7, and the fully extended TQFT factors through this completion by the cobordism hypothesis. For the 7‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G and twist ω ∈ H⁷(G, U(1)), the fusion 6‑category is the 6‑category of G‑graded 6‑vector spaces with associator twisted by ω. Its higher center is equivalent to the 7‑category of representations of the 7‑group B⁶G twisted by ω, and gapped boundaries are classified by subgroups H ⊂ G together with a trivialization of ω|_H. In the case of the 7‑dimensional Crane–Yetter model built from a modular fusion 5‑category 𝒞, the higher center reduces to the Drinfeld center of 𝒞 (a braided fusion 4‑category) and the boundary classification reproduces the Lagrangian algebra classification of gapped boundaries in the associated 6‑dimensional theory. The general theorem states that for any fusion n‑category 𝒟, the (n+1)‑category of gapped boundaries of the (n+1)‑dimensional TQFT is equivalent to the condensation completion of the En+1‑center of 𝒟.

Factorization Homology on Manifolds with Non‑Trivial Higher Homotopy Groups and the Computation of Higher Traces for Non‑Orientable Manifolds

Factorization homology ∫_M A of an E_n‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal (∞,1)‑category 𝒞 over a manifold M with non‑trivial higher homotopy groups requires a model for the E_n‑algebra in the ∞‑category of local systems on M. When M is non‑orientable, the orientation local system w₁ ∈ H¹(M, ℤ₂) twists the E_n‑structure: one works with an E_n‑algebra Aw₁ in the category of w₁‑twisted local systems. The higher trace of the identity on the object Z(∅) in the target (∞,n)‑category is then computed as ∫_M Aw₁. For a 3‑dimensional Turaev–Viro theory based on a spherical fusion category 𝒞, the E₃‑algebra is the Drinfeld center Z(𝒞) regarded as an E₃‑algebra in 3Vect. On a non‑orientable 3‑manifold such as ℝP³, the twisted factorization homology reduces to a state sum where each tetrahedron carries a sign factor (−1)w₁ ∪ w₂ with w₂ the second Stiefel–Whitney class of the triangulation. The result matches the modified trace on projective objects in the non‑semisimple category 𝒞 when 𝒞 is not semisimple. For a 4‑dimensional Crane–Yetter theory, the E₄‑algebra is the 4‑vector space Z(𝒞) and the twisted factorization homology on ℝP⁴ yields the partition function 1/|G| ∑[P]∈H¹(ℝP⁴,ℤ₂) (−1)∫ w₁ ∪ w₂(P) which vanishes because w₁ ∪ w₂ generates H³(ℝP⁴, ℤ₂) ≅ ℤ₂. The general excision formula for twisted factorization homology states that for a decomposition M = M₁ ∪_N M₂ along a codimension‑1 submanifold N, the invariant satisfies ∫_M Aw₁ ≃ ∫M₁ Aw₁ ⊠∫_N Aw₁|_N ∫M₂ Aw₁, where the relative tensor product is taken in the (∞,n)‑category of modules over the En-1‑algebra ∫_N Aw₁|_N. This formula is the computational engine for higher traces on manifolds with corners and non‑trivial homotopy.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Ten‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 8‑Categories

A fully extended 10‑dimensional TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₁₀G → 10Vect where 10Vect is the (∞,10)‑category of 10‑vector spaces. By the cobordism hypothesis, such theories are classified by fully dualizable objects in 10Vect. A systematic source of fully dualizable objects is provided by fusion 8‑categories: semisimple rigid monoidal 8‑categories with duals for objects, 1‑morphisms, …, 8‑morphisms. Given a fusion 8‑category ℱ, one forms the 9‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 8‑categories; this 9‑category is symmetric monoidal under relative tensor product. The object ℱ itself, viewed as a module over itself, is fully dualizable in ℱ‑Mod. The cobordism hypothesis then produces a 10‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_ℱ. The value of Z_ℱ on a point is ℱ, on a circle it is the Drinfeld center Z(ℱ) (a braided fusion 7‑category), on a 2‑sphere it is the 2‑fold center Z₂(ℱ) (a symmetric fusion 6‑category), and so on. The partition function on a closed 10‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 10‑j symbols of ℱ — higher associators and braidings up to level 8. When ℱ is the 8‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is conjectured to describe the topological sector of type IIB string theory on a 10‑manifold with appropriate fluxes. The anomaly of the theory is captured by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG for the tangential structure G (e.g., Spin, String, Fivebrane). For G = Spin, the group of invertible 10‑dimensional phases is π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ factor is the signature defect and the ℤ₂ factor is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant. The fusion 8‑category construction automatically yields a theory whose anomaly matches the chosen element of this group, providing a categorical realization of the Green–Schwarz mechanism in ten dimensions.

  • Confusing the level of the Drinfeld center in a 6‑dimensional TQFT: the center of a braided fusion 5‑category is a braided fusion 4‑category, not a 5‑category.
  • Assuming that a fivebrane structure is the same as a string structure; the fivebrane structure kills p₂/6 while the string structure kills p₁/2.
  • Treating the higher categorical center Z₇(𝒟) as an ordinary Drinfeld center; it is an E₇‑center living one categorical level higher.
  • Forgetting that twisted factorization homology on a non‑orientable manifold requires the orientation local system w₁ to twist the E_n‑algebra, not just the manifold.
  • Misidentifying the fusion 8‑category input for a 10‑dimensional TQFT as a fusion 9‑category; the input is an 8‑category because the theory is 10‑dimensional and the point gets an 8‑category.
  • Overlooking that the condensation completion of a fusion n‑category is an (n+1)‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 7‑D is an 8‑category, not a 7‑category.
  • Assuming the partition function on ℝP⁴ for a 4‑D Crane–Yetter model is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the twist w₁ ∪ w₂ is non‑trivial.
The advanced sections above complete the toolkit for analyzing extended TQFTs across dimensions, tangential structures, and higher categorical inputs.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Fivebrane Structures and the Classification of Seven‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

A fivebrane structure on a smooth manifold is a lift of a string structure that trivialises the next fractional Pontryagin class, namely p₂/6 ∈ H⁸(−; ℤ). In the language of tangential structures this corresponds to a map of ∞‑groupoids BFivebrane → BO whose homotopy fibre is the 7‑connected cover of BO. The Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTFivebrane therefore classifies fivebrane bordism, and Anderson duality applied to MTFivebrane yields the group of invertible seven‑dimensional topological phases with fivebrane structure. By the cobordism hypothesis, a fully extended 7‑dimensional TQFT with fivebrane structure is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₇Fivebrane → 7Vect, and such functors are classified by fully dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal (∞,7)‑category 7Vect equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(7)‑action twisted by the fivebrane map.

The computation of π₀(Iℤ MTFivebrane) has been carried out using the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The result is a direct sum ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ factor is the signature defect (the 7‑dimensional analogue of the 4‑dimensional signature), the first ℤ₂ is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant in dimension seven, and the second ℤ₂ is a new invariant detected by the fivebrane structure itself. This second ℤ₂ does not appear for string structures and reflects the fact that the fivebrane lift kills a torsion class in π₇(BO) that survives in the string case. Consequently, there exist exactly four distinct invertible seven‑dimensional phases with fivebrane structure, and any fully extended 7‑dimensional TQFT with fivebrane structure must have its anomaly valued in this group.

From the higher categorical side, a systematic source of fully dualizable objects in 7Vect is provided by fusion 5‑categories. Given a fusion 5‑category ℱ, the 6‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 5‑categories is symmetric monoidal, and ℱ as a module over itself is fully dualizable. The cobordism hypothesis then produces a 7‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_ℱ whose value on a point is ℱ, on a circle is the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ) (a braided fusion 4‑category), on a 2‑sphere is the 2‑fold centre Z₂(ℱ) (a symmetric fusion 3‑category), and so on. The partition function on a closed fivebrane 7‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 7‑j symbols of ℱ — higher associators and braidings up to level 5. When ℱ is the 5‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of M‑theory on a seven‑manifold with a fivebrane background.

The anomaly of Z_ℱ is determined by the image of the fundamental class [M] under the map MTFivebrane → Iℤ induced by the fully dualizable object. In practice one computes the anomaly by evaluating the partition function on a set of generating bordisms, for example the 7‑dimensional Milnor sphere Σ⁷ with its unique fivebrane structure. The result matches the ℤ₂ factor coming from the fivebrane lift, providing a categorical realisation of the Green–Schwarz mechanism in seven dimensions.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Six Dimensions

The braid group Bₙ admits a natural action on the n‑fold tensor product of any object in a braided monoidal category. In the fully extended setting this action lifts to an action of the (∞,1)‑category of braids on the (∞,n)‑category of higher vector spaces. For a six‑dimensional extended TQFT, the relevant input is a braided fusion 4‑category ℬ. The Drinfeld centre construction applied twice to a fusion 4‑category yields a braided fusion 4‑category, and the third centre yields a symmetric fusion 3‑category. The cobordism hypothesis then assigns to a point the fusion 4‑category ℬ, to a circle the braided fusion 3‑category Z(ℬ), to a 2‑sphere the symmetric fusion 2‑category Z₂(ℬ), and to a 3‑sphere the 3‑vector space Z₃(ℬ).

The braid group Bₙ appears as the mapping class group of the n‑punctured disc, which is the configuration space of n points in ℝ². In six dimensions the analogous configuration space is the space of n disjoint 2‑spheres in ℝ⁴, whose mapping class group is the 2‑braid group (also called the braid group of 2‑spheres). Higher categorical representations of this 2‑braid group are furnished by the action of the 2‑braid group on the 2‑fold tensor product of objects in a braided fusion 4‑category. Concretely, a 2‑braid is a motion of 2‑spheres in four dimensions, and its representation on the 6‑vector space assigned to a 4‑manifold with boundary is given by a sequence of higher R‑matrices satisfying the Zamolodchikov tetrahedron equation.

A state‑sum model for the six‑dimensional TQFT can be built from a triangulation of a closed 6‑manifold M. Each 6‑simplex carries a 6‑j symbol, each 5‑simplex a 5‑j symbol, and so on down to the 2‑j symbols which are the associators of the braided fusion 4‑category. The partition function is the contraction of this tensor network over all internal labels. When ℬ is the 4‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting invariants are expected to coincide with the six‑dimensional analogues of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants, and to compute the partition function of the (2,0) superconformal theory on M.

The anomaly of the six‑dimensional theory is classified by π₀(Iℤ MTString) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ factor is the signature defect in dimension six and the ℤ₂ factor is the Arf invariant of the associated 3‑manifold boundary. The braided fusion 4‑category input automatically carries a choice of anomaly via its pivotal structure; the cobordism hypothesis guarantees that the resulting extended TQFT has precisely this anomaly. In particular, the non‑trivial ℤ₂ anomaly corresponds to the choice of a non‑trivial quadratic refinement of the intersection form on H³(M; ℤ₂), which is encoded in the higher braiding data of ℬ.

Worked Example: Computing the Anomaly of a 6‑Dimensional TQFT from a Braided Fusion 4‑Category
Let ℬ be the braided fusion 4‑category of representations of the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩₂) at a primitive 5th root of unity. Determine the anomaly of the associated fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT Z_ℬ with string structure.
The anomaly group for string‑structured 6‑manifolds is π₀(Iℤ MTString) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂. The ℤ factor is the signature defect; for a 6‑manifold M it equals (1/45)∫_M p₁² where p₁ is the first Pontryagin class. The ℤ₂ factor is the Arf invariant of the 3‑dimensional boundary of a 4‑manifold bounding M. The braided fusion 4‑category ℬ has a pivotal structure determined by the quantum dimension of the fundamental representation, which at q = e2πi/5 equals 2cos(π/5). This pivotal structure yields a quadratic refinement of the intersection form on H³(−; ℤ₂) that is non‑trivial, corresponding to the non‑zero element in the ℤ₂ summand. The signature defect for ℬ is computed from the central charge c = 3k/(k+2) with k = 3 (since the level is 3 for the 5th root of unity), giving c = 9/5. The signature defect in six dimensions is proportional to c, and evaluates to the generator of the ℤ summand. Hence the anomaly of Z_ℬ is (1, 1) ∈ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the anomaly of an extended TQFT, first identify the tangential structure (framed, oriented, spin, string, fivebrane, …). Then recall the Anderson dual of the corresponding Madsen–Tillmann spectrum; the low‑dimensional homotopy groups of that spectrum are tabulated in the literature. Next, determine the fully dualizable object (fusion n‑category, Eₙ‑algebra, etc.) and compute its image under the cobordism hypothesis map to the anomaly group. In practice the anomaly is often read off from the pivotal or spherical structure of the input higher category. Memorise the anomaly groups for dimensions ≤ 7: framed → ℤ (signature), oriented → ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, spin → ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, string → ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂, fivebrane → ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂.

The Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Ten‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Higher Categorical Centers

A fully extended 10‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord₁₀G → 10Vect assigns to a point a fully dualizable object in 10Vect, typically a fusion 8‑category ℱ. Gapped boundaries of such a theory correspond to 9‑dimensional extended TQFTs living on the boundary, which by the cobordism hypothesis with singularities are classified by 1‑morphisms in the (∞,10)‑category of boundary conditions. In the higher categorical language, a gapped boundary is a left module 8‑category over ℱ, i.e. an object of the 9‑category ℱ‑Mod. Two boundaries are equivalent if the corresponding module 8‑categories are Morita equivalent, which is precisely the statement that they become isomorphic after passing to the condensation completion.

The condensation completion of a fusion 8‑category ℱ is a 9‑category Cond(ℱ) whose objects are condensable algebras in ℱ. The higher categorical centre Z₉(ℱ) — the 9‑fold Drinfeld centre — is a symmetric fusion (−1)‑category, i.e. a commutative algebra in 10Vect, and it classifies the possible topological defects of codimension one. The classification theorem states that the (∞,1)‑category of gapped boundaries of Z_ℱ is equivalent to the (∞,1)‑category of module 8‑categories over ℱ, which in turn is equivalent to the (∞,1)‑category of separable algebras in Z₉(ℱ). Consequently, the set of equivalence classes of gapped boundaries is in bijection with the set of Morita classes of condensable algebras in ℱ.

When ℱ is the 8‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the condensable algebras correspond to Lagrangian algebras in the modular tensor category Z₈(ℱ) (the 8‑fold centre). The number of such Lagrangian algebras is finite and can be computed from the modular data (S‑matrix, T‑matrix) of Z₈(ℱ). For example, for the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩₂) at level k = 4, the 8‑fold centre is a modular tensor category of rank 5, and it admits exactly three Lagrangian algebras, giving three distinct gapped boundaries for the associated 10‑dimensional TQFT.

The anomaly of a boundary theory is the restriction of the bulk anomaly to the boundary. In ten dimensions the bulk anomaly group for spin structure is π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂. A gapped boundary exists only if the bulk anomaly vanishes when pulled back to the boundary, which imposes a constraint on the choice of condensable algebra. This constraint is precisely the condition that the corresponding Lagrangian algebra be non‑anomalous, i.e. that its Frobenius–Schur indicator be trivial. The higher categorical centre Z₉(ℱ) encodes this condition internally, so the classification of boundaries automatically incorporates anomaly cancellation.

Factorization Homology and the Computation of Higher Traces on Manifolds with Non‑Trivial π₂ and π₃

Factorization homology ∫_M A of an Eₙ‑algebra A over an n‑manifold M satisfies excision: for a decomposition M = M₁ ∪_N M₂ along a codimension‑1 submanifold N, there is an equivalence ∫_M A ≃ ∫M₁ A ⊗∫_N A ∫M₂ A in the (∞,1)‑category of modules over the En‑1‑algebra ∫_N A. When M has non‑trivial higher homotopy groups, the factorization homology can be computed by a cellular decomposition that respects the Postnikov tower of M. In particular, if π₂(M) ≠ 0, one can attach 3‑cells to kill π₂, and the effect on factorization homology is captured by a higher trace map Tr : ∫S³ A → ∫pt A ≃ A. This trace is the (∞,1)‑categorical analogue of the ordinary trace of an endomorphism, but it lands in the Hochschild homology of A rather than in its centre.

For a 4‑dimensional TQFT coming from a braided fusion 2‑category ℬ (an E₃‑algebra in 3Vect), the factorization homology on a closed 4‑manifold M with π₂(M) = ℤ^r is computed by a state‑sum on a handle decomposition. Each 2‑handle contributes a copy of the object ∫S² A ≃ Z(ℬ) (the Drinfeld centre of ℬ), and the attaching maps of the 3‑handles induce higher trace maps Tr : Z(ℬ) → ℬ. The result is a tensor network whose contraction yields the partition function. When π₃(M) ≠ 0, 4‑handles contribute a further trace Tr : ∫S³ A → A, which is the 3‑dimensional higher trace. In the language of higher categories, these traces are the components of the (∞,4)‑categorical trace of the identity 4‑morphism on the fully dualizable object A.

Explicit formulas for the higher traces on spheres are known in terms of the categorical dimensions of the input higher category. For an Eₙ‑algebra A in nVect, the factorization homology of the n‑sphere is the n‑fold categorical trace of the unit object, which equals the n‑th categorical dimension dimₙ(1_A). For a fusion n‑category ℱ, dimₙ(1_ℱ) is the global dimension of ℱ, a non‑zero scalar in the ground field. When M has non‑trivial π₂ and π₃, the partition function factorises as a product of global dimensions raised to the Betti numbers b₂, b₃, multiplied by a correction factor coming from the k‑invariants of the Postnikov tower. This correction factor is a cohomology class in H⁴(M; π₃(A)) that can be evaluated using the higher associator data of ℱ.

These computations are essential for the classification of topological orders in condensed matter systems with higher‑form symmetries. The higher traces on spheres give the quantum dimensions of the corresponding anyonic excitations, while the correction factors encode the 't Hooft anomalies of the higher‑form symmetries. The factorization homology framework thus provides a unified mathematical language for both the bulk invariants and the boundary anomaly inflow.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Eleven‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 9‑Categories

An eleven‑dimensional fully extended TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₁₁G → 11Vect, where 11Vect is the (∞,11)‑category of 11‑vector spaces. By the cobordism hypothesis, such theories are classified by fully dualizable objects in 11Vect equipped with a G‑structure. A systematic source of fully dualizable objects is provided by fusion 9‑categories: semisimple rigid monoidal 9‑categories with duals for objects, 1‑morphisms, …, 9‑morphisms. Given a fusion 9‑category ℱ, one forms the 10‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 9‑categories; this 10‑category is symmetric monoidal under relative tensor product. The object ℱ itself, viewed as a module over itself, is fully dualizable in ℱ‑Mod. The cobordism hypothesis then produces an 11‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_ℱ.

The value of Z_ℱ on a point is ℱ, on a circle it is the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ) (a braided fusion 8‑category), on a 2‑sphere it is the 2‑fold centre Z₂(ℱ) (a symmetric fusion 7‑category), and so on up to the 10‑fold centre Z₁₀(ℱ), which is a commutative algebra in 11Vect. The partition function on a closed 11‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 11‑j symbols of ℱ — higher associators and braidings up to level 9. When ℱ is the 9‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is conjectured to describe the topological sector of M‑theory on an 11‑manifold with appropriate C‑field flux.

The anomaly of the theory is captured by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG for the tangential structure G. For G = Spin, the group of invertible 11‑dimensional phases is π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the first ℤ₂ is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant in dimension eleven and the second ℤ₂ is a new invariant detected by the spin structure. The fusion 9‑category construction automatically yields a theory whose anomaly matches the chosen element of this group, providing a categorical realisation of the Green–Schwarz mechanism in eleven dimensions.

From the perspective of higher gauge theory, the 11‑dimensional TQFT Z_ℱ can be coupled to a 3‑form gauge field C with field strength G₄ = dC. The partition function then includes a factor exp(2πi ∫_M C ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄), which is the topological term of M‑theory. In the higher categorical language, this coupling corresponds to a map of E₁₁‑algebras from the E₁₁‑algebra representing the 3‑form gauge field to the E₁₁‑algebra Z₁₁(ℱ) = Z₁₀(ℱ). The existence of such a map is equivalent to the condition that the anomaly of Z_ℱ cancels the anomaly of the C‑field, which is precisely the statement that the total anomaly in π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) vanishes.

  • Confusing the level of the Drinfeld centre in an 11‑dimensional TQFT: the centre of a braided fusion 8‑category is a braided fusion 7‑category, not an 8‑category.
  • Assuming that a fivebrane structure is the same as a string structure; the fivebrane structure kills p₂/6 while the string structure kills p₁/2.
  • Treating the higher categorical centre Z₉(ℱ) as an ordinary Drinfeld centre; it is an E₉‑centre living one categorical level higher.
  • Forgetting that twisted factorization homology on a non‑orientable manifold requires the orientation local system w₁ to twist the Eₙ‑algebra, not just the manifold.
  • Misidentifying the fusion 9‑category input for an 11‑dimensional TQFT as a fusion 10‑category; the input is a 9‑category because the theory is 11‑dimensional and the point gets a 9‑category.
  • Overlooking that the condensation completion of a fusion n‑category is an (n+1)‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 10‑D is an 11‑category, not a 10‑category.
  • Assuming the partition function on ℝP¹¹ for an 11‑D Crane–Yetter‑type model is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the twist w₁ ∪ w₂ ∪ w₃ is non‑trivial.

Ten‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 8‑Categories and the Cobordism Hypothesis

A ten‑dimensional fully extended topological quantum field theory is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₁₀ᴳ → 10Vect, where 10Vect denotes the (∞,10)‑category of 10‑vector spaces. By the cobordism hypothesis, such theories are classified by fully dualizable objects in 10Vect equipped with a G‑structure. A canonical source of fully dualizable objects is provided by fusion 8‑categories: semisimple rigid monoidal 8‑categories with duals for objects, 1‑morphisms, …, 8‑morphisms. Given a fusion 8‑category ℱ, one forms the 9‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 8‑categories; this 9‑category is symmetric monoidal under the relative tensor product ⊠_ℱ. The object ℱ itself, viewed as a module over itself, is fully dualizable in ℱ‑Mod. The cobordism hypothesis then produces a 10‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_ℱ.

The value of Z_ℱ on a point is ℱ. On a circle it is the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ), a braided fusion 7‑category. On a 2‑sphere it is the 2‑fold centre Z₂(ℱ), a braided fusion 6‑category with a sylleptic structure. Iterating, the k‑fold centre Zₖ(ℱ) is an Eₖ‑fusion (8−k)‑category. The 8‑fold centre Z₈(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category, i.e. a finite abelian group equipped with a quadratic form. The 9‑fold centre Z₉(ℱ) is an E₉‑algebra in 10Vect, and the 10‑fold centre Z₁₀(ℱ) is a commutative algebra in 10Vect whose underlying object is the ground field k (or a finite sum of copies of k when ℱ is multi‑fusion).

The partition function on a closed 10‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 10‑j symbols of ℱ — higher associators, braidings, and syllepses up to level 8. When ℱ is the 8‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to describe the topological sector of a ten‑dimensional string theory compactification with appropriate fluxes. The anomaly of the theory is captured by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG for the tangential structure G. For G = Spin, the group of invertible 10‑dimensional phases is π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the summands correspond to the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant in dimension ten, a spin‑structure‑dependent invariant detected by the η‑invariant of the Dirac operator, and a third invariant related to the w₂² Stiefel–Whitney class. The fusion 8‑category construction automatically yields a theory whose anomaly matches a chosen element of this group, providing a categorical realisation of the Green–Schwarz mechanism in ten dimensions.

From the perspective of higher gauge theory, the 10‑dimensional TQFT Z_ℱ can be coupled to a 2‑form gauge field B with field strength H₃ = dB. The partition function then includes a factor exp(2πi ∫_M B ∪ H₃ ∪ H₃ ∪ H₃), which is the topological term of the NS–NS sector of type II string theory. In the higher categorical language, this coupling corresponds to a map of E₁₀‑algebras from the E₁₀‑algebra representing the 2‑form gauge field to the E₁₀‑algebra Z₁₀(ℱ). The existence of such a map is equivalent to the condition that the anomaly of Z_ℱ cancels the anomaly of the B‑field, which is precisely the statement that the total anomaly in π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) vanishes.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Fivebrane Structures and the Classification of Ten‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

A fivebrane structure on a manifold M is a lift of the classifying map M → BSpin through the homotopy fibre of the map BSpin → K(ℤ, 8) representing the fractional Pontryagin class p₂/6. Equivalently, it is a trivialisation of the degree‑8 characteristic class p₂(TM)/6 ∈ H⁸(M; ℤ). The cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with fivebrane structures states that the (∞,10)‑category Bord₁₀Fivebrane of 10‑dimensional cobordisms equipped with fivebrane structures is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,10)‑category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object — the point equipped with its canonical fivebrane structure. Consequently, a fully extended 10‑dimensional TQFT with fivebrane structure is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₁₀Fivebrane → 𝒞, classified by a fully dualizable object in 𝒞.

The classification of invertible ten‑dimensional topological phases with fivebrane structure is given by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTFivebrane. The homotopy groups of MTFivebrane in low degrees are known: π₋₁₀(MTFivebrane) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂. The ℤ summand corresponds to the signature divided by a suitable normalisation (the E₈ index), while the two ℤ₂ summands are detected by the w₂² and w₄ Stiefel–Whitney classes. Anomalous phases are those that do not extend to a theory on manifolds with fivebrane structure; they are classified by the cokernel of the map π₋₁₀(MTFivebrane) → π₋₁₀(MTSpin) induced by the forgetful map MTFivebrane → MTSpin. This cokernel is ℤ₂, generated by the phase whose partition function on a closed 10‑manifold M with spin structure is (−1)∫_M w₂² ∪ w₄.

In the context of M‑theory and type IIA string theory, fivebrane structures appear naturally as the geometric data required to define the worldvolume theory of an M5‑brane. The anomaly inflow mechanism for the M5‑brane involves a 7‑dimensional bulk theory whose boundary is the 6‑dimensional (2,0) theory. The bulk theory is a 7‑dimensional extended TQFT classified by a fully dualizable object in 7Vect with a suitable tangential structure. The fivebrane structure on the 10‑dimensional spacetime ensures that the C‑field flux G₄ satisfies the quantisation condition G₄ − p₂/6 ∈ H⁴(M; ℤ). The extended TQFT constructed from a fusion 8‑category ℱ with a fivebrane structure automatically incorporates this quantisation condition through the higher categorical data of ℱ.

The state‑sum model for a 10‑dimensional TQFT with fivebrane structure on a triangulated manifold M assigns a 10‑j symbol to each 10‑simplex, a 9‑j symbol to each 9‑simplex, and so on down to 0‑j symbols (quantum dimensions) on vertices. The fivebrane structure enters as a constraint on the labelling of 8‑simplices: the product of 8‑j symbols around an 8‑simplex must equal the evaluation of the fivebrane class p₂/6 on that simplex. This constraint is implemented by twisting the state‑sum with a cocycle representing the fivebrane structure. The resulting partition function is independent of the triangulation and defines a topological invariant of the pair (M, fivebrane structure).

Worked Example: Anomaly Cancellation in a 10‑D Fusion 8‑Category TQFT
Let ℱ be a fusion 8‑category such that the associated 10‑D TQFT Z_ℱ has anomaly a ∈ π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂. Suppose we couple Z_ℱ to a 2‑form gauge field B with anomaly b = (1,0,1) in the same group. Determine the condition on a for the total anomaly to vanish.
The total anomaly is the sum a + b in the abelian group ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂. For the coupled theory to be anomaly‑free we need a + b = (0,0,0), i.e. a = b = (1,0,1). Thus Z_ℱ must have anomaly exactly (1,0,1). In terms of the fusion 8‑category, this means the 10‑fold centre Z₁₀(ℱ) must carry a commutative algebra structure whose associated quadratic form on the finite abelian group Z₈(ℱ) evaluates to the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant plus the w₂² invariant. Concretely, if ℱ = Rep(U_q(𝔰𝔩₂)) at a root of unity of order ℓ, the anomaly is determined by the parity of ℓ and the choice of pivotal structure. For ℓ odd, the anomaly is (1,0,1); for ℓ even, it is (0,1,0). Hence the anomaly‑free coupling requires ℓ odd.
Exam Strategy: Tracking Centre Levels
When a question asks for the value of an n‑dimensional extended TQFT on a k‑sphere, remember: the input is a fusion (n−2)‑category ℱ. The value on Sᵏ is the k‑fold Drinfeld centre Zₖ(ℱ), which is an Eₖ‑fusion (n−2−k)‑category. For k = n−2 you get an En−2‑fusion 0‑category (a finite group with quadratic form). For k = n−1 you get an En−1‑algebra in nVect. For k = n you get a commutative algebra in nVect. Write down the sequence ℱ, Z(ℱ), Z₂(ℱ), … explicitly to avoid off‑by‑one errors.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Six Dimensions

The braid group B_n admits a natural action on the n‑fold tensor product of any object in a braided monoidal category. In a fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord₆ᴳ → 6Vect constructed from a fusion 4‑category ℱ, the value on a circle is the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ), a braided fusion 3‑category. The mapping class group of the circle is ℤ, but the mapping class group of a punctured disk — which appears as the automorphism group of a configuration of n points in the 2‑disk — is the braid group B_n. Higher categorical representations of B_n arise from the action of the mapping class group on the factorization homology ∫_{D² ∖ {n points}} Z(ℱ).

Concretely, let X = D² ∖ {p1, …, p_n}. The factorization homology ∫_X Z(ℱ) is equivalent to the n‑fold tensor product Z(ℱ)⊠ n in the symmetric monoidal 4‑category Z(ℱ)‑Mod. The mapping class group π₀ Diff(X, ∂X) ≅ B_n acts on this object by braiding the n factors. This action is implemented by the braiding in the braided fusion 3‑category Z(ℱ). At the level of 4‑morphisms, the braiding is a 3‑natural equivalence βA,B : A ⊠ B → B ⊠ A satisfying the hexagon axioms up to coherent 4‑isomorphisms. The higher coherence data of the braided fusion 3‑category — the syllepsis and the 4‑dimensional braiding — ensure that the action of B_n extends to a representation on the full higher categorical structure, not just on the Grothendieck ring.

The partition function of the 6‑D TQFT on a 6‑manifold M with a codimension‑2 defect labelled by an object A ∈ Z(ℱ) can be computed by factorization homology on the complement of the defect. When the defect is a 4‑manifold Σ with n punctures, the factorization homology reduces to the B_n‑representation on A⊠ n. The trace of a braid β ∈ B_n in this representation gives the invariant of the link obtained by closing the braid in Σ × S¹. This construction generalises the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links in 3‑manifolds to invariants of 4‑dimensional submanifolds in 6‑manifolds.

When ℱ is the 4‑category of representations of a quantum group U_q(𝔤) at a root of unity, the braided fusion 3‑category Z(ℱ) is equivalent to the 3‑category of modules over the quantum double D(U_q(𝔤)). The higher categorical representation of B_n then recovers the higher quantum group invariants of 4‑knots studied by Carter, Saito, and others. The state‑sum model for the 6‑D TQFT assigns 6‑j symbols to 6‑simplices, 5‑j symbols to 5‑simplices, and so on. The braid group action appears in the gluing formulas for 4‑simplices along 3‑faces, where the associator and braiding 3‑cells of Z(ℱ) enter.

The Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Seven‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Higher Categorical Centers

A gapped boundary of a 7‑dimensional extended TQFT Z : Bord₇ᴳ → 7Vect is a symmetric monoidal functor ∂Z : Bord₆ᴳ → 7Vect equipped with a natural transformation to the restriction of Z to the boundary. By the cobordism hypothesis with singularities, such boundaries are classified by 1‑morphisms in 7Vect from the unit object to the value of Z on a point. When Z is constructed from a fusion 5‑category ℱ, the value on a point is ℱ. The category of gapped boundaries is therefore equivalent to the 6‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 5‑categories over ℱ.

The condensation completion of ℱ is the 6‑category Kar(ℱ) of separable algebra objects in ℱ and their bimodules. This 6‑category is the higher categorical centre Z₁(ℱ) in the sense of higher Morita theory. The objects of Kar(ℱ) correspond to gapped boundaries of the 7‑D bulk theory. The 1‑morphisms correspond to domain walls between boundaries, the 2‑morphisms to junctions of domain walls, and so on up to 6‑morphisms which correspond to local operators at the intersection of six domain walls.

The higher categorical centre Zₖ(ℱ) for k ≤ 5 classifies defects of codimension k. In particular, Z₂(ℱ) classifies codimension‑2 defects (4‑dimensional), Z₃(ℱ) classifies codimension‑3 defects (3‑dimensional), and Z₅(ℱ) classifies codimension‑5 defects (1‑dimensional, i.e. line operators). The 6‑fold centre Z₆(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category, i.e. a finite abelian group with a quadratic form, which classifies the possible topological orders of the 6‑dimensional boundary theory itself.

The classification of gapped boundaries in 7‑D is therefore a tower of higher categorical centres: ℱ‑Mod ≃ Z₁(ℱ) ⊃ Z₂(ℱ) ⊃ … ⊃ Z₆(ℱ). Each inclusion corresponds to the operation of taking the Drinfeld centre in the appropriate higher categorical sense. The condensation completion Kar(ℱ) is the universal 6‑category receiving a map from ℱ that sends all objects to fully dualizable objects. This universal property characterises the category of gapped boundaries independently of the choice of fusion 5‑category ℱ representing the bulk.

When ℱ is the 5‑category of representations of a finite group G (the Dijkgraaf–Witten case), the gapped boundaries are classified by subgroups H ⊂ G up to conjugation, together with a cohomology class in H³(H; U(1)). The higher categorical centres reproduce the higher group cohomology: Zₖ(Rep(G)) ≃ Rep(Λ^k G) where Λ^k G is the k‑fold loop group. For a general fusion 5‑category, the centres are not group‑theoretic but are described by the higher representation theory of the fusion 5‑category.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Twelve‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 10‑Categories

A twelve‑dimensional fully extended TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₁₂ᴳ → 12Vect, where 12Vect is the (∞,12)‑category of 12‑vector spaces. By the cobordism hypothesis, such theories are classified by fully dualizable objects in 12Vect equipped with a G‑structure. A systematic source of fully dualizable objects is provided by fusion 10‑categories: semisimple rigid monoidal 10‑categories with duals for objects, 1‑morphisms, …, 10‑morphisms. Given a fusion 10‑category ℱ, one forms the 11‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 10‑categories; this 11‑category is symmetric monoidal under relative tensor product. The object ℱ itself, viewed as a module over itself, is fully dualizable in ℱ‑Mod. The cobordism hypothesis then produces a 12‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_ℱ.

The value of Z_ℱ on a point is ℱ. On a circle it is the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ), a braided fusion 9‑category. On a 2‑sphere it is the 2‑fold centre Z₂(ℱ), a braided fusion 8‑category with sylleptic structure. Iterating, the k‑fold centre Zₖ(ℱ) is an Eₖ‑fusion (10−k)‑category. The 10‑fold centre Z₁₀(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category — a finite abelian group with a quadratic form. The 11‑fold centre Z₁₁(ℱ) is an E₁₁‑algebra in 12Vect, and the 12‑fold centre Z₁₂(ℱ) is a commutative algebra in 12Vect whose underlying object is the ground field k (or a finite sum of copies of k for multi‑fusion ℱ).

The partition function on a closed 12‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 12‑j symbols of ℱ — higher associators, braidings, syllepses, and higher coherences up to level 10. When ℱ is the 10‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is conjectured to describe the topological sector of F‑theory on a 12‑manifold with appropriate fluxes. The anomaly of the theory is captured by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG. For G = Spin, the group of invertible 12‑dimensional phases is π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, where the ℤ summand is the signature (the E₈ index in dimension 12), and the three ℤ₂ summands are detected by Stiefel–Whitney numbers w₂⁶, w₄ w₂⁴, and w₆ w₂³.

From the perspective of higher gauge theory, the 12‑dimensional TQFT Z_ℱ can be coupled to a 3‑form gauge field C with field strength G₄ = dC. The partition function then includes a factor exp(2πi ∫_M C ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄), which is the topological term of F‑theory. In the higher categorical language, this coupling corresponds to a map of E₁₂‑algebras from the E₁₂‑algebra representing the 3‑form gauge field to the E₁₂‑algebra Z₁₂(ℱ). The existence of such a map is equivalent to the condition that the anomaly of Z_ℱ cancels the anomaly of the C‑field, which is precisely the statement that the total anomaly in π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) vanishes.

  • Confusing the level of the Drinfeld centre in a 12‑dimensional TQFT: the centre of a braided fusion 9‑category is a braided fusion 8‑category, not a 9‑category.
  • Assuming that a fivebrane structure is the same as a string structure; the fivebrane structure kills p₂/6 while the string structure kills p₁/2.
  • Treating the higher categorical centre Z₁₀(ℱ) as an ordinary Drinfeld centre; it is an E₁₀‑centre living one categorical level higher.
  • Forgetting that twisted factorization homology on a non‑orientable manifold requires the orientation local system w₁ to twist the Eₙ‑algebra, not just the manifold.
  • Misidentifying the fusion 10‑category input for a 12‑dimensional TQFT as a fusion 11‑category; the input is a 10‑category because the theory is 12‑dimensional and the point gets a 10‑category.
  • Overlooking that the condensation completion of a fusion n‑category is an (n+1)‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 11‑D is a 12‑category, not an 11‑category.
  • Assuming the partition function on ℝP¹² for a 12‑D Crane–Yetter‑type model is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the twist w₁ ∪ w₂ ∪ w₃ ∪ w₄ is non‑trivial.

Thirteen-Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 11‑Categories and the Cobordism Hypothesis

A thirteen‑dimensional fully extended topological quantum field theory is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Bord₁₃ᴳ → 13Vect, where 13Vect denotes the (∞,13)‑category of 13‑vector spaces. By the cobordism hypothesis, such theories are classified by fully dualizable objects in 13Vect equipped with a tangential G‑structure. The canonical algebraic input is a fusion 11‑category ℱ — a semisimple rigid monoidal 11‑category with duals for all k‑morphisms up to k = 11. From ℱ one constructs the 12‑category ℱ‑Mod of left module 11‑categories, which inherits a symmetric monoidal structure from the relative tensor product. The object ℱ itself, regarded as a module over itself, is fully dualizable in ℱ‑Mod; the cobordism hypothesis then produces a 13‑dimensional extended TQFT Z_ℱ.

The value of Z_ℱ on a point is ℱ. On a circle it yields the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ), a braided fusion 10‑category. On a 2‑sphere one obtains the 2‑fold centre Z₂(ℱ), a braided fusion 9‑category with sylleptic structure. Iterating, the k‑fold centre Zₖ(ℱ) is an Eₖ‑fusion (11−k)‑category. The 11‑fold centre Z₁₁(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category — a finite abelian group equipped with a quadratic form. The 12‑fold centre Z₁₂(ℱ) is an E₁₂‑algebra in 13Vect, and the 13‑fold centre Z₁₃(ℱ) is a commutative algebra in 13Vect whose underlying object is the ground field k (or a finite direct sum of copies of k for multi‑fusion ℱ).

The partition function on a closed 13‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 13‑j symbols of ℱ — higher associators, braidings, syllepses, and higher coherences up to level 11. When ℱ arises as the 11‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of a hypothetical 13‑dimensional theory related to F‑theory or M‑theory compactifications. The anomaly of the theory is measured by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG. For G = Spin, the group of invertible 13‑dimensional phases is π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, detected by Stiefel–Whitney numbers w₂⁶ w₁, w₄ w₂⁴ w₁, w₆ w₂³ w₁, and w₈ w₂² w₁. In the language of higher gauge theory, the 13‑dimensional TQFT Z_ℱ can be coupled to a 3‑form gauge field C with field strength G₄ = dC; the partition function then includes a topological term exp(2πi ∫_M C ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄). This coupling corresponds to a map of E₁₃‑algebras from the E₁₃‑algebra representing the 3‑form gauge field to Z₁₃(ℱ), and anomaly cancellation is equivalent to the vanishing of the total anomaly in π₀(Iℤ MTSpin).

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Fivebrane Structures and the Classification of Twelve‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

A fivebrane structure on a manifold M is a lift of the classifying map of the tangent bundle τ_M : M → BSO through the 7‑connected cover BFivebrane → BString, where BString is the 3‑connected cover of BSO. The obstruction to a string structure is the fractional Pontryagin class p₁/2 ∈ H⁴(M; ℤ); the obstruction to a fivebrane structure is the next fractional Pontryagin class p₂/6 ∈ H⁸(M; ℤ). The cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with fivebrane structures states that the (∞,n)‑category BordₙFivebrane of n‑dimensional cobordisms equipped with fivebrane structures is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object — the point with its canonical fivebrane structure.

For n = 12, this yields a classification of fully extended 12‑dimensional TQFTs with fivebrane structure. The invertible phases are classified by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTFivebrane. The homotopy groups of MTFivebrane in low degrees are known: π₀(MTFivebrane) = 0, π₁ = 0, π₂ = 0, π₃ = 0, π₄ = 0, π₅ = 0, π₆ = 0, π₇ = 0, π₈ = ℤ (generated by p₂/6), π₉ = 0, π₁₀ = 0, π₁₁ = 0, π₁₂ = ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂. The ℤ summand in π₁₂ corresponds to the signature (the E₈ index in dimension 12), while the three ℤ₂ summands are detected by the Stiefel–Whitney numbers w₂⁶, w₄ w₂⁴, and w₆ w₂³. The Anderson dual Iℤ MTFivebrane therefore has π₀(Iℤ MTFivebrane) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, matching the invertible phases of 12‑dimensional Spin TQFTs but with the additional constraint that the p₂/6 obstruction vanishes.

Anomalous topological phases in twelve dimensions with fivebrane structure are classified by the torsion subgroup of π₀(Iℤ MTFivebrane), i.e. ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂. These anomalies can be realised by coupling a 12‑dimensional TQFT to a 3‑form gauge field C with field strength G₄; the anomaly inflow term is exp(2πi ∫_M C ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄ ∪ G₄). In the higher categorical language, a fivebrane structure corresponds to a trivialisation of the E₈‑gerbe associated to p₂/6, which in factorization homology is implemented by a map of E₁₂‑algebras from the E₁₂‑algebra representing the fivebrane structure to the E₁₂‑algebra Z₁₂(ℱ) of the TQFT. The existence of such a map is equivalent to the condition that the p₂/6 anomaly of the TQFT cancels against the background fivebrane structure.

Worked Example: Computing the 12‑Dimensional Anomaly for a Fivebrane‑Structured TQFT
Let Z be a fully extended 12‑dimensional TQFT with Spin structure, classified by a fully dualizable object in 12Vect. Suppose the partition function of Z on a closed 12‑manifold M is given by Z(M) = exp(2πi (σ(M)/8 + ∫_M w₂⁶ + w₄ w₂⁴ + w₆ w₂³)), where σ(M) is the signature. Now equip M with a fivebrane structure. Determine the anomaly of Z as a fivebrane‑structured theory.
The anomaly of a Spin TQFT in 12 dimensions is an element of π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂. The given partition function shows that the ℤ component (signature) is 1/8 times the signature, i.e. the E₈ index. The three ℤ₂ components are all non‑trivial because the Stiefel–Whitney numbers w₂⁶, w₄ w₂⁴, w₆ w₂³ appear with coefficient 1. A fivebrane structure requires p₂/6 = 0. The map MTFivebrane → MTSpin induces a map on Anderson duals Iℤ MTSpin → Iℤ MTFivebrane. The kernel of this map consists of anomalies that are trivialised by the fivebrane structure. Since p₂/6 is a ℤ‑valued characteristic class in degree 8, its vanishing does not directly affect the 12‑dimensional Stiefel–Whitney numbers. However, the fivebrane structure implies that the manifold is also a string manifold (p₁/2 = 0), which forces certain Stiefel–Whitney numbers to vanish. In particular, on a string manifold w₂ = 0 (since w₂ is the mod 2 reduction of p₁/2). Therefore w₂⁶ = 0, w₄ w₂⁴ = 0, and w₆ w₂³ = 0 automatically. The three ℤ₂ anomalies are thus trivialised by the fivebrane structure. The remaining anomaly is the ℤ component (signature), which is not affected by the fivebrane condition. Hence the anomaly of Z as a fivebrane‑structured theory is the element (1, 0, 0, 0) ∈ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ≅ π₀(Iℤ MTFivebrane).
Exam Strategy: Dimensional Ladder and Obstruction Classes
When a question asks about the anomaly of a TQFT with a higher tangential structure (String, Fivebrane, Ninebrane), first identify the obstruction class that defines the structure (p₁/2 for String, p₂/6 for Fivebrane). Then determine which lower‑dimensional characteristic classes vanish as a consequence (e.g., w₂ = 0 for String). Use the Anderson dual of the relevant Madsen–Tillmann spectrum to read off the anomaly group. Remember that each step up the Whitehead tower kills one more Pontryagin class and imposes more vanishing Stiefel–Whitney numbers, thereby trivialising more ℤ₂ anomaly summands.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Six Dimensions

The braid group B_n admits higher categorical representations in any braided monoidal (∞,k)‑category. For a six‑dimensional extended TQFT, the relevant algebraic structure is a braided fusion 4‑category ℬ. The braid group B_n acts on the n‑fold tensor power of any object in ℬ via the braiding isomorphisms and their higher coherences. This action lifts to a functor B_n → Autℬ(X⊗n), where Autℬ(X⊗n) is the 4‑groupoid of automorphisms of X⊗n in ℬ. The higher categorical representation theory of B_n in this setting is governed by the E₂‑algebra structure on the Drinfeld centre Z(ℬ), which is a braided fusion 3‑category with sylleptic structure.

Concretely, a six‑dimensional extended TQFT Z : Bord₆ᴳ → 6Vect constructed from a fusion 4‑category ℱ assigns to a point the 4‑category ℱ, to a circle the braided fusion 3‑category Z(ℱ), to a 2‑sphere the sylleptic fusion 2‑category Z₂(ℱ), to a 3‑sphere the symmetric fusion 1‑category Z₃(ℱ), to a 4‑sphere the symmetric fusion 0‑category Z₄(ℱ) (a finite abelian group with quadratic form), and to a 5‑sphere an E₅‑algebra in 6Vect. The braid group B_n appears naturally as the mapping class group of the n‑punctured disk D² {p₁,…,p_n}, which is a 2‑dimensional cobordism with corners. In the fully extended theory, this cobordism is evaluated by factorization homology of the E₂‑algebra Z(ℱ) over the punctured disk, yielding a 4‑category equivalent to the n‑fold tensor product of Z(ℱ) with itself. The action of B_n on this 4‑category is the higher categorical braid group representation.

When ℱ is the 4‑category of representations of a quantum group U_q(𝔤) at a root of unity, the braided fusion 3‑category Z(ℱ) is equivalent to the 3‑category of modules over the quantum double D(U_q(𝔤)). The braid group representations obtained in this way are the higher analogues of the Jones representations. The images of these representations are finite 4‑groups, and their invariants can be used to construct 6‑manifold invariants via the Crane–Yetter state sum. The partition function on a closed 6‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 6‑j symbols of ℱ — associators, braidings, syllepses, and the 4‑dimensional coherence data. The anomaly of the theory is captured by the Anderson dual of MTG; for G = Spin, π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ in dimension 6, where the ℤ summand is the signature and the ℤ₂ summand is detected by w₂³.

The Classification of Gapped Boundaries in Seven‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs via Higher Categorical Centers

A seven‑dimensional fully extended TQFT Z : Bord₇ᴳ → 7Vect constructed from a fusion 5‑category ℱ assigns to a point the 5‑category ℱ. Gapped boundary conditions for Z correspond to left module 5‑categories over ℱ. The collection of all such module 5‑categories forms a 6‑category ℱ‑Mod, which is symmetric monoidal under relative tensor product. The condensation completion of ℱ — the 6‑category obtained by freely adjoining condensation defects — is equivalent to ℱ‑Mod. The gapped boundaries are therefore classified by the objects of ℱ‑Mod, i.e. by left module 5‑categories over ℱ.

The higher categorical centres of ℱ provide a systematic way to construct and classify these module 5‑categories. The Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ) is a braided fusion 4‑category. The 2‑fold centre Z₂(ℱ) is a sylleptic fusion 3‑category. The 3‑fold centre Z₃(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 2‑category. The 4‑fold centre Z₄(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 1‑category. The 5‑fold centre Z₅(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category — a finite abelian group with a quadratic form. Each centre Zₖ(ℱ) carries an action of the Ek+1‑operad, and the forgetful maps Zk+1(ℱ) → Z_k(ℱ) are Ek+1‑monoidal.

A gapped boundary in seven dimensions is specified by a Lagrangian algebra in the braided fusion 4‑category Z(ℱ) — an E₂‑algebra A ∈ Z(ℱ) such that the category of A‑modules in Z(ℱ) is equivalent to Vect as a braided fusion 4‑category. The classification of such Lagrangian algebras is equivalent to the classification of maximal isotropic subcategories of Z(ℱ) with respect to the braiding. For the Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G (where ℱ = 5Vect^G), the gapped boundaries are classified by subgroups H ⊂ G up to conjugation, together with a cohomology class in H⁴(H; U(1)). The higher categorical centres reproduce the higher group cohomology: Zₖ(5Vect^G) ≃ 5VectΛ^k G where Λ^k G is the k‑fold loop group. For a general fusion 5‑category, the centres are described by the higher representation theory of ℱ, and the Lagrangian algebras in Z(ℱ) correspond to indecomposable module 5‑categories over ℱ.

Higher Categorical Centers and the Classification of Topological Orders in Nine‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs

A nine‑dimensional fully extended TQFT Z : Bord₉ᴳ → 9Vect constructed from a fusion 7‑category ℱ assigns to a point the 7‑category ℱ. The topological orders in nine dimensions — i.e. the phases of matter described by such TQFTs — are classified by the Morita equivalence classes of fusion 7‑categories. Two fusion 7‑categories ℱ and 𝒢 are Morita equivalent if their 8‑categories of module 7‑categories ℱ‑Mod and 𝒢‑Mod are equivalent as symmetric monoidal 8‑categories. The higher categorical centres provide a complete set of Morita invariants: the k‑fold centre Zₖ(ℱ) is an Eₖ‑fusion (7−k)‑category, and the sequence of centres (Zₖ(ℱ))k=17 determines ℱ up to Morita equivalence.

The 7‑fold centre Z₇(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category — a finite abelian group A equipped with a quadratic form q : A → U(1). This quadratic form is the higher analogue of the modular data of a modular tensor category. The 8‑fold centre Z₈(ℱ) is an E₈‑algebra in 9Vect, and the 9‑fold centre Z₉(ℱ) is a commutative algebra in 9Vect whose underlying object is the ground field k. The partition function on a closed 9‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose data are the 9‑j symbols of ℱ. When ℱ is the 7‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is related to the topological sector of a hypothetical 9‑dimensional theory.

The anomaly of a nine‑dimensional TQFT with Spin structure is classified by π₀(Iℤ MTSpin) ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ in dimension 9, detected by the Stiefel–Whitney numbers w₂⁴ w₁ and w₄ w₂² w₁. For a fivebrane structure, the anomaly group is π₀(Iℤ MTFivebrane) ≅ ℤ₂ in dimension 9, detected by w₄ w₂² w₁ (since w₂ = 0 on a fivebrane manifold). The higher categorical centres encode the anomaly via the Eₖ‑algebra structures: the anomaly is the obstruction to extending the E₉‑algebra Z₉(ℱ) to an E₁₀‑algebra, which is measured by a class in the André–Quillen cohomology of the E₉‑operad with coefficients in the units of Z₉(ℱ).

  • Confusing the categorical level of the Drinfeld centre in a 13‑dimensional TQFT: the centre of a braided fusion 10‑category is a braided fusion 9‑category, not a 10‑category.
  • Assuming that a fivebrane structure implies a string structure automatically; while true, the converse is false — a string structure does not guarantee a fivebrane structure because p₂/6 may be non‑zero.
  • Treating the higher categorical centre Z₁₁(ℱ) in a 13‑dimensional theory as an ordinary Drinfeld centre; it is an E₁₁‑centre living one categorical level higher than the symmetric fusion 0‑category Z₁₀(ℱ).
  • Forgetting that the condensation completion of a fusion n‑category is an (n+1)‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 7‑D is an 8‑category, not a 7‑category.
  • Misidentifying the fusion 7‑category input for a 9‑dimensional TQFT as a fusion 8‑category; the input is a 7‑category because the theory is 9‑dimensional and the point gets a 7‑category.
  • Overlooking that the braid group representation in 6‑D comes from the E₂‑algebra structure on Z(ℱ) (a braided fusion 3‑category), not from the braiding on ℱ itself (which is only a monoidal 4‑category).
  • Assuming the partition function on ℝP⁹ for a 9‑D Crane–Yetter‑type model is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the twist w₁ ∪ w₂ ∪ w₃ ∪ w₄ is non‑trivial.

Extended TQFTs and the Classification of Topological Phases via Higher Categorical K‑Theory

The Freed–Hopkins theorem identifies the group of deformation classes of invertible topological phases in dimension d with the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum MTSO(d) or its spin, pin, or string variants.
