Higher categorical K‑theory provides a computational bridge: the K-theory of the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category of fully dualizable objects in nVect recovers the same groups. Concretely, for a fully extended TQFT Z : BordnG → nVect the value on a point is a fully dualizable object X ∈ nVect. The higher categorical K‑theory spectrum K(nVectfd) carries a natural E∞-structure, and its homotopy groups πk classify k-dimensional invertible phases with G-structure. In low dimensions this reproduces the known periodic table: π0 ≅ ℤ (the integer quantum Hall effect), π1 ≅ ℤ2 (the topological insulator in class AII), etc. The mechanism is that the cobordism hypothesis equips BordnG with a universal property as the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals on a single object equipped with a G-structure. Mapping this universal object into nVect yields a functor whose homotopy class is exactly an element of the Anderson dual. Higher categorical K‑theory thus serves as a concrete algebraic model for the otherwise homotopy‑theoretic classification, making it accessible to explicit computation via higher Morita theory and the theory of En-algebras.

An important exception occurs for non‑invertible phases. The K‑theory of the (∞,n)-category of all (not necessarily fully dualizable) objects captures only the invertible summand; the non‑invertible sectors require the full higher categorical centre construction described in later sections. Examiners often test the distinction by asking whether the K‑theory of nVect classifies all topological orders — the correct answer is that it classifies only the invertible ones.

Factorization Homology and the Computation of Higher Traces for Non‑Semisimple Fusion Categories

Factorization homology ∫M A of an En-algebra A on a framed n-manifold M was originally developed for semisimple En-algebras (e.g. modular tensor categories). The extension to non‑semisimple fusion categories uses the derived (∞,1)-category of modules over the fusion category, which carries a natural En-monoidal structure after Drinfeld centre completion. The higher trace (or higher categorical dimension) of an object X in a non‑semisimple fusion n-category is computed as the factorization homology of the corresponding En-algebra on the n-sphere with a marked point labelled by X. This yields a value in the ground field that coincides with the quantum dimension when the category is semisimple, but acquires corrections from the projective covers of simple objects in the non‑semisimple case. The computation proceeds by a cell decomposition of M and a state‑sum where each cell contributes a tensor from the En-algebra; the non‑semisimplicity manifests as non‑trivial higher Ext‑groups that appear as additional summation indices. A landmark result (Ben‑Zvi–Francis–Nadler, 2015) shows that for a finite tensor category C the factorization homology on a closed 3‑manifold recovers the Turaev–Viro invariant built from the Drinfeld centre Z(C), while on manifolds with boundary it computes the Hochschild homology of the associated module categories. This provides a uniform framework for both closed and open TQFTs and explains why the state‑sum formulas for non‑semisimple theories involve modified 6j-symbols satisfying pentagon equations up to coherent homotopy.

Worked Example: Higher Trace on a Non‑Semisimple Fusion Category
Let C be the representation category of the small quantum group uq(𝔰𝔩2) at a root of unity, a non‑semisimple fusion category. Compute the higher trace of the projective cover P of the trivial simple object on the 3‑sphere using factorization homology.
The E3-algebra associated to C is the Drinfeld centre Z(C), a braided fusion category. Factorization homology on S3 with a marked point labelled by P reduces to the categorical dimension of P in Z(C). Because P has Loewy length two, its dimension equals the quantum dimension of the trivial object (which is 1) plus the contribution from the radical, which is measured by the modified trace on the projective ideal. The modified trace t satisfies t(idP) = 0, so the higher trace of P on S3 vanishes. This matches the known result that the Turaev–Viro invariant of S3 for uq(𝔰𝔩2) is zero.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a partition function on a closed manifold in a non‑semisimple theory, first identify the relevant En-algebra (usually a Drinfeld centre). Then recall that factorization homology on a sphere computes the higher trace, which often vanishes for projective covers because the modified trace is zero on the identity. This shortcut avoids a full state‑sum expansion.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Six Dimensions

The Baez–Dolan stabilization hypothesis predicts that a fully extended n-dimensional TQFT is determined by a fusion (n−2)-category. For n=6 the input is a fusion 4‑category ℱ. The braid group relevant in six dimensions is the mapping class group of a punctured 5‑sphere, which is isomorphic to the spherical braid group Bk(S5). Representations of this group arise from the E2-algebra structure on the Drinfeld centre Z(ℱ), a braided fusion 3‑category. Concretely, the half‑braiding in Z(ℱ) yields a functor Bk(S5) → AutZ(ℱ)(X⊗k) for any object X ∈ Z(ℱ). The Douglas–Reutter construction (2018) builds a 6‑dimensional extended TQFT Zℱ : Bord6 → 6Vect whose value on a point is ℱ. The braid group representation appears when evaluating Zℱ on a 6‑manifold containing a codimension‑2 defect labelled by X; the monodromy of the defect around a link of spheres implements the braid group action. This mechanism generalises the 3‑dimensional case where the braid group acts on the space of conformal blocks of a modular tensor category. In six dimensions the higher categorical nature of Z(ℱ) means the representation is not merely linear but takes values in the 3‑category of module 2‑categories over Z(ℱ), and the coherence data are governed by the E2-operad. A key computational tool is the higher categorical trace on Z(ℱ), which reduces the braid group representation to a scalar invariant on closed 6‑manifolds (the partition function). For the Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G (where ℱ = 4VectG) the braid group representation factors through the group cohomology H4(G;U(1)) and reproduces the known 6‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten invariants.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Ten‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 8‑Categories

Applying the cobordism hypothesis in dimension ten requires a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,10)-category 10Vect. The Baez–Dolan hypothesis identifies such objects with fusion 8‑categories. A fusion 8‑category ℱ is a semisimple monoidal 8‑category with simple unit and finite‑dimensional Hom‑spaces. The fully extended TQFT Zℱ : Bord10 → 10Vect assigns to a point the 8‑category ℱ, to a 1‑manifold the 7‑category of ℱ-module 7‑categories, and so on, down to the 0‑category of vector spaces assigned to a closed 10‑manifold. The construction proceeds by first forming the (∞,10)-category of higher vector spaces 10Vect as the E∞-algebra of chain complexes of 9‑categories, then exhibiting ℱ as a fully dualizable E10-algebra via its iterated Drinfeld centres. The k-fold centre Zk(ℱ) is an Ek-fusion (8−k)-category; in particular Z8(ℱ) is an E8-fusion 0‑category, i.e. a finite abelian group with a quadratic form, and Z9(ℱ) is an E9-algebra in 10Vect. The partition function on a closed 10‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose local weights are the 10‑j symbols of ℱ, generalising the Crane–Yetter 4‑dimensional state sum. When ℱ is the 8‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of a hypothetical 10‑dimensional M‑theory compactification. Anomaly cancellation in ten dimensions is governed by the Anderson dual of MTSpin(10) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ2, detected by the Pontryagin numbers p12 and p2. The higher categorical centres encode the anomaly: the obstruction to extending the E10-algebra Z10(ℱ) to an E11-algebra lives in André–Quillen cohomology of the E10-operad with coefficients in the units of Z10(ℱ).

Higher Categorical Centers and the Classification of Topological Orders in Ten‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs

For a fully extended 10‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord10 → 10Vect built from a fusion 8‑category ℱ, the sequence of higher categorical centres Zk(ℱ) for k=1,…,10 provides a complete set of Morita invariants. Z1(ℱ) is the Drinfeld centre, a braided fusion 7‑category; Z2(ℱ) is an E2-fusion 6‑category, and so on. The 8‑fold centre Z8(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category — a finite abelian group A equipped with a quadratic form q : A → U(1). This quadratic form is the higher analogue of the modular data of a modular tensor category and determines the braiding on Z7(ℱ). The 9‑fold centre Z9(ℱ) is an E9-algebra in 10Vect, and the 10‑fold centre Z10(ℱ) is a commutative algebra in 10Vect whose underlying object is the ground field k. Two fusion 8‑categories ℱ and 𝒢 are Morita equivalent iff their 9‑categories of module 8‑categories are equivalent as symmetric monoidal 9‑categories; this is equivalent to the existence of an equivalence of the towers of centres (Zk(ℱ)) ≃ (Zk(𝒢)) as Ek-fusion categories for all k. For Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G (where ℱ = 8VectG) the centres are Zk(8VectG) ≃ 8VectΛkG where ΛkG is the k-fold loop group. The quadratic form on Z8(ℱ) is then the higher cup‑product pairing on H4(G;U(1)). Gapped boundaries in ten dimensions correspond to Lagrangian algebras in the braided fusion 7‑category Z1(ℱ), i.e. E2-algebras A ∈ Z1(ℱ) such that the category of A-modules is equivalent to 9Vect. Their classification reduces to maximal isotropic subcategories of Z1(ℱ) with respect to the braiding, generalising the Lagrangian algebra classification in three dimensions.

  • Confusing the categorical level of the Drinfeld centre in a 10‑dimensional TQFT: the centre of a braided fusion 7‑category is a braided fusion 6‑category, not a 7‑category.
  • Assuming that a fivebrane structure implies a string structure automatically; while true, the converse is false — a string structure does not guarantee a fivebrane structure because p2/6 may be non‑zero.
  • Treating the higher categorical centre Z9(ℱ) in a 10‑dimensional theory as an ordinary Drinfeld centre; it is an E9-centre living one categorical level higher than the symmetric fusion 0‑category Z8(ℱ).
  • Forgetting that the condensation completion of a fusion 8‑category is a 9‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 10‑D is a 9‑category, not an 8‑category.
  • Misidentifying the fusion 8‑category input for a 10‑dimensional TQFT as a fusion 9‑category; the input is an 8‑category because the theory is 10‑dimensional and the point gets an 8‑category.
  • Overlooking that the braid group representation in 6‑D comes from the E2-algebra structure on Z(ℱ) (a braided fusion 3‑category), not from the braiding on ℱ itself (which is only a monoidal 4‑category).
  • Assuming the partition function on ℝP10 for a 10‑D Crane–Yetter‑type model is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the twist w1 ∪ w2 ∪ w3 ∪ w4 is non‑trivial.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Higher Tangential Structures and the Classification of Anomalous Phases in Dimension Eleven

The cobordism hypothesis extends naturally to manifolds equipped with a tangential structure given by a map to a classifying space BG for a topological group G. In eleven dimensions the relevant structures are lifts of the stable normal bundle through the sequence BO ← BSpin ← BString ← BFivebrane ← BNinebrane. A Ninebrane structure trivialises the fractional Pontryagin class p₃/240, which is the next obstruction after the fivebrane class p₂/6. The (∞,11)-category Bord₁₁Ninebrane of eleven‑dimensional cobordisms with Ninebrane structure is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,11)-category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object. By the cobordism hypothesis, symmetric monoidal functors Z : Bord₁₁Ninebrane → C are classified by fully dualizable objects of C equipped with a homotopy fixed point for the O(11)-action twisted by the Ninebrane map. When C = 11Vect, the (∞,11)-category of higher vector spaces, the classification reduces to the choice of a fusion 9‑category ℱ whose iterated Drinfeld centres satisfy the required E₁₁‑algebra structure. The anomaly of such a theory is captured by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTNinebrane(11). Computations using the Adams spectral sequence show that MTNinebrane(11) has homotopy groups ℤ in degree 0, ℤ₂ in degree 4 (detected by p₁²), ℤ₂ in degree 8 (detected by p₂), and a ℤ summand in degree 12 (detected by p₃). The invertible phases are therefore classified by a combination of Pontryagin numbers and a secondary invariant valued in ℤ₂. This matches the expectation from M‑theory that the eleven‑dimensional topological sector is governed by the C‑field flux quantisation condition G₄ = p₁/2 + λ with λ a degree‑4 integral class.

Concretely, a fully extended eleven‑dimensional TQFT assigns to a point a fusion 9‑category ℱ, to a 1‑manifold the 10‑category of ℱ-module 9‑categories, and so on down to the 0‑category of vector spaces assigned to a closed 11‑manifold. The partition function on a closed 11‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose local weights are the 11‑j symbols of ℱ, generalising the Crane–Yetter 4‑dimensional and the 10‑dimensional state sums. When ℱ is the 9‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of a hypothetical eleven‑dimensional M‑theory compactification. Anomaly cancellation in eleven dimensions is governed by the Anderson dual of MTNinebrane(11) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ, detected by the Pontryagin numbers p₁², p₂, and p₃. The higher categorical centres encode the anomaly: the obstruction to extending the E₁₁‑algebra Z₁₁(ℱ) to an E₁₂‑algebra lives in André–Quillen cohomology of the E₁₁‑operad with coefficients in the units of Z₁₁(ℱ).

Extended TQFTs from Fusion 8‑Categories and the Construction of Eleven‑Dimensional State Sum Models

A fully extended eleven‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord₁₁ → 11Vect is determined by a fusion 8‑category ℱ that is fully dualizable as an E₁₁‑algebra in 11Vect. The construction proceeds by first forming the (∞,11)-category of higher vector spaces 11Vect as the E∞‑algebra of chain complexes of 10‑categories, then exhibiting ℱ as a fully dualizable E₁₁‑algebra via its iterated Drinfeld centres. The k-fold centre Zk(ℱ) is an Ek-fusion (9−k)-category; in particular Z₉(ℱ) is an E₉‑fusion 0‑category, i.e. a finite abelian group with a quadratic form, and Z₁₀(ℱ) is an E₁₀‑algebra in 11Vect. The partition function on a closed 11‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose local weights are the 11‑j symbols of ℱ, generalising the Crane–Yetter 4‑dimensional state sum and the 10‑dimensional model described earlier. When ℱ is the 8‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of a hypothetical 11‑dimensional M‑theory compactification. Anomaly cancellation in eleven dimensions is governed by the Anderson dual of MTNinebrane(11) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ, detected by the Pontryagin numbers p₁², p₂, and p₃. The higher categorical centres encode the anomaly: the obstruction to extending the E₁₁‑algebra Z₁₁(ℱ) to an E₁₂‑algebra lives in André–Quillen cohomology of the E₁₁‑operad with coefficients in the units of Z₁₁(ℱ).

The state‑sum construction uses a triangulation of M and assigns to each 11‑simplex a weight given by the 11‑j symbol of ℱ, which is a morphism in the 0‑category Z₁₀(ℱ) ≃ k. The product over all 11‑simplices, summed over labellings of lower‑dimensional simplices by simple objects of the appropriate fusion categories, yields the partition function Z(M). This construction is invariant under Pachner moves because the 11‑j symbols satisfy the higher pentagon and hexagon equations that are precisely the coherence conditions for an E₁₁‑algebra. For Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G (where ℱ = 8VectG) the centres are Zk(8VectG) ≃ 8VectΛkG where ΛkG is the k-fold loop group. The quadratic form on Z₉(ℱ) is then the higher cup‑product pairing on H⁵(G;U(1)). Gapped boundaries in eleven dimensions correspond to Lagrangian algebras in the braided fusion 8‑category Z₁(ℱ), i.e. E₂‑algebras A ∈ Z₁(ℱ) such that the category of A-modules is equivalent to 10Vect. Their classification reduces to maximal isotropic subcategories of Z₁(ℱ) with respect to the braiding, generalising the Lagrangian algebra classification in three and ten dimensions.

Worked Example: Computing the 11‑j Symbol for a Simple Fusion 8‑Category
Let ℱ = 8Vectℤ₂ be the fusion 8‑category of ℤ₂‑graded 8‑vector spaces. Compute the 11‑j symbol associated to the unique non‑trivial simple object and verify that the state‑sum on the 11‑sphere S¹¹ yields the partition function Z(S¹¹) = 1.
In 8Vectℤ₂ there are two simple objects: the trivial object 𝟙 (grade 0) and the non‑trivial object σ (grade 1). The fusion rule is σ ⊗ σ ≅ 𝟙. The Drinfeld centre Z₁(ℱ) is the braided fusion 7‑category 7Vectℤ₂ with the symmetric braiding given by the quadratic form q(1) = −1. Iterating the centre nine times yields Z₉(ℱ) ≃ 0Vectℤ₂ equipped with the quadratic form q⁹(1) = (−1)⁹ = −1. The 10‑fold centre Z₁₀(ℱ) is the commutative algebra k in 11Vect. The 11‑j symbol is the evaluation of the unique 11‑valent vertex labelled by σ on the boundary of an 11‑simplex. Because the fusion rule forces an even number of σ labels on any closed diagram, the only admissible labelling of the 11‑simplex boundary has all edges labelled by 𝟙. Hence the 11‑j symbol equals the identity morphism id𝟙 in k. The state‑sum on S¹¹ triangulated by two 11‑simplices glued along their boundary therefore gives a product of two identity morphisms, i.e. 1 ∈ k. Thus Z(S¹¹) = 1, as required for a unitary TQFT.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the categorical level of the Drinfeld centre in an n-dimensional fully extended TQFT, remember the pattern: the input is a fusion (n−3)-category, its first centre is a braided fusion (n−4)-category, and the k-th centre is an Ek-fusion (n−3−k)-category. Write down the sequence for the given dimension before attempting any computation; this avoids the common off‑by‑one error.

Higher Categorical Centers and the Classification of Topological Orders in Eleven‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs

For a fully extended eleven‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord₁₁ → 11Vect built from a fusion 8‑category ℱ, the sequence of higher categorical centres Zk(ℱ) for k = 1,…,11 provides a complete set of Morita invariants. Z₁(ℱ) is the Drinfeld centre, a braided fusion 7‑category; Z₂(ℱ) is an E₂‑fusion 6‑category, and so on. The 9‑fold centre Z₉(ℱ) is a symmetric fusion 0‑category — a finite abelian group A equipped with a quadratic form q : A → U(1). This quadratic form is the higher analogue of the modular data of a modular tensor category and determines the braiding on Z₈(ℱ). The 10‑fold centre Z₁₀(ℱ) is an E₁₀‑algebra in 11Vect, and the 11‑fold centre Z₁₁(ℱ) is a commutative algebra in 11Vect whose underlying object is the ground field k. Two fusion 8‑categories ℱ and 𝒢 are Morita equivalent iff their 10‑categories of module 9‑categories are equivalent as symmetric monoidal 10‑categories; this is equivalent to the existence of an equivalence of the towers of centres (Zk(ℱ)) ≃ (Zk(𝒢)) as Ek-fusion categories for all k. For Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G (where ℱ = 8VectG) the centres are Zk(8VectG) ≃ 8VectΛkG where ΛkG is the k-fold loop group. The quadratic form on Z₉(ℱ) is then the higher cup‑product pairing on H⁵(G;U(1)). Gapped boundaries in eleven dimensions correspond to Lagrangian algebras in the braided fusion 7‑category Z₁(ℱ), i.e. E₂‑algebras A ∈ Z₁(ℱ) such that the category of A-modules is equivalent to 10Vect. Their classification reduces to maximal isotropic subcategories of Z₁(ℱ) with respect to the braiding, generalising the Lagrangian algebra classification in three and ten dimensions.

The condensation completion of a fusion 8‑category is a 9‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 11‑D is a 9‑category, not an 8‑category. This shift in categorical level is a frequent source of error. Moreover, the braid group representation that appears in the 7‑dimensional boundary theory comes from the E₂‑algebra structure on Z₁(ℱ) (a braided fusion 7‑category), not from any braiding on ℱ itself (which is only a monoidal 8‑category). The partition function on a non‑orientable 11‑manifold such as ℝℙ¹¹ for a Crane–Yetter‑type model vanishes when the Stiefel‑Whitney class combination w₁ ∪ w₂ ∪ w₃ ∪ w₄ ∪ w₅ is non‑trivial, reflecting the fact that the E₁₁‑algebra structure does not extend to an O(11)-equivariant one without a Ninebrane structure.

Factorization Homology on Manifolds with Corners and the Computation of Higher Traces for Manifolds with Non‑Trivial π₂

Factorization homology ∫M A of an En-algebra A on a manifold M with corners computes the value of the associated fully extended TQFT on M. When M has non‑trivial second homotopy group π₂(M) ≠ 0, the standard excision formula must be supplemented by a higher‑categorical trace that records the action of π₂ on the En-algebra. Concretely, for an E₅‑algebra A in 5Vect (the input for a 5‑dimensional TQFT), the factorization homology on a 5‑manifold M with corners decomposes as a colimit over the exit path ∞‑category of M. The higher trace Trπ₂(A) is defined as the homotopy fixed points of the π₂‑action on the E₅‑centre Z₅(A). In the case where A = Z(ℱ) for a fusion 2‑category ℱ, Z₅(A) is a symmetric fusion (−1)-category, i.e. a finite abelian group with a quadratic form. The π₂‑action factors through the Postnikov invariant k ∈ H³(π₁(M); π₂(M)), and the resulting trace computes the partition function on M as a sum over flat π₁‑connections weighted by the quadratic form evaluated on the obstruction class in H²(M; π₂(M)). This formalism reproduces the known state‑sum invariants for 5‑manifolds with π₂ ≠ 0, such as S² × S³, and matches the prediction of the cobordism hypothesis with singularities where the singular strata are labelled by the π₂‑action. The same pattern persists in higher dimensions: for an E₁₁‑algebra A in 11Vect, the higher trace Trπ₂(A) lives in the 11‑fold centre Z₁₁(A) ≃ k and is computed by the André–Quillen cohomology of the E₁₁‑operad with coefficients in the π₂‑module structure on the units of A.

Practically, to compute the partition function on a manifold M with corners and π₂(M) ≠ 0, one first chooses a handle decomposition compatible with the corner stratification. The factorization homology is then the iterated coend over the handles, where each 2‑handle contributes a factor of the higher trace Trπ₂(A). The result is independent of the handle decomposition because the higher trace satisfies the cyclic invariance property that follows from the En-algebra structure. This method has been used to compute the partition function of the 5‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten theory on S² × Σ³ for a surface Σ³ with non‑trivial π₁, yielding a formula involving the Verlinde‑type sum over representations of the fundamental group twisted by the π₂‑action.

The Use of Higher Categories in the Construction of Twelve‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs from Fusion 9‑Categories

A fully extended twelve‑dimensional TQFT Z : Bord₁₂ → 12Vect is determined by a fusion 9‑category ℱ that is fully dualizable as an E₁₂‑algebra in 12Vect. The (∞,12)-category 12Vect is the E∞‑algebra of chain complexes of 11‑categories. The iterated Drinfeld centres Zk(ℱ) form a tower of Ek-fusion (10−k)-categories; in particular Z₁₀(ℱ) is an E₁₀‑fusion 0‑category (a finite abelian group with a quadratic form), Z₁₁(ℱ) is an E₁₁‑algebra in 12Vect, and Z₁₂(ℱ) is a commutative algebra whose underlying object is the ground field k. The partition function on a closed 12‑manifold M is computed by a state‑sum model whose local weights are the 12‑j symbols of ℱ, generalising the 11‑dimensional construction. When ℱ is the 9‑category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, the resulting TQFT is expected to capture the topological sector of a hypothetical twelve‑dimensional F‑theory compactification. Anomaly cancellation in twelve dimensions is governed by the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTTwelvebrane(12), where a Twelvebrane structure trivialises the fractional Pontryagin class p₄/1440. The homotopy groups of MTTwelvebrane(12) include ℤ in degree 0, ℤ₂ in degrees 4, 8, 12 (detected by p₁², p₂, p₃) and a ℤ summand in degree 16 (detected by p₄). The invertible phases are therefore classified by a combination of Pontryagin numbers up to p₄ and secondary ℤ₂ invariants. The higher categorical centres encode the anomaly: the obstruction to extending the E₁₂‑algebra Z₁₂(ℱ) to an E₁₃‑algebra lives in André–Quillen cohomology of the E₁₂‑operad with coefficients in the units of Z₁₂(ℱ).

Gapped boundaries in twelve dimensions correspond to Lagrangian algebras in the braided fusion 8‑category Z₁(ℱ), i.e. E₂‑algebras A ∈ Z₁(ℱ) such that the category of A-modules is equivalent to 11Vect. Their classification reduces to maximal isotropic subcategories of Z₁(ℱ) with respect to the braiding. The condensation completion of a fusion 9‑category is a 10‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 12‑D is a 10‑category. A common pitfall is to confuse the categorical level of the Drinfeld centre: the centre of a braided fusion 8‑category is a braided fusion 7‑category, not an 8‑category. Another subtlety is that a Twelvebrane structure implies a Ninebrane structure, but the converse fails because p₄/1440 may be non‑zero even when p₃/240 vanishes. The state‑sum on a non‑orientable 12‑manifold such as ℝℙ¹² vanishes when the Stiefel‑Whitney class combination w₁ ∪ w₂ ∪ w₃ ∪ w₄ ∪ w₅ ∪ w₆ is non‑trivial, reflecting the need for a Twelvebrane structure to define the theory on unoriented manifolds.

  • Confusing the categorical level of the Drinfeld centre in an 11‑dimensional TQFT: the centre of a braided fusion 7‑category is a braided fusion 6‑category, not a 7‑category.
  • Assuming that a Ninebrane structure implies a Twelvebrane structure automatically; while a Twelvebrane structure implies a Ninebrane structure, the converse is false because p₄/1440 may be non‑zero.
  • Treating the higher categorical centre Z₁₀(ℱ) in an 11‑dimensional theory as an ordinary Drinfeld centre; it is an E₁₀‑centre living one categorical level higher than the symmetric fusion 0‑category Z₉(ℱ).
  • Forgetting that the condensation completion of a fusion 8‑category is a 9‑category, so the category of gapped boundaries in 11‑D is a 9‑category, not an 8‑category.
  • Misidentifying the fusion 8‑category input for an 11‑dimensional TQFT as a fusion 9‑category; the input is an 8‑category because the theory is 11‑dimensional and the point gets an 8‑category.
  • Overlooking that the braid group representation in 7‑D comes from the E₂‑algebra structure on Z₁(ℱ) (a braided fusion 7‑category), not from the braiding on ℱ itself (which is only a monoidal 8‑category).
  • Assuming the partition function on ℝℙ¹¹ for an 11‑D Crane–Yetter‑type model is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the twist w₁ ∪ w₂ ∪ w₃ ∪ w₄ ∪ w₅ is non‑trivial.

The Cobordism Hypothesis and the Classification of Topological Phases via Cobordism Groups of Manifolds with Singularities

The cobordism hypothesis, proved by Lurie, asserts that a fully extended topological quantum field theory of dimension n is uniquely determined by a fully dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category. When the target category is the (∞,n)-category of higher vector spaces, the classification of invertible topological phases reduces to the computation of the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG associated to a tangential structure G. Manifolds with singularities enter naturally because the cobordism category BordnG can be enlarged to include stratified spaces where singular strata carry lower‑dimensional topological data. The resulting cobordism groups ΩdG,sing classify deformation classes of d-dimensional invertible phases with prescribed singularities. For example, the group Ω4Spin,sing captures four‑dimensional fermionic phases with codimension‑two defects, and its computation involves the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem on manifolds with boundary. The Freed–Hopkins theorem identifies the group of invertible phases with the homotopy groups of the Anderson dual IℤMTG, and the inclusion of singular strata corresponds to a map of spectra MTG → MTGsing whose effect on homotopy groups can be analysed via the Adams spectral sequence. In practice, the classification of topological orders with non‑invertible symmetries requires the higher categorical analogue: the (∞,n)-category of cobordisms with singularities is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by a single object equipped with a chosen singularity datum. This universal property translates the physical problem of classifying gapped boundaries and domain walls into a purely algebraic problem of computing higher Morita categories of fusion n-categories. Recent work shows that the condensation completion of a fusion n-category yields an (n+1)-category whose objects are gapped boundaries, and that the cobordism hypothesis with singularities predicts a bijection between such boundaries and Lagrangian algebras in the appropriate higher Drinfeld centre. The upshot is that cobordism groups of manifolds with singularities provide the invariant that distinguishes distinct topological phases even when their bulk invariants coincide.

Higher Categorical Structures in the Classification of Anomalies in Quantum Field Theory

Anomalies in quantum field theory are obstructions to gauging a global symmetry, and they are classified by invertible field theories one dimension higher. In the language of extended TQFT, an anomaly for a d-dimensional theory with symmetry group G corresponds to a fully extended (d+1)-dimensional invertible TQFT equipped with a G-structure. The cobordism hypothesis identifies such theories with fully dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal (∞,d+1)-category of higher vector spaces that carry an action of the Ed+1-operad. The higher categorical structure enters at two levels: first, the symmetry G itself may be a higher group (a 2-group, 3-group, etc.), and second, the anomaly theory is an Ed+1-algebra in (d+1)Vect. The classification of anomalies therefore reduces to the computation of the Picard ∞-groupoid of the Ed+1-algebra of higher vector spaces, which is equivalent to the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG for the appropriate tangential structure. For a finite group G, the anomaly group in dimension d is Hd+1(BG;U(1)), recovered as the degree‑d+1 homotopy group of IℤMTG. When G is a higher group, the classifying space is replaced by a higher classifying space, and the anomaly group becomes a higher cohomology group with coefficients in a spectrum of units. The higher categorical perspective also clarifies anomaly inflow: a d-dimensional theory with anomaly α can be realised as the boundary of a (d+1)-dimensional invertible theory whose partition function on a closed manifold M is exp(2πi∫M α). In the extended setting, the bulk theory is a fully extended TQFT whose value on a point is a fully dualizable Ed+1-algebra, and the boundary condition is a module over this algebra. The higher Morita category of such modules encodes the possible boundary theories, and the condensation completion of the bulk algebra yields the category of gapped boundaries. This framework has been used to classify anomalies of six‑dimensional (2,0) theories, where the relevant higher group is the String 2‑group and the anomaly is captured by a class in H8(BString;ℤ). The same machinery applies to non‑invertible symmetries, where the symmetry category is a fusion n-category and the anomaly lives in the Brauer‑Picard ∞-groupoid of that fusion n-category.

Worked Example: Anomaly Classification for a 3‑D Theory with ℤ₂ 1‑Form Symmetry
Consider a three‑dimensional topological quantum field theory with a ℤ₂ 1‑form symmetry. The symmetry is described by the 2‑group Bℤ₂. Determine the group of ’t Hooft anomalies for this theory using the cobordism hypothesis.
A 1‑form symmetry in three dimensions corresponds to a 2‑group symmetry Bℤ₂. The anomaly is a four‑dimensional invertible TQFT with Bℤ₂‑structure. By the Freed–Hopkins theorem, the anomaly group is the degree‑4 homotopy group of the Anderson dual of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MT(Bℤ₂). The spectrum MT(Bℤ₂) is equivalent to Σ²MTℤ₂, where MTℤ₂ is the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −γ over Bℤ₂ (γ the canonical line bundle). Its homotopy groups are known: π₄(MT(Bℤ₂)) ≅ ℤ₂ generated by the Pontryagin square operation. The Anderson dual Iℤ shifts degrees by one, so π₄(IℤMT(Bℤ₂)) ≅ π₅(MT(Bℤ₂)) ≅ ℤ₂. Hence the anomaly group is ℤ₂. The non‑trivial anomaly corresponds to the four‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with gauge group ℤ₂ and the non‑trivial cohomology class in H⁴(Bℤ₂;U(1)) ≅ ℤ₂. This matches the known result that a ℤ₂ 1‑form symmetry in 3‑D has a ℤ₂‑valued ’t Hooft anomaly.
Exam Strategy
When asked to compute an anomaly group, first identify the symmetry type (0‑form, 1‑form, higher group) and the spacetime dimension. Translate the symmetry into a tangential structure G (e.g., BⁿG for an n‑form symmetry). Then recall that the anomaly group is πd+1(IℤMTG) ≅ πd+2(MTG). Use known calculations of Madsen–Tillmann spectra for common groups (finite groups, Spin, String, etc.) rather than attempting a full spectral sequence computation in the exam.

Extended TQFTs and the Computation of Invariants for Manifolds with Non‑Trivial Higher Homotopy Groups

State‑sum constructions such as the Turaev–Viro model and the Crane–Yetter model assign invariants to closed manifolds by evaluating a combinatorial formula on a triangulation. For manifolds with non‑trivial higher homotopy groups, the standard state sum must be refined to incorporate the action of the fundamental group and higher homotopy groups on the algebraic data. In the extended framework, the value of a fully extended TQFT on a manifold M is computed by factorization homology of the corresponding En-algebra over M. When M has non‑trivial π₁, the factorization homology decomposes as a homotopy colimit over the fundamental groupoid, yielding a sum over flat G-bundles weighted by the partition function of the theory on the universal cover. For a Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with finite group G, this reproduces the familiar formula Z(M) = |G|−χ(M) Σ[ρ] exp(2πi∫M ρ*ω), where the sum runs over conjugacy classes of representations ρ: π₁(M) → G. For higher homotopy groups, the En-algebra input must be an n-fold category with appropriate higher duals. The factorization homology on a manifold M with πk(M) ≠ 0 for k > 1 involves the higher categorical traces of the En-algebra evaluated on spheres Sk. Concretely, the partition function on S2 × Σd−2 for a 3‑dimensional TQFT built from a modular tensor category C is given by the Verlinde formula Σi (S0i)χ(Σ), where S is the modular S-matrix. In four dimensions, the Crane–Yetter invariant of S2 × Σ2 involves the categorical dimension of the Drinfeld centre of the input fusion category. For five‑dimensional theories from fusion 2‑categories, the invariant on S3 × Σ2 requires the computation of the 3‑trace of the fusion 2‑category, which is a higher categorical analogue of the quantum dimension. These higher traces are computed by iterating the Drinfeld centre construction: the k-trace of a fusion n-category is the Ek-centre Zk(ℱ), which is a fusion (n−k)-category. The partition function on a manifold with non‑trivial πk then reduces to a state sum whose local weights are the (k+1)-j symbols of Zk(ℱ). This method has been used to compute the partition function of the 5‑dimensional Dijkgraaf–Witten theory on S2 × Σ3 for a surface Σ3 with non‑trivial π₁, yielding a formula involving a Verlinde‑type sum over representations of the fundamental group twisted by the π₂‑action.

The Role of Higher Categories in the Construction of Extended TQFTs from Higher Gauge Theories

Higher gauge theories generalize ordinary gauge theory by replacing the gauge group with a higher group (a 2‑group, 3‑group, etc.) and the gauge field with a higher connection, i.e., a differential cocycle in a suitable differential cohomology theory. The action functional is a higher Chern–Simons term, which is a differential character on the classifying space of the higher group. In the extended TQFT framework, a higher gauge theory in dimension d gives rise to a fully extended d-dimensional TQFT whose value on a point is the Ed-algebra of higher vector spaces obtained by quantizing the space of higher connections on the (d−1)-sphere. The cobordism hypothesis then guarantees that this assignment extends uniquely to a symmetric monoidal functor BorddG → dVect, where G is the tangential structure encoding the higher group. The higher categorical structure appears at three levels: (1) the higher group itself is a group object in (∞,1)-categories, (2) the space of higher connections is an (∞,1)-stack, and (3) the quantization yields an Ed-algebra in dVect, i.e., an object with coherent higher duals. For a finite 2‑group 𝔾, the 3‑dimensional higher gauge theory is the Yetter model, whose state sum uses the 2‑category of representations of 𝔾. The extended version assigns to a point the fusion 2‑category Rep(𝔾), to a circle its Drinfeld centre Z(Rep(𝔾)) (a braided fusion category), and to a surface the category of modules over that braided fusion category. The partition function on a closed 3‑manifold M is the state sum of the Yetter invariant, which can be expressed as the factorization homology of the E3-algebra Z₂(Rep(𝔾)) over M. For a Lie 2‑group such as the String 2‑group, the 4‑dimensional higher Chern–Simons theory yields the Crane–Yetter model based on the modular tensor category of representations of the quantum group at a root of unity. The extended TQFT assigns to a point the fusion 2‑category of representations of the quantum group, and the higher categorical duality ensures that the theory is fully dualizable. The anomaly of the higher gauge theory is captured by the obstruction to extending the Ed-algebra to an Ed+1-algebra, which lives in the André–Quillen cohomology of the Ed-operad with coefficients in the units of the algebra. This obstruction class coincides with the characteristic class of the higher gauge field (e.g., the fractional Pontryagin class for String structures). Thus higher categories provide the algebraic backbone that organizes the fields, symmetries, and anomalies of higher gauge theories into a single extended TQFT.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Higher Tangential Structures and the Classification of Exotic Smooth Structures

The cobordism hypothesis has been extended to manifolds equipped with higher tangential structures, such as String, Fivebrane, Ninebrane, and higher G-structures defined by lifts of the stable normal bundle through a tower of connected covers of BO. Each stage in the tower kills a higher homotopy group of O and corresponds to a higher cohomology class (e.g., the first fractional Pontryagin class p₁/2 for String, p₂/6 for Fivebrane, etc.). The Madsen–Tillmann spectra MTString, MTFivebrane, MTNinebrane, … classify cobordism groups of manifolds with these structures. The Anderson duals of these spectra classify invertible topological phases with the corresponding tangential structure. Exotic smooth structures on spheres and other manifolds are detected by the difference between the smooth cobordism groups and the topological cobordism groups. In dimensions ≥ 5, the Kervaire–Milnor group of exotic spheres Θn fits into an exact sequence involving the stable homotopy groups of spheres and the image of the J‑homomorphism. The cobordism hypothesis with higher tangential structures predicts that a fully extended TQFT with, say, String structure can distinguish exotic spheres that are not detected by ordinary Spin TQFTs. Concretely, the partition function of a String‑structured TQFT on an exotic 7‑sphere Σ⁷ is given by the evaluation of the corresponding E7-algebra on Σ⁷, which reduces to a higher trace of the algebra on the generator of π₇(MTString). Computations using the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence show that the group of invertible String phases in dimension 7 is ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, generated by the Arf‑Brown invariant and a secondary invariant related to the Kervaire invariant. The exotic 7‑spheres represent non‑zero elements in this group, so a String TQFT can detect them. In dimension 4, where exotic ℝ⁴’s exist, the relevant tangential structure is a Pin⁺ structure combined with a 2‑framing. The cobordism hypothesis for manifolds with corners and singularities allows one to define a TQFT on a 4‑manifold with an exotic smooth structure by stratifying the manifold along a handle decomposition and assigning higher categorical data to each stratum. The resulting invariant is a state sum over 2‑categories associated to the handles, and its value distinguishes exotic ℝ⁴’s because the higher categorical duality data encodes the failure of the h‑cobordism theorem in dimension 4. This programme connects the classification of exotic smooth structures to the representation theory of higher categories and provides a physical interpretation of exotic smoothness as a topological phase of matter.

  • Confusing the categorical level of the Drinfeld centre in a higher gauge theory: the centre of a braided fusion 2‑category is a braided fusion 1‑category, not a 2‑category.
  • Assuming that a String structure automatically implies a Fivebrane structure; the obstruction is the fractional Pontryagin class p₂/6, which can be non‑zero even when p₁/2 vanishes.
  • Treating the anomaly of a higher gauge theory as an ordinary group cohomology class when the symmetry is a higher group; the correct invariant lives in the cohomology of the higher classifying space.
  • Overlooking that factorization homology on a manifold with non‑trivial π₂ requires the E2-centre of the input algebra, not the ordinary Drinfeld centre.
  • Misidentifying the input fusion n-category for a d-dimensional TQFT: the point receives a fusion (d−2)-category, not a fusion (d−1)-category.
  • Assuming that the partition function of a Crane–Yetter type model on a non‑orientable manifold is always non‑zero; it vanishes when the relevant Stiefel‑Whitney class combination is non‑trivial.
  • Confusing the condensation completion of a fusion n-category (which is an (n+1)-category) with the original fusion n-category when classifying gapped boundaries.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Fivebrane Structures and the Classification of Seven‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

A Fivebrane structure on a smooth manifold is a lift of the stable normal bundle through the 7‑connected cover of BO, usually denoted BFivebrane → BO. The primary obstruction to such a lift is the fractional Pontryagin class p₂/6 ∈ H⁸(−;ℤ). When this class vanishes, the set of inequivalent Fivebrane structures is a torsor for H⁷(−;ℤ). The Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTFivebrane represents the cobordism theory of manifolds equipped with a Fivebrane structure, and its Anderson dual IℤMTFivebrane classifies invertible topological phases protected by that tangential structure. By the cobordism hypothesis, a fully extended 7‑dimensional TQFT with Fivebrane structure corresponds to a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,7)‑category of higher vector spaces equipped with an action of MTFivebrane. The partition function on a closed 7‑manifold M is the evaluation of this object on the fundamental class [M] ∈ π₇(MTFivebrane). Computations using the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MTFivebrane show that the group of invertible Fivebrane phases in dimension 7 is a finite abelian group whose generators are higher analogues of the Arf–Brown invariant and a secondary invariant related to the Kervaire invariant. In particular, exotic 7‑spheres that are invisible to Spin and String TQFTs can be detected by a Fivebrane TQFT because their non‑trivial image in π₇(MTFivebrane) pairs non‑trivially with the dualizable object. This provides a physical realisation of the fact that the Kervaire–Milnor group Θ₇ injects into the Fivebrane cobordism group, and it illustrates how higher tangential structures refine the classification of topological phases beyond the reach of lower structures.

Higher Categorical Traces on Manifolds with Non‑Trivial π₃ and the Computation of Partition Functions

Factorization homology ∫ₘA of an E_d‑algebra A on a d‑manifold M computes the value of the associated fully extended TQFT on M. When M has non‑trivial higher homotopy groups, the local‑to‑global principle forces the input algebra to carry higher central structures. For a 4‑manifold with π₃(M) ≠ 0, the relevant higher trace is the E₃‑center ZE₃(A), which is a braided monoidal 2‑category. Concretely, if A is a fusion 2‑category assigned to the point, the partition function on M is obtained by evaluating the 4‑dimensional higher trace of A on a generator of π₃(M). This trace factors through the condensation completion of A, which is a braided fusion 3‑category, and then through the E₃‑center, which captures the braiding of loop‑like defects. The resulting invariant is a state sum over a triangulation of M where each 3‑simplex is labelled by objects of the braided fusion 3‑category and each 2‑simplex by 1‑morphisms, with the braiding data ensuring invariance under Pachner moves. When M is a 4‑sphere, π₃(S⁴) ≅ ℤ, and the partition function reduces to the categorical dimension of the unit object in ZE₃(A), reproducing the known formula for the Crane–Yetter invariant. For manifolds with non‑trivial π₃ such as S³ × S¹, the invariant detects the higher holonomy of the 3‑form gauge field associated to the braided fusion 3‑category. This mechanism explains why certain 4‑dimensional TQFTs vanish on manifolds with non‑zero Pontryagin class p₁: the obstruction to extending the E₃‑center to an E₄‑center is precisely the class p₁/2, and its non‑vanishing forces the partition function to be zero.

Worked Example: Partition Function on S³ × S¹
Let A be the fusion 2‑category of representations of a finite group G, viewed as an E₄‑algebra in 2‑Vect. Compute the partition function of the associated 4‑dimensional extended TQFT on M = S³ × S¹.
The E₃‑center of A is the Drinfeld center Z(Rep(G)) ≅ Rep(D(G)), a braided fusion 1‑category. Its condensation completion is the braided fusion 2‑category 2‑Rep(D(G)). The higher trace on S³ × S¹ evaluates to the categorical trace of the identity 1‑morphism on the unit object in 2‑Rep(D(G)), which equals the number of simple objects in Rep(D(G)), i.e. the number of conjugacy classes of G times the number of irreducible representations of their centralisers. This matches the known Dijkgraaf–Witten partition function on S³ × S¹.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the partition function on a product manifold, identify the relevant higher homotopy group of the factor that is a sphere. The higher trace reduces to a categorical dimension in the appropriate higher center. Memorise the pattern: π_k(S^k) ≅ ℤ gives a trace in the E_k‑center; πk+1(S^k) gives a secondary invariant.

Extended TQFTs from Higher Categorical Representations of the Braid Group in Six Dimensions

In six dimensions, codimension‑2 defects are 4‑dimensional worldvolumes whose linking spheres are 3‑spheres. The configuration space of n ordered 3‑spheres in ℝ⁶ has fundamental group the higher braid group B_n(ℝ⁶,3), which is isomorphic to the pure braid group of 3‑spheres. A fully extended 6‑dimensional TQFT assigns a fusion 4‑category C to the point. The braid group action on the space of defects is encoded by a higher categorical representation of B_n(ℝ⁶,3) on the 4‑category of defect junctions, which is the iterated Drinfeld center Z(2)(C). By the cobordism hypothesis with singularities, such a representation extends uniquely to a symmetric monoidal functor from the (∞,6)‑category of 6‑dimensional cobordisms with codimension‑2 singularities to the (∞,6)‑category of higher vector spaces. The resulting TQFT is completely determined by the fusion 4‑category C together with a braided monoidal 3‑category structure on Z(C) and a sylleptic structure on Z(2)(C). Concretely, the state‑sum construction on a triangulated 6‑manifold labels 4‑simplices by objects of C, 3‑simplices by 1‑morphisms, 2‑simplices by 2‑morphisms, and 1‑simplices by 3‑morphisms, with the braiding and sylleptic data providing the coherence isomorphisms needed for invariance under the 6‑dimensional Pachner moves. When C is the fusion 4‑category of 4‑vector spaces graded by a finite 3‑group G, the resulting theory is a higher Dijkgraaf–Witten model whose partition function computes the cohomology of the mapping space Map(M, B³G). This construction realises the expectation that 6‑dimensional topological orders are classified by braided fusion 3‑categories, and it provides a concrete higher categorical framework for the braid group representations that appear in the study of 4‑dimensional defect loops in 6‑dimensional quantum field theories.

The Cobordism Hypothesis for Manifolds with Ninebrane Structures and the Classification of Ten‑Dimensional Anomalous Phases

A Ninebrane structure is a lift of the stable normal bundle through the 15‑connected cover of BO, denoted BNinebrane → BO. The obstruction to such a lift is the fractional Pontryagin class p₃/… ∈ H¹²(−;ℤ). The Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTNinebrane represents the cobordism theory of Ninebrane manifolds, and its Anderson dual classifies invertible topological phases with Ninebrane structure. By the cobordism hypothesis, a fully extended 10‑dimensional TQFT with Ninebrane structure corresponds to a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,10)‑category of higher vector spaces equipped with an MTNinebrane‑action. The partition function on a closed 10‑manifold M is the evaluation of this object on the fundamental class [M] ∈ π₁₀(MTNinebrane). The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MTNinebrane shows that the group of invertible Ninebrane phases in dimension 10 is a finite abelian group whose generators are higher analogues of the Arf–Brown invariant and a tertiary invariant related to the third Kervaire invariant. In particular, exotic 10‑spheres that are not detected by String or Fivebrane TQFTs can be distinguished by a Ninebrane TQFT because their non‑trivial image in π₁₀(MTNinebrane) pairs non‑trivially with the dualizable object. This provides a physical interpretation of the fact that the Kervaire–Milnor group Θ₁₀ injects into the Ninebrane cobordism group, and it illustrates the systematic refinement of topological phase classification as one ascends the Whitehead tower of BO.

The Role of Higher Categorical Centers in the Classification of Topological Orders in Nine‑Dimensional Extended TQFTs

A fully extended 9‑dimensional TQFT assigns a fusion 7‑category C to the point. The Drinfeld center Z(C) is a braided fusion 6‑category, and its iterated center Z(2)(C) is a sylleptic fusion 5‑category. The condensation completion of C is a fusion 8‑category whose objects are gapped boundaries of the 9‑dimensional bulk. The classification theorem states that topological orders in 9 dimensions are in bijection with equivalence classes of braided fusion 6‑categories that arise as Drinfeld centers of fusion 7‑categories. The higher categorical center Z(C) encodes the braiding of codimension‑2 defects (7‑dimensional worldvolumes), while Z(2)(C) encodes the sylleptic structure of codimension‑3 defects. The state‑sum construction on a triangulated 9‑manifold labels 7‑simplices by objects of C, 6‑simplices by 1‑morphisms, …, and 0‑simplices by 7‑morphisms, with the braiding and sylleptic data ensuring invariance under the 9‑dimensional Pachner moves. When C is the fusion 7‑category of 7‑vector spaces graded by a finite 6‑group G, the resulting theory is a higher Dijkgraaf–Witten model whose partition function computes the cohomology of Map(M, B⁶G). The higher categorical center thus plays the same organising role in 9 dimensions that the ordinary Drinfeld center plays in 3 dimensions: it classifies the bulk topological order, its gapped boundaries, and the anyon condensation transitions between them.

  • Confusing the obstruction class for a Fivebrane structure (p₂/6) with that for a String structure (p₁/2).
  • Assuming that the E₃‑center of an E₄‑algebra is a braided fusion 1‑category; it is a braided monoidal 2‑category.
  • Treating the higher braid group B_n(ℝ⁶,3) as an ordinary braid group; its representation theory requires sylleptic monoidal 3‑categories.
  • Overlooking that the Ninebrane obstruction lives in degree 12, not degree 8, and that the relevant Madsen–Tillmann spectrum is MTNinebrane, not MTFivebrane.
  • Misidentifying the iterated Drinfeld center Z(2)(C) as a braided fusion 6‑category; it is sylleptic of one categorical level lower.
  • Assuming that the partition function of a Ninebrane TQFT on an exotic 10‑sphere always vanishes; it can be non‑zero and detects the exotic smooth structure.
  • Neglecting that the condensation completion of a fusion 7‑category is a fusion 8‑category, not a fusion 7‑category, when classifying gapped boundaries.
